website in that context, which does not imply a presumed outcome. Decisions taken in relation to this document and updates to it, if any,
will be made publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

activities through its widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, having

A.21. Executive summary
4. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity and challenges for
agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the
pressures of a changing climate. The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is
evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to
climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. The effects of climate change are
expected to be severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing climate is weak
— exacerbating the continent’s food insecurity and climate vulnerability challenges.
5. The impacts of climate change have increased over the past decades in Africa, manifesting in more
frequent, intense, and prolonged extreme weather events, such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, pest
outbreaks, landslides, desertification, and sandstorms. These extreme weather events have resulted in
increased temperatures and humidity, shifts in precipitation patterns, water stress, and soil erosion. Most
African countries already face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often leading to short
growing periods reducing the viability of farming in marginal agricultural areas. Projected reductions in
yield in some countries could reach as much as 50% by 2030, and crop net revenues may fall by up to
90% by 2100, with smallholder farmers being the most affected. Food security, however, is affected not
only by changes in crop production but also by changes throughout the crop value chain, including post-
harvest. There is, therefore, a crucial need to address the impacts of climate change on the full agriculture
value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, processing, and distribution.
6. While there is a wealth of information on the observed and projected impacts of climate change on crop
production pre-harvest (i.e., germination, fruiting, cultivation, maturation, seed or grain quality, and most
of all yields), relatively little attention has been paid to how climate change variability and hazards affect
harvesting, post-harvest handling and storage (e.g., threshing, sorting, drying, warehousing), and
processing, transport and logistics (grading, packaging, storage, transport, distribution, and marketing).
7. Of the range of climate hazards that affect harvest and post-harvest value chains of cereal crops and
legumes, scholarly publications and country experts pointed to variable, erratic, and unpredictable
rainfall (including an increase in heavy rainfall events), high temperatures (extreme heat), and
flooding as the priority hazards of concern. Literature and stakeholders also highlighted drought as an

A.20. Executing
Entity
information |  |  | The project will be executed directly by AGRA through their Programme Operational Unit
– which falls under the same legal entity as the PSAA Applicant. As a continental
institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA is well placed to undertake the planned
activities through its widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, having
strong AGRA country teams and infrastructure in the target countries. Using AGRAs
continental forums, the Africa Food Systems Forum (AFSF) and Agribusiness Deal
Room, AGRA brings on board close working relationships with governments, partners
and other stakeholders to enhance ownership and coordinate key actors, knowledge and
resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation under changing climates
to scale. The executing entity role of AGRA will be fulfilled both through the Nairobi-
based headquarters, and their country offices, all of which are the same legal entity as
the PSAA Applicant. |  |  |  |

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5
A
important hazard. To a lesser degree, sources also acknowledged the role of wildfires and landslides.
Consistently, stakeholder input coalesced around the several stage-specific risks and impacts:
▪ Harvesting Processes: shattering of the grain/seed due to untimely heavy rains ; challenges to field
drying due to excessive rainfall; rotting, mold, and mycotoxin (principally aflatoxin) contamination in
the field due to high humidity; damage to stored harvest from flooding; pest and rodents.
▪ Post-Harvest Handling and Storage: damage to or decay of grain/seed quality from high
temperatures and humidity; rotting, mold, and mycotoxin (aflatoxin) contamination due to damage to
storage infrastructure from flooding;
▪ Processing, Transport, and Logistics: damaged or inaccessible road networks and transport
infrastructure due to flooding; damage to processing and storage facilities and infrastructure from
excessive rainfall and flooding; damage to or decay of grain/seed quality from high temperatures and
humidity, and lower shelf life; disruption of mobility and access to markets due to extreme weather,
impeding distribution and marketing.
8. In addition to the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food losses, such losses also contribute to
greenhouse gas emissions.
9. In response to the critical gaps noted above, the RE-GAIN programme will support the innovative
adaptation of the harvest and post-harvest value chain in seven African countries — Burkina Faso,
Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia; strengthening food security by addressing the
climate change impacts on post-harvest food losses through the wide-scale adoption of Food Loss-
Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) among smallholder farmers. In addition to increasing the amount of food
available for consumption due to reduced losses, this approach will also improve the quality of produce
leaving the farms, thereby increasing nutritional value, marketability and income potential, while
simultaneously reducing the GHG emissions associated with food loss. To achieve the project goal, actions
will be taken across three primary pathways — farmer-centric, supplier-centric, and institutional support
— which together will form the foundation of a sustainable and scalable market for FL-RS in the target
countries. These interconnected solutions will address food loss under smallholder settings of Africa.
10. The first development pathway focuses on increasing demand and absorption capacity for FL-RS among
smallholder farming communities. This will involve raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of
climate change on post-harvest food loss, as well as on the availability of FL-RS options on the market
and how FL-RS will help reduce food loss under changing climate conditions. This awareness raising will
be coupled with training on the use of specific FL-RS that have been identified for key crops in each country
as well as on the effective use of weather information and advisories, while supporting the initial transfer
of suitable technologies to the farmers to catalyse uptake and demonstrate the effectiveness of FL-RS.
These activities will be further supported through actions to improve market linkages between farmers and
agri-value chain actors such as processors and aggregators.
11. The second development pathway complements the demand-side activities by enhancing the supply and
affordability of FL-RS. The first output of this will support business development linked to the local supply
of FL-RS, targeting both existing suppliers, as well as entrepreneurs seeking to enter the market –
particularly MSMEs, local cooperatives and youth groups. Support to local businesses will increase the
supply of FL-RS in local markets, which in turn will enable cost reduction, underpinning the outcome of
improved affordability. This will be complemented by the establishment of innovative financial mechanisms
for smallholders and MSMEs to support the adoption of FL-RS. In particular, the project will bring together
key stakeholders — including suppliers, agro-dealers, financial institutions, MSMEs and local farmers —
to establish finance models that stimulate the FL-RS market, using catalytic co-payments and conditional
procurement to provide added security to the transactions.
12. Combined, the increased demand and the enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS stimulate the
market, enabling suppliers to develop products and services that are affordable to smallholder farmers,
thereby facilitating a balance between supply and demand, and establishing a self-sufficient market that
will operate beyond the project support. The integrated nature of these Outputs and the causal pathways
reinforcing the market development are depicted in Figure 3 below.

=== PAGE 8 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6
A
These first two development pathways will be underpinned and sustained by the third, which seeks to
create an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS; which will not only mobilise increased investment
in the sector but also provide wider policy interventions in strengthening the market systems and advisory
services. This will involve policy revisions and put in place enforcement mechanisms in each of the target
countries in support of enabling FL-RS — including stimulating tax incentives and promoting standards for
FL-RS quality — as well as creating platforms for the scaling and replication of successful FL-RS business
models.
13. Overall, the resulting uptake of FL-RS, at scale, will reduce food loss, improve food quality and enhance
local capacity, thereby enabling the long-term outcome of improved food and nutritional security. This will
have the co-benefit of increasing food safety, while the increased investment in FL-RS resulting will create
additional employment opportunities in the market. Moreover, the project will reduce GHG emission, both
directly – through the reduced emissions from the wasted food itself – and indirectly by reducing land use
change for agricultural expansion driven by the need to compensate for food losses.

=== PAGE 9 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 7
B
B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION
B.1. Climate context
14. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity and challenges for
agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the
pressures of a changing climate3. The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is
evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to
climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. The effects of climate change are
expected to be severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing climate is weak
— exacerbating the continent’s food insecurity challenge.
15. A great deal of attention has been paid to the impacts of climate change on crop cultivation, with extensive
research on how climate variability affects crop yields, and management strategies to address these
challenges4. However, food security is influenced not only by changes in crop production but also by the
entire crop value chain, including post-harvest stages5. There is, therefore, a crucial need to consider how
climate change impacts the full agriculture value chain, including production, aggregation, storage,
processing, and distribution.
Harvesting and Post Harvesting Value Chains
16. The RE-GAIN programme will support the adaptation of the harvest and post-harvest management
innovations in seven African countries — Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Tanzania, Uganda, and
Zambia; strengthening food security by addressing the climate change impacts on post-harvest food
losses. For the purpose of the RE-GAIN Programme proposal, the post-harvest value chain is considered
to start at the point of harvest, and spans across three key stages, as shown in Figure 1. Strategic6
Harvesting processes Post-harvest handling and storage Processing, transportation and logistics
Including harvesting processes and Including threshing, cleaning, sorting, Including packaging and distribution,
skills storage and primary processing and impact on shelf life
Figure 1. Strategic value chain stages included in the RE-GAIN Programme.
Climate Risks for Post-Harvest Value Chains
17. Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of the
value chains compound the climate change-driven productivity and yield losses during cultivation. This
results in a double-blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising temperatures, higher
moisture levels (warmer air holds more moisture), increased precipitation, more frequent or intense heavy
rainfall events, flooding.
18. Climate change is thus a threat-multiplier to crop value chains. Climate change impacts in the post-
production value chain, which result in substantial post-harvest losses and reduce the output that farmers
have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize livelihoods of small-holder farmers (IFPRI, 2022). This
perpetuates vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses and prolongs the cycle of persistent poverty.
19. In the face of climate change-driven post-harvest losses, farmers have an impetus to keep less of the
reduced output for their domestic consumption and to offset the volume losses (during harvesting, field-

drying, on-farm storage) by selling more of what remains in the market. This worsens household food
insecurity in countries that already have markedly high levels of undernourishment.
Climate change impact on food loss
20. In all seven countries, historic trends in temperature (such as annual average/mean surface temperature,
and the number of days with extreme heat – i.e., temperatures over 35C) have displayed an increasing
trend over time, with the rate of increase accelerating in recent decades. Historic rainfall trends display far
greater variability, and for some countries a measure of uncertainty regarding the direction of the trend.
21. By and large, future projections of rainfall (such as annual average/mean precipitation, and the number of
consecutive wet days, and – as a corollary – the number of consecutive dry days) suggest a projected rise
in rainfall levels and rainfall days across most of East Africa, with the exception of Zambia (where
projections display a slight drying signal, consistent with regional trends for Southern Africa).

7 The analysis uses data form the World Bank’s Climate Change Knowledge Portal, and includes two distinct future climate change emissions
scenarios, to allow a study of the range of potential climate change impacts in 2040 regardless of which of the two emissions pathways the
world is more likely to reflect in 2040. It is important for the design and operationalization of investments under RE-GAIN to plan and prepare
for where emissions pathways are already heading, as well as a ‘worst case scenario’ so that investments are effective in addressing potential
risks and do not fall short of the protections they are intended to afford.

Impact of climate change on food loss
22. The impact of climate change on post-harvest food loss has not been well studied, either in terms of
observed impacts (i.e., research on historic food losses in the post-harvest value chain, with some
attribution to climatic factors), or in terms of projected impacts (i.e., research on future anticipated food
losses in the post-harvest value chain, with plausible attribution or linkage to rise in temperature, increased
rainfall variability, and a climate change-induced exacerbation of hazards such as flooding and drought).
23. Thus, in the table below the observed impacts of climate change in the post-harvest value chain are
captured simply as the level of climate-sensitivity of the grain/seed (drawn from peer reviewed literature
that focused on yield and productivity impacts of climate change). The column on projected impacts of
climate change in the post-harvest value chain is populated based on country (national and local)
stakeholder guidance (including farmers and agricultural decision-makers) plus some additional sectoral
expert judgment.

Adaptive capacity – current capacity to anticipate or respond to the impact of climate change on
food loss
24. All seven RE-GAIN countries are recognized as having low adaptive capacity and low readiness for climate
change impacts, in terms of their relative global standing. Per the ND-GAIN index9, based on scores for
2021, the countries’ ranking on adaptive capacity10 and readiness11 – out of 192 countries – is presented in
Table 3. This economy-wide low level of adaptive capacity also applies to the agricultural sector, including
post-harvest management of value chains. This was affirmed and validated through inputs in national and
sub-national stakeholder workshops, at which stakeholders identified several factors that contribute to low
adaptive capacity against climate-induced risks and impacts. Stakeholder inputs across all 14 crops in the
seven countries points to consistent, common factors affecting adaptive capacity in all fourteen value
chains (of eight different crops). This consistency is both understandable (given common or similar barriers
in all seven countries’ agricultural value chains) and valuable (as it offers opportunity for RE-GAIN’s
9 Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative, n.d
10 Under the ND GAIN Index, ‘Adaptive Capacity’ is the ability of society and its supporting sectors to adjust to reduce potential damage and to
respond to the negative consequences of climate events. This captures a collection of means, readily deployable to deal with sector-specific climate
change impacts.
11 Under the ND GAIN Index, ‘Readiness’ is the ability to make effective use of investments for adaptation actions thanks to a safe and efficient
business environment. Readiness has three components: economic readiness, governance readiness, and social readiness.

Table 3 Applicable ND-GAIN Adaptive Capacity Rankings for RE-GAIN countries
Country Adaptive Capacity Rank (out of 192) Readiness Rank (out of 192)

25. Across the board, the major factors of vulnerability that impede adaptive capacity, highlighted by
stakeholders in all seven countries, are summarised as follows:
▪ Infrastructure: Reliance on climate-vulnerable (non-climate-robust) storage and transport
infrastructure leads to greater vulnerability and exposure to ambient temperature and moisture, as
well as during extreme weather conditions such as flooding and heavy rainfall events.
▪ Finance: Lack of access or limited access to credit reduces the ability for risk-management, and limits
financial capacity to invest in climate-adaptive technology, equipment, facilities, and tools. Limited
information about markets reduces the ability to plan for volumes of storage, transport, and sale, with
the risk of more of the harvested crop being exposed to temperature and humidity than optimal.
▪ Technology: Reliance on traditional methods and practices rather than greater mechanization and
specialized tools (for harvesting, threshing, sorting, grading, packaging) reduces ability to protect the
harvested crop from the impacts of high temperatures, moisture, humidity, and exposure to flooding.
Limited information about pest control also increases the harvested crop’s exposure to climate-
sensitive pests (including insects).
The lack of / limited access to climate information services (such as early warning systems and
weather alerts) also straitjackets adaptive capacity against hazards like extreme weather events.
▪ Capacity: Lack of/limited access to technology, equipment, facilities, and tools (for threshing, drying,
storage, and transport) reduces ability to protect harvested crop from the impacts of high
temperatures, moisture, humidity, and increases exposure to flooding and heavy rains.
26. Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of the
value chains compound the climate change-driven productivity and yield losses during cultivation. This
results in a double-blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising temperatures, higher
moisture levels (warmer air holds more moisture), increased precipitation, more frequent or intense heavy
rainfall events, flooding. Climate change is thus a threat-multiplier to crop value chains. The impacts from
climate change in the post-production value chain stages, which result in substantial post-harvest losses
and reduce the output that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize livelihoods of small-
holder farmers. This perpetuates vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses and prolongs the cycle of
persistent poverty.
27. Furthermore, in the face of climate change-driven post-harvest losses, farmers have an impetus to keep
less of the reduced output for their own domestic consumption and to offset the volume losses (during
harvesting, field-drying, on-farm storage) by selling more of what remains in the market. This worsens
household food insecurity in countries that already have markedly high levels of undernourishment (per
FAO figures). All of these reduce adaptive capacity and must be ameliorated to ensure resilience of
smallholder farmers in the seven countries.
28. In light of these findings, it is imperative for RE-GAIN to support adaptation to climate change through
harvest and post-harvest loss-reduction solutions, and enable climate resilience in crops’ post-harvest
value chains of smallholder farmers and communities.

Adaptation Needs and Climate Change Solutions
29. The impact of climate change on post-harvest food value chain stage is felt largely through the exposure
of the grain/seed to high temperatures, high moisture levels, direct contact with water (such as during
heavy rainfall events), and damage caused by extreme weather events such as flooding. Additionally, high
temperature and moisture levels also foster the growth of pests and diseases, including fungal and
parasitic agents. Thus, the most effective solutions for reducing climate-change induced food losses in the
post-harvest value chain is to reduce the harvested seed’s/grain’s exposure to the climatic variable or
hazard. This involves interventions that improve efficiency and speed of harvest and related actions (such
as threshing, sorting, grading etc. – to reduce the time period of exposure), as well as interventions that
offer higher protection from climatic elements (barriers between the grain/seed and the external
environment), plus interventions that provide higher levels of actionable information on the levels of or
prevalence of the climatic variables (e.g., measurement tools).
30. The mere provision of such FL-RS solutions by itself is not enough if this is not accompanied by capacity-
building, training, awareness-generation of how best to use such solutions, and how to increase financial
wherewithal to maintain and augment such solutions in the future. To address the pressing need for
broader implementation of solutions aimed at reducing climate-related harvest and post-harvest food loss,
it is necessary to raise awareness and build capacity to promote the adoption of Food Loss Reduction
Solutions (FL-RS) in the light of expected climate impacts. This needs to be complemented by
strengthened multisectoral institutional capacities, facilitating the uptake of FL-RS by end users and
service providers, increasing options of solutions’ availability, and enabling practical application through
policy interventions. There is also an urgent need for enhanced financial access for farmers and Micro,
Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), empowering them to invest in climate-friendly FL-RS and
incentivising vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers of climate-adapted FL-RS, fostering a robust end-to-
end market ecosystem.
31. For this programme, a key focus of adaptation support needs to be on strengthening the capabilities of
countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest infrastructure, through providing physical solutions
alongside technical and institutional capacity building for actors across harvest and postharvest value
chain stages – from processing through to market distribution. This includes investing in strategic
approaches that leverage finance and build markets for food loss reduction strategies. By enhancing
access to markets of existing postharvest solutions, the program will encourage farmers to adopt FL-RS
products and services, thereby boosting their climate and economic resilience.

32. According to the World Bank estimates, a one percent reduction in postharvest losses in Sub-Saharan
Africa could lead to economic gains of $40 million each year, and most of the benefits would go directly to
smallholder farmers12. Moreover, food loss and waste depict an extremely inefficient use of resources and
account for about 3.3 gigatonnes of greenhouse gas emissions globally13. In addition, large amounts of
water and fertilizer go into the production of this food that never reaches human mouths. Recovering the
food that is lost during harvest and postharvest handling can help close that calorie gap in Africa while
strengthening livelihoods and improving food security— without requiring any additional environmental
cost.
Complementarity with ongoing and planned initiatives
The RE-GAIN Programme operates in a complex environment of ongoing initiatives, and has been
designed with this in mind. While many of these initiatives are focused on the production side of the
agriculture value chain, there are also initiatives in each of the target countries targeting various aspects
of the post-harvest value chain. Through their ongoing operations, AGRA has developed a strong
understanding of the landscape of initiatives in the target countries and has developed working relations
with many of the organisations operating these initiatives. The design of this programme has closely
considered the ongoing work in each country and seeks to complement and collaborate with these
initiatives to maximise the effectiveness of the programme14. A list of the key initiatives in each of the target
countries is presented in Table 10-2 of Annex 2.

B.2. (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram
Goal Statement
33. IF the capacity of the target countries and communities to respond to climate-triggered food losses is
strengthened through improved and inclusive access to financing, promotion of context-specific and
gender-responsive innovations to reduce food losses, and better enabling conditions for public and private
investments, THEN smallholder farmers will have enhanced food security and livelihood resilience,
BECAUSE the widespread use of food loss-reduction technologies will reduce food loss and reduce the
carbon footprint of food systems, while increasing household income and building the resilience of
smallholder farmers, MSMEs and rural communities to climate shocks.
Barriers
34. Several barriers currently impede the uptake of food loss-reducing solutions in Africa. While the specifics
of each barrier might vary between countries and farming systems, six common barriers have been
identified, cutting across several themes, including limited knowledge of the options available and
capacities to use what is available, access to and supply of affordable harvesting and post harvesting
equipment and technologies, and access to sustainable and inclusive finance opportunities, all
encompassed by an overarching lack of an enabling environment. An outline of these core barriers is
presented below, with deeper country-specific analysis provided in the appendices to the Feasibility Study
(Annex 2).
Barrier 1: Inadequate access to information and solutions for smallholder farmers
35. While most smallholder farmers are acutely aware of the impacts of post-harvest losses on their food and
income security, many do not have access to the information needed to adopt improved post-harvest
processing and storage practices needed reduce such losses. The resulting knowledge gaps extend
across the full range of pre- and post-harvest processes — including harvesting, handling, storage,
transporting, and packaging of agricultural commodities – and are typically more significant for women-
managed farms, contributing to notable gender gaps in farm productivity and profitability. For some
farmers, the knowledge gaps are fundamental, in that they lack the basic understanding of resilient post-
harvest processes and the options available to reduce food loss. For others, there is a general
understanding of the need for improved practices, or even the availability of food loss-reducing solutions
(FL-RS) but lack the understanding of where and how to access such solutions, or how to properly
implement them. These knowledge gaps are systemic in the agriculture sector of the target countries, with
limited integration of FL-RS information into sectoral policies and agricultural extension services. As a
result, farmers lack access to the required information to adopt FL-RS. Overcoming this barrier requires a
combination of targeted and inclusive awareness raising among smallholder farmers, as well as the
establishment of an enabling environment in which information on FL-RS is mainstreamed into agriculture
sectoral policies and support services.
Barrier 2: Limited awareness of climate change and climate risks
36. As noted above, climate change impacts the agriculture sector across its entire value chain – from
production, through harvest, storage, processing and distribution. Despite this significant impact, many
smallholder farmers do not fully understand climate change and the associated climate risks and the
impacts they have on food security, and consequently are not adequately motivated to adopt resilient
solutions. Given that the impacts of climate change on the agricultural sector are expected to increase in
the future, the consequences of inaction stemming from limited awareness will become increasingly
severe. Although there is growing understanding among farmers of the impacts of climate change on
production – particularly as observed during increasingly frequency extreme climate events – such
awareness is less prevalent for post-harvest losses. This is partly due to the links being less obvious to
the observer, for example where a changing climate influences the prevalence of pests or disease during
storage. This lack of awareness perpetuates the information gaps noted in Barrier 1. Specifically, when a
farmer does not understand the links between climate change and food loss, efforts to seek resilient
solutions are often subdued. Moreover, a lack of awareness of potential future climate trajectories

=== PAGE 21 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 19
B
exacerbates the problem, as farmers are less likely to act early if they don’t understand how the problem
of climate change-induced food loss will increase over time.
Barrier 3: Limited availability of appropriate harvest and post-harvest technology
37. While a wide range of FL-RS options have been developed globally, penetration of such solutions into
smallholder farming communities in Africa is limited due to limited access to technologies and practices,
low community engagement and insufficient demand and perceptions of risk from FL-RS providers. This
lack of market penetration has been attributed to several closely linked factors. First, because of the limited
awareness noted in Barriers 1 and 2, there is currently limited demand for FL-RS in rural communities.
The lack of demand is further exacerbated by unavailability of working capital or access to affordable
finance (see Barrier 4 below), with many farmers therefore considering it unfeasible even if they wanted
to adopt improved FL-RS.
38. Second, as a result of the limited demand, suppliers have not demonstrated sufficient interest to develop
the market for FL-RS. In particular, without the economies of scale driven by sufficient demand, FL-RS
providers are not incentivised to develop localised production, distribution networks and markets that
would bring the cost of FL-RS down. This also inhibits research into improving FL-RS solutions that could
improve efficiency or affordability of the products.
39. Third, the development of local markets for FL-RS is further constrained by the perceived risk for suppliers
of engaging with smallholder farmers. As with financial institutions, FL-RS providers need to balance risk
in setting up operations within, and providing services to, smallholder farming communities. Creating a
market for smallholder farmers – for whom working capital is limited – requires innovative market tools to
be developed, many of which require the sharing of risk between different stakeholders – including
providers, users and financial operators. However, such systems ultimately rely on the ability of
smallholder farmers to secure sufficient income from their produce. Consequently, FL-RS suppliers often
perceive smallholder farmers as high risk because of their general limited capacity to sustainably produce,
process and market agricultural goods.
40. The ultimate result of these challenges is that smallholder farmers do not have easy access to affordable
FL-RS. To overcome this barrier, a catalyst is required to initiate demand and establish a sustainable local
market that would provide the necessary incentives to attract FL-RS providers.
Barrier 4: Limited access to finance for FL-RS uptake
41. As noted above, the market for FL-RS for smallholder farmers in Africa is influenced by the limited access
to markets for smallholder farmers, working capital for MSMEs and access to finance for the purchasing of
equipment. While there is a diverse financial market across all target countries — ranging from commercial
banks to micro-finance institutions (MFIs) — several inter-related factors impede access to finance. First,
MSMEs are perceived as high risk, largely because of the uncertainty of production (particularly for rainfed
agriculture under changing climate conditions) and limited access to smallholder produce. The feasibility
of agricultural loans is dependent on MSMEs being able to develop a profitable business case for the FL-RS
equipment to be able to repay the loan at the end of the season. However, the vast array of factors that
impact the business case creates a high level of uncertainty, while inadequate on-farm post-harvest storage
and processing can lead to further food losses before a farmer is ever able to use the FL-RS services. This
uncertainty is being exacerbated by climate change, with growing risk that entire harvest can be lost in a
single extreme climate event. Consequently, FIs are often hesitant to provide agricultural loan products
that target agrifood MSMEs. In instances where financial products have been developed, the terms of the
credit are often untenable for agrifood MSMEs.
42. Second, and exacerbating the risk perceptions mentioned above, is the lack of or limited collateral and
track record among smallholder farmers and the companies serving them, including youth groups, agro-
dealers and cooperatives. To offset the risk and comply with Central bank regulation, FIs generally require
collateral from the borrowers, generally in the form of an immovable asset, such as land or property.
However, most small and medium sized enterprises especially women and youth led businesses lack
suitable collateral or have already pledged their assets, restricting access to available finance products.
For example, land tenure arrangements in rural areas of Africa are generally dominated by communal land

=== PAGE 22 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 20
B
rights, in which land is not owned by the individual, but rather an individual is given access to land under
customary law. In such instances, the land cannot be used as collateral, and in the absence of other
valuable assets, entrepreneurs cannot meet the collateral requirements. This challenge is generally
amplified for women and other vulnerable groups who have less access to and control of assets due to
sociocultural norms and practices that override the implementation of gender equitable laws (which now
in exist in many African countries) relating to land and inheritance. Another factor commonly used to offset
risk is the presence of a strong track record; the business case for FL-RS service provision is not proven
in many countries, hence the hesitation for financial institutions to invest in productive assets that will be
rented out or used by small scale farmers. To create sustainable and more equitable markets for FL-RS,
it is essential to improve access to affordable finance for agripreneurs. This requires innovation in the
market, working with various stakeholders to develop innovative finance products that share the risk and
maximise opportunities for the sustainable adoption of FL-RS. Such innovations need to consider – and
address – gender disparities in access to collateral, farm productivity, and control over use of proceeds,
to ensure such financial support is accessible to both women and men. This includes enabling activities,
such as the sensitization of local communities on women’s land rights, as a basis for promoting women’s
greater ownership and control over land and other productive assets, all of which are key enablers for
securing credit
Barrier 5: Inadequate policy and regulatory frameworks to enable FL-RS investments
43. Development of national policies, strategies, frameworks and implementation plans aiming to address
agricultural sector and climate change challenges has been a priority for many of the African governments
in the recent years. Despite this, there are still considerable gaps in the policy and regulatory environment
across the target countries that impede the mainstreaming of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector.
This gap is underpinned by several factors. First, there is limited or no use of the scientific evidence base
in the formulation of policies and strategies on post-harvest loss reduction. This challenge is exacerbated
by limited technical and institutional capacities and systems for institution-level data collection, monitoring,
and evaluation of implementation and effectiveness policies and programmes.
44. Second, policies generally lack coordination and harmonisation across sectors, limiting opportunities for
integrated approaches to building resilience in the agriculture sector. For example, the volatility and
vulnerability of food systems, and associated food loss, is often exacerbated by poor infrastructure and/or
uneven and deteriorating power or energy access; and the lack of harmonisation of policies across these
different sectors prevents collaborative action, perpetuating the challenges.
45. Third, where policies or regulatory frameworks are in place, they are often constrained to agricultural input
subsidies — mainly for fertilizer and seeds — with no subsidy on post-harvest technologies. This further
extends to the focus of information and extension services, which are generally geared towards primary
production, and less so to post-harvest processes. Similarly, existing taxation regimes do not favour or
incentivise investments in storage and processing technologies for food in the smallholder system.
46. The underdeveloped private sector in these countries require policy incentive mechanisms, for example
import tax exemption that would enable reduction of cost of import and distribution of FS equipment and
services. Further, tax exemptions on key components that can be used in the manufacturing or assembly
of FL-RS should also be a key consideration in these countries.
47. The lack of access to finance as described above is also restricted by Central bank regulation and
prudential guidelines on loan loss provisioning, consideration of movable assets as collateral and value of
guarantees for banks in reducing their reserve requirements. Addressing these policy barriers can unlock
financing for equipment (movable assets), storage facilities (fixed assets) and other FL-RS identified. The
existence of warehouse receipt legislation and authorities are also an important factor to consider
enhancing the uptake and usage of storage solutions. Without cash in hand at harvest, farmers will be
hesitant to store their produce.
Barrier 6: Insufficient public sector capacity to steer investments in FL-RS
48. Actions to overcome the full range of barriers described above require a strong public sector to create the
enabling environment and steer investments into FL-RS. The public sector plays an important role in

=== PAGE 23 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 21
B
steering investment, not only by creating an enabling policy environment (see Barrier 5), providing context-
specific extension and advisory services to smallholder farmers but also through its role in coordination
and as a facilitator of public-private-community partnerships. However, public sector actors across the
target countries currently have limited technical and institutional capacity to create the required enabling
environment. This barrier is partly rooted in human capacity constraints, with insufficient staff available in
key public institutions stretching available human resources; but also, in the technical capacity of those
staff that are available to facilitate investments, technology adoption and partnerships. This includes public
sector staff having limited understanding and awareness of FL-RS and the innovative models available to
develop sustainable business and market opportunities.
Proposed Solution
49. The proposed project will contribute to food security in seven African countries by promoting the wide-
scale adoption of Food Loss-Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) among smallholder farmers. In addition to
increasing the amount of food available for consumption due to reduced losses, this approach will also
improve the quality of produce leaving the farms, thereby increasing nutritional value, marketability and
income potential, while simultaneously reducing the GHG emissions associated with food loss. To achieve
the project goal, and actions, will be taken across three primary pathways — farmer-centric, supplier-
centric, and institutional support — which together will form the foundation of a sustainable and scalable
market for FL-RS in the target countries. Interconnected solutions will be addressing food loss under
smallholder setting of Africa.
Figure 2. Interconnected solutions addressing food loss under smallholder setting of Africa.
50. The first development pathway focuses on increasing demand and absorption capacity for FL-RS among
smallholder farming communities. Activities under this pathway will target overcoming Barriers 1 and 2,
raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food loss, as well as
on the availability of FL-RS options on the market and how FL-RS will help reduce food loss under
changing climate conditions. This awareness raising will be coupled with training on the use of specific FL-
RS that have been identified for key crops in each country as well as on the effective use of weather
information and advisories, while supporting the initial transfer of suitable technologies to the farmers to
catalyse uptake and demonstrate the effectiveness of FL-RS. These combined activities will be catalysing
the demand and build the absorption capacity needed to achieve short-term Outcome 1: FL-RS adopted
by smallholder farmers. Contributions to Outcome 1 are further enhanced through Output 1.2, which will
improve market linkages between farmers and agri-value chain actors such as processors and
aggregators. This will enhance the value-add of FL-RS, ensuring that the increased quality produce
resulting from improved harvesting methods, post-harvest handling, storage, and processing is able to
make it to suitable markets to maximize income; creating sustainability in the whole value chain that will
enable future scaling of and maintain demand for FL-RS.

=== PAGE 24 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22
B
51. The second development pathway complements the demand-side activities by enhancing the supply and
affordability of FL-RS (Outcome 2). This component involves two Outputs, one focused on business
development for the improved provision of FL-RS on local markets, and the second focused on
complementary financial mechanisms for smallholders and MSMEs to enable the adoption of FL-RS.
Activities under Output 2.1 will support business development linked to the local supply of FL-RS, targeting
both existing suppliers, as well as entrepreneurs seeking to enter the market – particularly MSMEs, local
cooperatives and youth groups. The business development support will include providing access to market
intelligence, as well as facilitating access to existing credit/guarantee schemes as well as any new financial
mechanisms established under Output 2.2. Support to local businesses will increase the supply of FL-RS
in local markets, which in turn will enable cost reduction, underpinning the outcome of improved
affordability. This will be complemented by activities under Output 2.2 aimed at establishing innovative
financial mechanisms for smallholders and MSMEs to support the adoption of FL-RS. In particular, the
project will establish partnerships across the FL-RS value chain to create accessible finance opportunities
based on the foundations of risk sharing and cooperation. These partnerships will bring together key
stakeholders — including suppliers, agro-dealers, financial institutions, MSMEs and local farmers — to
establish finance models that stimulate the FL-RS market. Catalytic co-payments and conditional
procurements under the system using GCF grants will provide added security to the transactions, further
enhancing the risk-sharing nature of the approach. Finance models will be adapted to fit the local context
of each target community, considering the nature of the targeted crop and the required solutions, the needs
of the local farmers, and the capacity of stakeholders to absorb risk. Where available, the development of
financial modalities will integrate existing credit lines available from local FIs, as well as supporting the
inclusion of FL-RS in climate-resilient input packages.
52. Combined, the increased demand from Outcome 1 and the enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS
from Output 2 will be catalytic in the market, overcoming Barriers 3 and 4. Specifically, the transactions
facilitated by the project will stimulate the market, enabling suppliers to develop products and services that
are affordable to smallholder farmers, thereby facilitating a balance between supply and demand, and
establishing a self-sufficient market that will operate beyond the project support. The integrated nature of
these Outputs and the causal pathways reinforcing the market development are depicted in Figure 3 below.
Figure 3: Causal pathways underpinning the Theory of Change.
53. These first two development pathways will be underpinned and sustained by the third, which seeks to
create an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS (Outcome 3); which together with Outcome 2 will
not only mobilise increased investment in the sector but also provide wider policy interventions in
strengthening the market systems and advisory services. Through Output 3.1, the capacity of national
institutions will be enhanced to enable investments in FL-RS – specifically tackling Barriers 5 and 6. This
will involve policy revisions and put in place enforcement mechanisms in each of the target countries in
support of enabling FL-RS — including stimulating tax incentives and promoting standards for FL-RS

=== PAGE 25 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 23
B
quality — as well as creating platforms for the scaling and replication of successful FL-RS business
models.
54. Overall, the resulting uptake of FL-RS, at scale, will reduce food loss, improve food quality and enhance
local capacity, thereby enabling the long-term outcome of improved food and nutritional security. This will
have the co-benefit of increasing food safety, while the increased investment in FL-RS resulting from
Outcome 3 will create additional employment opportunities in the market. Moreover, the project will reduce
GHG emission, both directly – through the reduced emissions from the wasted food itself – and indirectly
by reducing land use change for agricultural expansion driven by the need to compensate for food losses.
Assumptions
55. The Theory of Change described above and illustrated in Figure 4, is underscored by several assumptions.
These assumptions are outlined below, and mapped against specific Outcomes and Outputs in Section E.
▪ The proposed FL-RS technologies are market-ready in Africa or globally (i.e. transfer and adoption
ready).
▪ MSMEs and smallholder farmers are receptive to capacity development and adopt alternative post-
harvest management technologies, with focus on managing contamination by mycotoxins and
adaptation practices for key crops and value chains, including the use of renewable energy.
▪ MSMEs and smallholder farmers, mainly youth and women, are willing to adopt climate-smart
postharvest handling techniques, land-use practices and RETs and are motivated to sustain them
over the programmes duration (5 years) and lifetime (10 years).
▪ Market actors (input suppliers and off-takers) are interested in providing the FL-RS solutions and
in buying improved FL-RS at better prices.
▪ Existing financial schemes and local financial institutions are willing to participate in the proposed
interventions.
▪ Accurate weather information and climate advisory is readily available to be disseminated to
farmers to enhance FL-RS effectiveness.

B.3. Programme description
58. The programme description below outlines the overarching design of the programme’s approach as it
applies across all seven countries. The activities are presented thematically as they apply across the focal
countries as a whole, with specific details on the nature of interventions for each individual country
presented in the country-specific Appendixes to Annex 2.
Crop and Site Selection
59. Key crops were identified by expert
assessment, supported by AGRA and the
National Designated Authority (NDA) of each
target country through consultative
processes. Two crops per target country
under AGRA’s portfolio were selected to
ensure that sufficient resources would be
available for the crafting and execution of
targeted solutions (as depicted in Figure 10).
Selected crops are representative of the
agricultural dynamics of each country and
aligned with the specific needs and strategic
agricultural goals of the nation. In addition,
these crops hold substantial importance to
the country’s food security and/or experience
particularly high rates of loss within the value
chain. Finally, these crops are produced in
large parts of the country through a significant
number of smallholder farmers engaged in Figure 5. Selected crops for each of the seven target countries.
the value chain. The key crops therefore reflect the agronomic and economic realities of each country and
provide opportunities for targeted enhancement of food security and sustainable agricultural practices and
improved management of these crops is also expected to reduce GHG emissions given the aggregated
contributions of these value chains to country’s GHG emissions, contributing to the NDCs of the target
country. Please see Annex 2: Section 3 and 4 for details on the climate vulnerability of selected crops.
60. Within each country, a set of target regions have been identified, as outlined in Table 5. Within these sites,
the specific beneficiaries will be selected following a set of eligibility criteria, as presented in Section 5.2,
5.6 and 6.4 of Annex 2. The selection of beneficiaries will involve social and environmental risk screening,
including screening for the presence of indigenous communities15; in locations where indigenous
communities are present, careful consideration and tailored approaches will be designed to address the
unique circumstances and challenges faced by Indigenous communities in these areas with continuous
engagement to proactively include their participation in the RE-GAIN programme.

Southern Province Central Province
Zambia
Eastern Province
Component 1: Food Loss-Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) demand side development to increase the
adoption of FL-RS by farmers
Outcome 1:Increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers
61. The first project component focuses on enhancing demand for, and adoption of, FL-RS in the target
communities. It is this adoption of FL-RS that will ultimately reduce food losses and result in the core
benefits of the programme for smallholder farmers – namely improved food security, increased resilience
to climate shocks, and reduced GHG emissions. The approach for achieving this Outcome is grounded to
two programme Outputs that will directly support the farmers through awareness, training, technology
transfer and market linkages. However, given the integrated nature of the project approach, achieving the
full impact of the Outcome will also rely on the supply-side interventions under Outcome 2, and the
strengthening of the enabling environment under Outcome 3.
Output 1.1:Smallholder farmers supported to adopt FL-RS
62. At the heart of the demand-side development is the direct support provided to smallholder farmers to not
only know what FL-RS options are available, but also to understand the need for adopting such options –
particularly when considering the impacts of a changing climate – as well as how to optimise the use of
FL-RS. Activities under this project will involve technical support targeting several core themes:
▪ Raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of climate change on food loss and the
availability of improved, climate-resilient FL-RS options;
▪ Training farmers on the use of improved FL-RS, as well as adapting existing post-harvest food
loss-reduction technologies to climate change;
▪ Training farmers on best practices for complementary crop management (including use of
weather/climate information) to maximise the efficiency of FL-RS;
▪ Actively demonstrating the effectiveness of FL-RS systems in local contexts; and
▪ Supporting the transfer of technologies and boosting immediate availability of FL-RS solutions in
target communities.
63. Training activities will cover three key stakeholder groups, namely: i) smallholder farmers; ii) extension
workers and agro-dealers; and iii) agricultural traders and processors.
64. The activities under Output 1.1 will focus specifically on short-listed FL-RS interventions, tailoring the
approach to each community’s individual context. AGRA will train and engage VBAs (Village Based
Advisors), who are community-based, and are usually young people who are lead farmers, unemployed
youth and/or entrepreneurial farmers to provide extension services and agribusiness linkages. This
extension model has been well developed and is operational in these target countries. They will play an
important role in training smallholder farmers on FL-RS solutions. An evaluation of proposed physical FL-
RS was conducted across all seven target RE-GAIN countries to identify those with the highest potential
to reduce harvest and postharvest food losses and protect the harvests against growing impacts from
climate hazards. The output of this assessment established a shortlist of FL-RS options tailored to each
country and crop, as presented in Section 5 of Annex 2 – Feasibility Study. These solutions include:
Communal storage structures; Warehouses; Harvesting machinery; Hermetic bags; Mechanical multi-crop
threshers and shellers; Metal and plastic silos; Moisture meters; Storage protectants and control agents;
Storage structures; and; Tarpaulins and plastic sheets. Further details on the nature of interventions per
country are available in the Appendixes to Annex 2.
Activity 1.1.1: Conduct an awareness-raising activities targeting smallholder farming communities
focussed on the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food losses and the availability of locally-
appropriate and climate-resilient FL-RS.
65. The limited awareness on post-harvest food loss management at both the value chain and policy level,
combined with inadequate communication on the importance of post-harvest food loss reduction has led
to the limited adoption of available FL-RS as well as inadequate integration of food loss reduction
strategies into relevant policies and strategies by governments in Africa. This has, in turn, resulted in

--- Table 1 on Page 28 ---
Zambia | Southern Province Central Province
Eastern Province
Component 1: Food Loss-Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) demand side development to increase the |

=== PAGE 29 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 27
B
inadequate investment in solutions that reduce food losses17. Approximately 5,350 VBA will be in raising
awareness, each targeting 200 to 250 farmers, resulting in a total reach of ~1,110,000 people trained.
66. To establish a foundation for increasing demand for FL-RS, awareness and capacity building focused
actions and activities will be undertaken in each of the target communities across the seven countries,
using local institutions, chiefdoms and community networks to increase the understanding of postharvest
food losses among farmers, local stakeholders in the agriculture value chain, and the public. The
awareness raising will target several key topics, as outlined below.
▪ RE-GAIN programme and its objectives to reduce food losses and for climate change adaptation.
▪ Major drivers and causes of harvest and postharvest loss in the target locations, with emphasis on
climate-food loss nexus
▪ Impact of postharvest losses on food security, income, economy, and the environment (incl. climate
change) and the importance to reduce FL.
▪ Available best practices and improved technologies and methods (e.g., timing of harvesting,
methods and technologies for harvesting, storage, etc.) to reduce harvest and postharvest losses
and their benefits (food security, income environment).
▪ Role of different actors (local government, extension services, farmer organisations, agro-dealers,
financial institutions) to provide access for FL-RS across scales
▪ Cross-cutting themes: climate change awareness, climate smart agriculture, farm management,
marketing, product quality management, access to finance, gender and youths, etc.
67. The nature of awareness raising activities will be tailored to agricultural context of each country, accounting
for the target crops, the availability of local information dissemination systems (including mass media such
as radio, television, digital platforms and social media, as well as brochures, flyers and local extension
services), cultural considerations and local languages. The campaign will collaborate with local
governments and farmer organisations, build on existing outreach systems to maximise reach and
participation. AGRA will closely monitor the awareness campaigns, assessing impacts and adapting
outreach strategies as needed.
Activity 1.1.2: Train smallholder farmers on the use of improved climate-resilient FL-RS.
68. Training will be provided to develop the capacities of smallholder farmers on improved crop management
and storage techniques to reduce food losses and to maintain quality of produce, increase farmers' income
by reducing losses and improving marketability, and improve supply of financial services and FL-RS to
smallholders and other value chain actors. AGRA will procure the services of suitable local entities (for
example, NGOs, VBAs) to facilitate training in target communities. Training will then be provided through
various channels — such as onsite demonstration of best practices, agricultural extension services, lead
farmers and Village-Based Advisors (VBAs), farmer cooperatives, industry associations, and digital
platforms — depending on the local context and the existing networks of the local service provider.
Approximately 5,350 VBA will be engaged for training, each targeting 200 to 250 farmers, resulting in a
total reach of ~1,100,000 people trained.
69. The nature and content of the training will be tailored to each group and demand as outlined below.
Training will include baseline topics, including an introduction to the RE-GAIN programme and the core
concepts of food security, post-harvest food losses and the impact of climate change – building on and
leveraging material from the awareness campaign (Activity 1.1.1). The sequencing of the training will also,
where feasible, follow Activity 1.1.5 to enable integration of the equipment demonstrations (Activity 1.1.6)
into the training of farmers. In addition to this baseline, technical training will be provided on specific subject
matter tailored to the local context of each community. A list of potential training topics has been identified,
which will be refined for the local needs in each target community during the first year of implementation,
once the communities are identified. This list includes:
▪ Major triggers and incidences of food loss and the impact they have on food security and health
▪ Identification of the optimal timing of harvesting, thrashing and transport.
▪ Methods to reduce contamination of produce by mycotoxins and other diseases at harvest and storage
▪ Use of available weather forecast data.
17 Bellagio Statement 2018

=== PAGE 30 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 28
B
▪ Key reasons of food losses during harvesting and postharvest management and storage.
▪ Major impacts of climate change on agriculture, with focus on food loss.
▪ Technical approaches on maintaining crop quality during harvesting, postharvest handling and
storage.
▪ Approaches and solutions to prevent pest attacks, and proper storage methods.
Types of storage facilities and their advantages and disadvantages.
▪ An overview of FL-RS options and suppliers
▪ Approaches to measuring and keeping optimal moisture content in crops to prevent aflatoxin
contamination.
▪ Best harvesting methods and tools, including mechanization to reduce food losses for various target
crops.
▪ Proper use and maintenance of physical FL-RS, including operation and maintenance of machinery,
and their environmental and safety aspects.
▪ Record-keeping, financial literacy and access to finance.
▪ Benefits of and processed for farmer aggregation.
▪ Packaging and marketing of crops.
▪ Methods and materials for proper on-farm storage, safe and proper use of pesticides and fungicides,
pre-storage crop treatment and preparations, and monitoring storage losses and quality of crops
during storage.
70. All training will be prepared with specific attention to gender responsiveness and opportunities for youth
development, as well as environmental considerations for proposed solutions. The Gender and Inclusion
Action Plan (Annex 8) stipulates how this will be achieved, with a strong focus on ensuring the VBA /
Farmer Based Organisation (FBO) training content is gender-responsive and youth -oriented, and that
these VBAs / FBOs are suitably trained on engaging specifically with women and youth. In addition to this
targeted engagement, the Gender and Inclusion Action Plan also specifies that all aspects of the
Programme’s awareness raising and capacity building initiatives must include dedicated modules /
components focussed on gender and youth inclusion and empowerment. These will seek to tackle issues
of acceptance / sensitization of male counterparts by focussing on the benefits of working with / supporting
women and youth -led initiatives. This approach extends to the training activities outlined below, which will
ensure agricultural traders, agro-dealers and extension workers are equally exposed to this content.
Activity 1.1.3: Train agricultural traders and processors on complementary services for FL-RS
71. Building on the training of farmers, the programme will provide additional training to agricultural traders
(agro-dealers, aggregators etc) and processors on complementary services that support and maximise
the effectiveness of FL-RS. This training will include the same baseline concepts, including an introduction
to the RE-GAIN programme and the core concepts of food security, post-harvest food losses and the
impact of climate change. Beyond that, the training will include best-bet approaches to introduce FL-RS to
smallholder farmers and the types of initial support required to enhance demand, tailored to the processing
and trade of produce, covering topics such as:
▪ post-harvest food losses at the trade and processing stages, their causes and solutions;
▪ improved quality management and adherence to quality standards;
▪ improved storage, transport logistics and packaging (including food-safe packaging materials and
methods);
▪ the sustainable use of storage protectants;
▪ farmer aggregation methods and systems and associated risks of food loss;
▪ improved processing (including whole grain processing) and value addition;
▪ supplier management;
▪ effective marketing strategies; and
▪ access to finance and credit.
72. Approximately 5,350 VBA will be engaged for training, each targeting four to five traders or processors,
resulting in a total reach of ~22,000 people trained.
Activity 1.1.4: Implement a training-of-trainers programme targeting extension workers and agro-dealers.

=== PAGE 31 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 29
B
73. In addition to the direct training of farmers under the programme, RE-GAIN will facilitate broader training
opportunities through a training-of-trainers (ToT) approach, particularly for local leader extension agents,
who will provide onward training to farmer. Using the same service providers procured for Activity 1.1.2,
key stakeholders who engage regularly with farming communities — such as extension officers, VBAs and
agro-dealers — will be trained as trainers, focussing on the subject matter outlined for Activity 1.1.2. In
addition to ensuring that trainers have a context-specific extension content and firm understanding of the
training content, they will be trained on the practical approaches needed to effectively reach and engage
communities, including on the setup of trainings and the use of demonstrations and promotional materials,
as well as concepts of gender-sensitivity and youth engagement. This training will enable these
stakeholders to integrate the concepts of FL-RS promoted by the project into their day-to-day support
provided to smallholder farmers, leading to further proliferation of knowledge to the broader farming
community in the target districts. Training will target 5,350 VBAs or extension officers across the seven
countries.
Activity 1.1.5: Conduct scoping missions to identify and engage manufacturers of FL-RS equipment.
74. Under this activity, AGRA will develop an inventory and characterization of FL-RS equipment
manufacturers and agro-dealers that will be engaged in developing/strengthening local markets and
enhancing the immediate availability of affordable of FL-RS (establishing the basis for delivery models
under Component 2). By mapping the market and building networks of local manufacturers and
importers/agro-dealers, this activity will enable a better understanding of current suppliers of FL-RS, and
their production scalability potential. To ensure the reach of FL-RS to remote and rural areas, the
programme will facilitate engagement and increased collaboration between solution manufacturers and
local agro-dealers in those target areas, thereby ensuring availability of solutions, and their accessibility
by local farmers. Where feasible the programme will facilitate the development or strengthening of
platforms that will bring together demand, supply and other stakeholders that enhance commerce and an
exchange of goods and services.
75. The scoping missions will include assessments of the current market in each target region to better
understand the market needs and opportunities. This will include identifying opportunities for exchanges
between African manufacturers and companies in other developing countries on the continent and beyond
to help introduce new technologies into the African market. Through these scoping missions, AGRA will
facilitate knowledge and technology sharing and transfer between suppliers, farmers, and service
providers.
Activity 1.1.6: Conduct demonstrations of locally-appropriate FL-RS
76. The programme will facilitate active demonstrations of short-listed FL-RS to target communities. This
activity will be sequenced after and will build on the relationships developed under Activity 1.1.5, partnering
with vendors for the implementation of product demonstrations, leveraging the increased sales of their
solutions through the programme-supported outreach as an incentive for vendors to engage in
demonstrations. Under this model, agro-dealers or suppliers set up and/or provide the inputs for an FL-
RS demonstration that will be established either at the agro-dealers' property or on-farm in partnership
with the smallholder farmers. These demonstration sites act as learning points, while also connecting
farmers to the suppliers of the products. Where possible, demonstrations will be done in conjunction with
the training under Activity 1.1.2 to improve efficiencies and ensure full connectivity between the theoretical
training and practical demonstrations. Demonstrations, trainings and the awareness creation of the food
loss–climate change nexus will be designed taking into account local context. They will take the form of a
combination of on-farm and communal demonstrations, presented through one-on-one farmer trainings,
innovation hubs, and by agro-dealers, targeting farmer and youth groups, aggregators and processors.
The project will undertake detailed participatory assessment to design and implement the various
modalities of demonstrations and trainings. At least 2 demonstrations will be done in each target district
per year for five years, resulting in a total of 410 total demonstrations.
Activity 1.1.7: Provide technical assistance to food processors to facilitate a shift to whole-grain flour
production.
77. In addition to the FL-RS approaches that will be promoted above, the programme will provide technical
assistance to promote the transition from refining grains to wholegrain flour production. As depicted in

=== PAGE 32 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 30
B
