the same quantity of unprocessed wheat, helping reduce the “hidden food loss” associated with over
processing. The intervention will target food producers – millers in particular – providing technical
assistance along several themes, including: i) developing MSME capacity; ii) raising awareness of market
opportunities for wholegrains; iii) support for technology transfers; and iv) facilitating access to finance. At
least 10% of processers trained under 1.1.3 will be targeted for training on wholegrain production, targeting
2,200 people total.
78. To maximise demand for the increased production of whole
grains, RE-GAIN will create linkages with major existing
programmes and programmes in the areas of school meals
and wholegrain. The activity will engage broader initiatives
that are promoting wholegrain food for use in institutional
markets such as schools that not only act a pull mechanism
for markets but works at sustainably changing behaviours
for future markets. For example, Rockefeller Foundation has
large programs to promote the use of FWG in school meal
programs. This will create the demand for FWG.
Output 1.2: Improved market linkages between agri-value
chain actors
79. The proliferation of unstructured market systems is one of
the major causes of post-harvest losses, with poor handling
Figure 6. Benefits of wholegrain flour production as
and low compliance with quality standards creating
an approach to reducing food waste.
substantial risk for produce during the post-harvest phase of
production. To address this challenge, RE-GAIN will provide technical assistance to enable the
development of more structured relationships between producers, value-adding agents and off-takers. By
structuring the linkages on the premise of increased food quality linked to improved post-harvest handling,
these activities will create incentives for quality compliance and investments in climate-resilient FL-RS.
Moreover, the improved market linkages will help de-risk potential external investments in FL-RS,
enhancing opportunities for smallholder farmers and cooperatives to adopt FL-RS at scale.
Activity 1.2.1: Provide technical assistance to support the structuring of value chains and coordination
between market actors
80. The technical assistance under this activity will facilitate market linkages between smallholders, MSMEs
and potential buyers – such as retailers, processors and exporters – using the consortia model as tested
and proven by AGRA. Other opportunities such as institutional markets will also be explored on this
programme to optimise the role of markets in the reduction of food losses.
81. The process will start with an assessment of the existing market in each target community, drawing on
and expanding the initial market assessment conducted during preparation of the RE-GAIN programme
(see Annex 2). This extended market analysis will map out key actors in the agricultural value chains at
the local level, including potential institutional markets (e.g. schools, hospitals, prisons) that are not yet
fully accessible to smallholder farmers, but that have potential to act as reliable off-takers. Gaps in the
value chains will be mapped, drawing connections between various actors to identify opportunities for
filling gaps. Based on this stakeholder mapping, the programme will provide technical assistance to local
actors to structure value chains for different crops and institutional markets. Actions to develop linkages
will specifically consider existing constraints faced by women in the sector and identify and promote
opportunities for women to actively engage in the market.
82. This activity will culminate in clear action plans for structuring value chains for target crops in each
community, including an outline of business development opportunities (see Output 2.1) to fill gaps in the
market. The implementation of value chain development will be further supported by establishing local
coordination mechanisms and supporting the establishment and or strengthening of cooperatives where
needed to facilitate improved coordination between actors.

=== PAGE 33 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 31
B
Activity 1.2.2: Facilitate linkages between farmers and institutional markets such as school feeding
programs, food processors and other social safety programs.
83. Drawing on the structures identified under Activity 1.2.1, the RE-GAIN programme will engage key actors
in the agri-value chain to link smallholder farmers to institutional markets such as school feeding
programmes, food processors and other social safety programmes. AGRA will support local farmers and
cooperatives in connecting them with other actors in the value chain and provide technical assistance to
establish formal mechanisms that will secure offtake agreements for produce that meets the quality
standards of the institutional market. This will include support for accessing institutional markets for
wholegrain flour.
Component 2: FL-RS supply side development to increase availability and affordability of FL-RS
Outcome 2: Enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS
84. The second component will complement the first by enhancing the supply and affordability of FL-RS. This
will be achieved through two Outputs that address key gaps in the FL-RS value chain, introducing
innovations that transform the market. The first Output will build the capacity of local MSMEs to provide
access to FL-RS, either through improving local supply or offering services to farmers. This will be
complemented by the second Output, which will implement innovative financial mechanisms that will
catalyse investments in FL-RS through partnerships and conditional procurements18. Ultimately, when
combined with the demand-side interventions under Component 1, the two Outputs under this component
will unlock the local market, encouraging vendors of FL-RS equipment19 to invest in distribution channels,
marketing, maintenance capacity and other needed infrastructure to make the equipment available and
serviceable in a sustainable manner. This shift will lead to reduced prices and increased affordability,
creating a self-sustaining system that will enable the phased out of programme support.
Output 2.1: Business development support provided to local MSMEs to facilitate the improved provision of
FL-RS on local markets
85. Activities under this Output will develop the business case for investing in the FL-RS supply chain and
support local MSMEs to develop business operations for the supply of FL-RS services. At the heart of this
approach is the demonstration of solutions though pluralist extension services such as the VBA extension
systems model and agro-dealers along with employing service models that enable shared access to larger,
more expensive FL-RS equipment, such as harvesting machinery, mechanical multi-crop threshers and
shellers (preferably solar-powered), moisture meters and communal storage structures. To ensure the
sustainability of such models, the technical capacity of MSMEs will be enhanced, including for the
development of sustainable business cases and improved business management practices. Activities
under this Output will target at least 2,000 MSMEs.
Activity 2.1.1: Train new FL-RS service providers (MSMEs, cooperatives, incl. women- and youth-led
initiatives) on the setup and operation of FL-RS service provision operations
86. The RE-GAIN programme will identify and train local MSMEs and cooperatives to develop FL-RS service
provision operations in the target communities. This training will prioritise women- and youth-led initiatives
to maximise the social benefits and build resilience among these vulnerable groups20. The programme will
adopt a rigorous process of scouting and in-depth assessment using standardized tools of the potential
service providers and food processors complemented by intensive vendor support to make sure operations
and maintenance skills for the equipment are transferred. The identification of suitable entities will be
supported by government (decentralised and local) authorities as well as through digital platforms, social
18 Conditional procurements will act as a form of ‘smart subsidy’ through which AGRA will support the procurement of good in tandem with co-
investments by the farmers or MSMEs.
19 African manufacturers might need additional technical skills to produce efficient and durable equipment at scale, while India- and China-
based manufacturers might not see the market opportunity justifying their investment in the African market.
20 This will include encouraging MSMEs to engage with informal youth groups for the provision of services on the ground, with the MSME
playing a supervisory role over the youth groups, ensuring accountability and providing the youth group with an opportunity to build a track
record of successful operations and governance.

=== PAGE 34 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 32
B
media, industry players where potential service providers are connected or enrolled, and via the scoping
exercises under Activity 1.2.1 (and will therefore be sequenced after Activity 1.2.1).
87. Once service providers are identified and enrolled on the programme, a rigorous assessment of their
capacity and skills will be conducted, before organising intensive “boot camps” to train these entities on
the FL-RS service provision. These training camps will cover several technical topics related to FL-RS
service provision, including: i) service management and process optimization; ii) the safe, effective,
efficient and sustainable operation of the equipment; iii) compliance with international and local quality
standards for post-harvest services; and iv) the management of the fee-for-service model. In addition to
the technical training on FL-RS service provision, entities will be supported on develop or strengthening
their business delivery models into a sustainable entity. This will include training on basic business
practices (such as financial literacy, cash flow management, record keeping, and risk management) and
partnership development support, as well as training on value-add services such as certifications
processes to enhance product value and market access. Guidance will also be provided on how to access
to finance through innovative de-risking schemes under Output 2.2.
88. Vendors of the FL-RS equipment will be engaged in the implementation of this activity, incentivising their
distribution channels to increase their outreach activities, including though VBAs and other last mile
delivery channels. This model is not only sustainable in the long run but also creates an incentive for end
users to scale up the technology in the very initial stages. Exchange visits will also be arranged to share
experiences between service providers operating in nearby villages.
Activity 2.1.2: Provide business development support and market intelligence
89. To complement the development of MSME service providers, the RE-GAIN programme will support
business development among manufacturers, importers and distributors of FL-RS to boost local markets.
This will include providing market intelligence to increase marketing, distribution and after-sales services
to develop the supply chain for FL-RS products.
▪ Support for the youth groups to be able to run a "fee for service" business, including financial
management, equipment management and marketing and business planning
▪ Ecosystem support/market intelligence which includes mapping available FL-RS equipment, feasibility
studies for FL-RS deployment, scoping of the potential demand for FL-RS services, mapping of
financial services for FL-RS purchase, among others.
90. Through this training, RE-GAIN will showcase functional and validated components of FL-RS in specific
country contexts and develop these as scenario-based business models that will be validated and used
for expanding business opportunities. Entities will also be supported to develop partnerships with other
actors in the supply chain, as well as to engage with innovative de-risking schemes piloted under Output
2.2. The business development support will focus on two main elements:
Activity 2.1.3: Capacity and market development for all supply-side market actors
91. In addition to the targeted support for FL-RS suppliers and service providers, RE-GAIN will provide general
capacity development support to actors across the FL-RS supply chain to further develop the local supply
market for FL-RS. The nature of this support will vary depending on the context of each target country and
the actors involved but could include the convening of actors (including manufacturers, agro-dealers,
service providers, MSMEs and financial institutions) and development of partnerships (this will be done by
the CIU with support from the PIU), establishment of cooperatives to optimise the procurement and shared
use of FL-RS among farmers (through other AGRA programming and other development partners’ work
with smallholder farmers; including that of CNFA, Agriterra, ACDI VOCA, Technoserve, IFDC, SNV, and
local NGO partners), market mapping and dissemination of market intelligence reports (using AGRA
knowledge management and networks such as SAFIN and AMEA), and building network of local
manufacturers and importers/agro-dealers (through support to private sector associations provided as part
of AGRA and other partners’ other programming and interventions). Implementation of the supply-side
market development support will include linkages with the agri-value chains market development initiatives
under 1.2.1, sharing resources to optimise cost efficiency and co-develop both sides of the market.
Output 2.2: Financial mechanisms established to support smallholders and MSMEs to adopt FL-RS

=== PAGE 35 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 33
B
92. One of the key transformative approaches in the RE-GAIN programme is the introduction of innovative
financial models that will strategically use GCF grants as catalysts to develop the market for FL-RS. The
approach taken in financial model design is focused on mitigating the current market dynamics and
challenges faced by smallholder farmers in each of the programme’s target countries, and to work with the
additional activities in the programme to respond to some of the key challenges identified and the gaps in
the current delivery of food-loss solutions. The goal of the financial model design is to specifically address
these challenges and support the growth and development of the market, as a whole. In addition to the
core financial mechanisms, the programme will support several other pathways to financing of FL-RS,
including the inclusion of FL-RS in existing input packages provided by local financial institutions, and
structuring prefinancing partnership arrangements that include FL-RS.
Activity 2.2.1. Facilitate the development and deployment of catalytic procurement models for FL-RS
focussing on women and youth collectives, and enterprises as key beneficiaries.
93. Under this Activity, RE-GAIN will implement two financial models split into two distinct categories:
interventions at a smallholder level; and interventions at a youth group/co-operative level. An overview of
each of the two financial models is provided below, with further details – including descriptions of the key
actors and eligibility criteria – provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. In all instances, the models draw on
the outputs of the scoping exercises under Activities 1.1.5 and 1.2.1 to identify the potential actors and
tailor the models to the local context. The models leverage partnerships between these actors to share
risk and facilitate and incentivise the development of robust markets.
94. Interventions at a smallholder farmer level
95. Model 1 promotes the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional procurements to effectively
subsidise interventions at smallholder farmer level (referred to hereafter as ‘smart-subsidies’). At its base,
the smart-subsidy model enables agro-dealers to provide FL-RS to smallholder farmers at a discounted
rate by using GCF funds to procure one item for every two items21 procured and sold by an agro-dealer.
The subsidy is passed down to the smallholder farmers as a discount on the purchase price22.
96. The interventions are typically focused on smallholder farmers, with lower individual ticket sizes to the end-
users — i.e. for FL-RS such as tarpaulins and plastic sheets, metal and plastic silos, and hermetic bags.
The flow of funds will be facilitated through a trust/escrow account23 at a local financial institution (FI) and
released once proof of offtake by eligible smallholder farmers has been obtained. The instruction for
release of the funds will be made by AGRA once the implementation unit is satisfied that the conditions
have been met. On release, the funds will be transferred directly to the supplier. However, while funds will
pass through an FI, it should be noted that no GCF funds will be transferred to a financial institution as the
end beneficiary. The aim of the model is two-fold:
▪ to stimulate production and manufacturer capacity by placing pre-emptive orders of FL-RS, however,
maintaining control over risk through a conditional release of funds to the manufacturer; and
▪ to reduce the cost of interventions at smallholder farmer level, additional profitability will drive
additional demand and facilitate knowledge sharing about the benefits of interventions.
97. An overview of Model 1 is presented in Figure 7, with more detailed descriptions of each step in the text
that follows. Under Model 1, RE-GAIN will target ~540,000 households for adoption of FL-RS. Average
ticket size is $153 per household, of which approximately 30% on average will be subsidized through
Model 1.
21 Note: the specific ratios will vary based on context of local markets in each country, with more developed markets receiving a smaller
subsidy than less developed markets. A three-for-two model has been used as a base for demonstration purposes. The rations for each target
market will be finalised when the contract between suppliers FIs and AGRA are entered into. RE-GAIN staff will initiate negotiations on value
for money in line with the state of the local market, which will be used to determine the ratios.
22 The maximum retail sales price and number of items per customer — as well as the eligibility criteria and geography where the sales at
reduced cost can be made — will be included as part of the agreement between AGRA and the suppliers as a condition for participation in the
scheme.
23 The account will be held in AGRA’s name with AGRA as the primary beneficiary. Depending on the agreements reached, the other parties
(suppliers/financiers) will be included as co-signers as needed; however AGRA will maintain final say.

=== PAGE 36 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 34
B
Figure 7. Schematic of Financial Model 1.
98. Step 1: RE-GAIN will facilitate the agreement and AGRA will enter into an agreement with a supplier. Each
supplier will act through its agro-dealer network in the areas where eligible farmers are based. During this
process, the details of the agreement will be negotiated between parties, including the level of smart
subsidy provided and the conditions on final sale price that will be provided to the smallholder farmers.
Through this process, AGRA will pass down eligibility criteria and the conditions of the agreement to the
agro-dealers, ensuring that the benefits of the smart-subsidies are reaching the target groups.
99. Step 2: RE-GAIN places an order for FL-RS and deposits the value of the order into a holding account.
Funds to be released on the completion of Steps 4-8.
100. Step 3: The supplier provides three units to the participating agro-dealers for every one unit procured by
RE-GAIN. In some cases, the agro-dealers pay the supplier for the two non-subsidized units on delivery.
In other cases, the suppliers give the units on credit (see Step 7).
101. Step 4: As per the agreement set out under Step 1, the agro-dealer offers the FL-RS to smallholder
farmers at a discounted rate24, transferring the full value of the smart-subsidy provided through GCF
support to the farmers. Agro-dealers will take details of the buyers of the subsidized goods (there is a limit
to how many bags per person can be bought to maintain the demonstration goal and prevent re-selling)
and report on the sales made. This will help RE-GAIN to monitor the transfer of benefits to target groups
and ensure impact is being achieved.
102. Step 5: Farmers buy the FL-RS at the discounted rate.25
103. Step 6: The agro-dealer makes a payment to the manufacturer for two units for every one unit of the initial
procurement from RE-GAIN (if not already paid on delivery; see Step 3). This payment — and the
24 Note: Given that agro-dealers will need to apply a markup to the FL-RS to account for their income in the deal, the discount to the farmers
might fall below 33% of the usual market price. However, agro-dealers bound by the nature of the agreement to transfer the full value of the
subsidy to the farmers.
25 If needed, the farmer may approach a micro-finance institution (MFI) to help secure a short-term bridging loan to purchase the FL-RS before
or at harvest, when income might only be expected further down the line
.

=== PAGE 37 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 35
B
submission of a corresponding report detailing the conclusion of the agreement — will trigger the release
of the smart-subsidy payment through RE-GAIN to the supplier.
104. Should the suppliers, agro-dealers or farmers require additional finance for their role in the system, they
will be able to approach local financial institutions that already exist in all the target countries. For example,
if the supplier needs additional working capital or capital investment to satisfy the additional FL-RS
demand26, they can enter into a loan agreement with an FI to cover the additional liquidity needs for
providing FL-RS. While AGRA can offer advice to suppliers or agro-dealers on this matter, the agreements
themselves will be outside of the scope of the RE-GAIN Programme and will not include AGRA. The order
placed by RE-GAIN will help de-risk the loan provision to the supplier, but no RE-GAIN Programme funds
will be used to lend to the supplier or to make payments to an FI.
105. This model creates a win-win scenario for all actors, with the manufacturer ultimately receiving the full
value of the FL-RS provided, the agro-dealer making income from their markup, and the farmers receiving
FL-RS at a highly discounted rate. The market created will enable manufacturers to increase production
with reduced risk, eventually bringing down the cost of FL-RS in the local market, enabling the smart-
subsidies to be phased out.
106. Interventions at a cooperative or SME level
107. Model 2 will use GCF grants to provide a unique approach to unlocking higher value items and food loss
reducing equipment to farmer collectives or small to medium sized enterprises. The term ‘youth groups’ is
used in this model description to highlight the priority given to this vulnerable demographic27; however, the
model will also apply to other cooperatives, including women’s groups and local MSMEs. The aim of Model
2 is to create multi-stakeholder agreements with suppliers, youth groups, and financial institutions, with
the goal of:
▪ Enhancing creditworthiness – through repurchase assurances from the suppliers that lower the loss
given default.
▪ Reducing borrowing costs – Through a combination of the lowered credit risk (as per above) and
subsidies on the purchase price. The structure will ensure higher value FL-RS become more
affordable and thus accessible to youth groups who provide services to smallholder farmers.
108. At the heart of Model 2 is the engagement of local youth groups that will be supported to act as service
providers for FL-RS that require more expensive equipment that can service multiple farmers — such as
mechanical multi-crop threshers and shellers (preferably solar-powered), moisture meters and communal
storage structures. The establishment of the service operations will be supported through the business
development under Output 2.1, ensuring that the youth groups have the necessary foundation to provide
a reliable service28. However, it is recognised that despite the proposed capacity development activities,
some informal groups may still not fully meet the eligibility criteria for Model 2. In such instances, RE-GAIN
will work with formalised MSMEs to partner with local youth groups; with the established MSMEs taking
the lead in entering into the multistakeholder agreement (as per Step 1 below) and taking accountability
for the management of the agreement. The MSMEs will recruit and work with informal youth groups from
the community to operate the equipment and in the process develop their capacities29. Through this
MSME-supervised approach, the informal youth groups will have the opportunity to build a track record,
demonstrating their governance structures and creditworthiness for future opportunities. This approach
leverages several key concepts to unlock the targeted benefits:
▪ Collectivism provides benefits to smallholder farmers in economies of scale through cost sharing and
increased bargaining power with off-takers. These should promote further profitability and therefore
additional demand for FL-RS.
▪ Post-harvest handling will increase the quality and quantity of agricultural produce, allowing
smallholder farmers to capture more value, hence increased incomes.
26 For example, to finance working capital, additional processing lines or imports that are required to satisfy the additional demand for FL-RS
27 Preferentially targeting youth groups will help build resilience among this vulnerable group of the population, as well as unlock the energy
and innovation that youth can bring to the process.
28 Including governance and leadership capacity, as well as financial literacy and business knowledge
29 The income generated by the equipment is shared between the MSME and the youth group, whereby the MSME bears the credit risk and
the informal group the operational risk.

=== PAGE 38 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 36
B
▪ The inclusion of financiers will further unlock access to finance in a typically underserved market. The
structure aims to reduce credit risk by providing a partial subsidy that will lead to lower borrowing costs
(thanks to smaller loan size and interest payments).
109. While the concessionality under this model is first and foremost targeted at the youth groups themselves
— as livelihood development support to these vulnerable members of the communities — when combined
with the business development support, the RE-GAIN programme will enable youth groups to structure
their fees to account for the actual (discounted) cost of the equipment and provide a service at a fair rate,
which will in turn indirectly pass the benefits of the concessions down to the farmers using the service.
110. An overview of Model 2 is presented in Figure 8, with more detailed descriptions of each step in the text
that follows. While RE-GAIN will facilitate the establishment of the full process, the active involvement of
the RE-GAIN programme beyond Step 4 will focus on monitoring the delivery of services and providing
continued support to youth groups. The ownership of Steps 5-9 lies directly with the three partners (youth
groups, suppliers and FIs) who will enter into a separate loan agreement to which AGRA will not be a
party. This model will target the 2,000 service providers supported under Output 2.1. Average ticket size
is $153 per household, of which approximately 30% on average will be subsidized through Model 2. The
total market value of each ticket item is comprised of the collective inputs of between 100 and 200 people
per solution.
Figure 8. Schematic of Financial Model 2.
111. Step 1: Drawing on the scoping exercises under Activities 1.1.5 and 1.2.1, the RE-GAIN programme will
match youth groups with suppliers and FIs, facilitating the signing of a multi-stakeholder agreement. Under
this agreement, AGRA will commit to an upfront co-payment of 30%30 of the purchase price for the targeted
equipment, on condition that the youth group commits to the remaining 70%. The youth group payment
will in turn be facilitated through a loan from the partner FI, with the supplier providing a repurchase
assurance, thereby sharing the risk between the supplier and the FI. RE-GAIN will facilitate the
negotiations, ensuring that all aspects of the agreement meet the eligibility criteria.
112. Step 2: Once the agreement is in place, the FI will deposit the 70% down-payment directly into the
supplier’s account on behalf of the youth group, triggering Step 3. At this stage, the remaining 30% co-
30 Note: 30% is used as an average co-payment value for demonstration purposes; however, the specific co-payment will vary depending on
the nature of the goods purchased and the state of development of the local market.

=== PAGE 39 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 37
B
payment is deposited into a blocked USD holding account, pending delivery of the equipment to trigger its
release (see Step 4).
113. Step 3: On receipt of the 70% payment from the FI, the supplier delivers the equipment to the youth group
and reports on delivery to AGRA’s RE-GAIN PIU.
114. Step 4: On receipt of the delivery report from the supplier, AGRA will release the 30% co-payment from
the holding account to the supplier, completing the initial purchase agreement. At this stage, the youth
group will have control over the use of the equipment, but ownership of the assets remains with the supplier
or the FI (as agreed during Step 1).
115. Step 5: The youth group, now in possession of the equipment, provides an FL-RS service to local farmers.
Support under Output 2.1 will ensure that the youth groups are adequately capacitated to operate the
service enterprise.
116. Step 6: Smallholder farmers pay the youth group for the FL-RS service, with the youth group collecting
income from multiple farmers; thereby distributing the cost of the equipment across multiple beneficiaries.
117. Step 7: The youth groups use the income from the services to make repayments to the FI on the loan,
covering the cost of the loan and the agreed interest. The upfront co-payment through RE-GAIN reduces
the repayment burden on youth groups as compared to a scenario where a 100% loan would have been
required, thereby decreasing the loan loss given default.
118. Step 8: At the end of the agreed loan period, the FI concludes the transaction and reports on the outcome
of the repayment, triggering Step 9.
119. Step 9: The conclusion of the transaction will lead to one of two possible outcomes, as outlined below.
a. Outcome 1: Market development was successful, indicated by the youth group operating an FL-RS
service, enabling the full repayment of the loan. Under this scenario, the ownership of the asset will
be formally transferred to the youth group, which will be able to continue to offer the service beyond
the initial agreement, without the costs of servicing the loan.
b. Outcome 2: Market development was unsuccessful, indicated by the failure of the youth group to make
the required repayments on the loan. Under this scenario, the supplier’s repurchase assurance is
triggered, through which the supplier buys back the asset (accounting for depreciation). The value of
the repurchase will first go towards the repayment of any outstanding loan amount, and any associated
transaction fees. Should the repurchase value exceed the outstanding loan amount, with any
remaining value after transaction fees will be transferred to back to the youth group to compensate
for any payments made before default.
120. Alternative variations to these models may be introduced depending on the local context and the nature
of the FL-RS. For example, the agreements may be adjusted to incorporate a rent-to-own mechanism31
directly with the supplier, through which incentives would be created for employment of local youth to
operate the machines, with these youth taking ownership of the machine once the original cost of the
machines is paid back from income generated by the machine. In all cases, GCF grants will be used to
make a co-payment on the equipment on behalf of the beneficiary (youth group or MSME), thereby
reducing the financial burden of the transaction and de-risking the transaction for the suppliers or FIs
involved in the agreement.
Activity 2.2.2. Support inclusion of FL-RS in climate-resilient input packages provided by local financial
institutions
121. While access to credit for farmers is limited across all the target countries, there are increasing efforts to
develop tailored agricultural input packages from various financiers and government input subsidy
31 The rent-to-own model is a variation of Model 2, where the supplier can provide the equipment to the youth group without the need to
include an FI. Under this variation, the youth group will make monthly rental payments to the supplier, but the ownership will remain with the
supplier until such time that the agreed purchase value has been reached. In such instances, the GCF contribution will be used as a
downpayment to reduce the monthly rental fees, or to reduce the duration of the rent-to-buy agreement.

=== PAGE 40 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 38
B
programs. Financing is provided mostly through rural financial institutions, cooperatives, digital and
alternative lenders, that assist farmers in accessing agricultural inputs. However, these packages generally
focus on initial production, often excluding FL-RS from the list of supported inputs. The RE-GAIN
programme will, therefore, provide technical support through CIU and AGRA staff time to facilitate
embedding the cost of FL-RS (e.g. hermetic technology) within the existing financing arrangements for
input subsidies (in countries where such subsidy schemes exist like Ethiopia, Malawi and Zambia) to allow
farmers to try out the technology and ask for inclusion in their input loan package in the following years.
Under this activity, GCF funds will not be used to directly fund subsidies, but rather to promote the budling
of FL-RS into existing subsidy programmes.
Activity 2.2.3. Structure prefinancing partnership arrangements that include FL-RS
122. To further unlock financing for accessing FL-RS, the RE-GAIN programme will provide technical
assistance through its PIU, CIU and AGRA staff to key value chain players — for example off-takers, input
suppliers and farmers — and financial institutions to support the structuring of prefinancing partnerships
that will derisk the provision of finance. Assistance will include convening key actors (through workshops,
meetings, or other collaborative platforms), and training/capacity development that will help structure
financial products based on risk sharing and risk reducing arrangements Some of these products are
widely promoted by AGRA, after they have been successfully implemented in several of its focus countries,
including Burkina Faso, Tanzania and Malawi.
Component 3: Enabling environment to ensure sustainability of the FL-RS market
Outcome 3: Strengthened enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS
123. The impacts of climate change on food loss in Africa is aggravated by the lack of an inadequate policy
environment to support the development of sustainable markets for FL-RS. These policy-level constraints
include the limited: i) availability of pan-African platforms on climate change that would facilitate action to
minimize post-harvest losses and that are supported by evidence and responding to African needs and
priorities; ii) access to climate change adaptation incentives, particularly finance to catalyse action and
support SME investments; iii) weak regulatory and enforcement mechanism that would have reduced food
loss and iv) biosystems-specific climate policies and implementation strategies supporting the
entrepreneurial skills of youth in minimizing food losses. In response to the challenges, Component 3 of
the RE-GAIN programme will support the development of an enabling environment for the adoption of FL-
RS at scale, working within the existing policy environment where needed, and supporting change where
possible.
Output 3.1: Enhanced capacity of national institutions to enable investments in FL-RS
124. To increase the sustainability and scalability of the positive impact created by the improved demand and
supply of FL-RS, and improved access to financing and established market linkages, RE-GAIN programme
will support the revision and operationalization of policies in support of enabling FL-RS investments,
including tax exemptions/incentives, certification and standards for FL-RS quality, and promote successful
FL-RS business models for scaling-up and replication.
125. Active involvement and support from government organizations, both central and local, will be crucial. The
programme will align with other projects and programmes to leverage synergies, utilize existing laws and
policies on FL reduction, SME promotion, and smallholder support, and ensure effective and efficient
programme management. This includes rigorous monitoring and incorporating lessons learned.
126. Effective stakeholder engagement is essential and will involve raising awareness, providing programme
information, and ensuring inclusivity for women, youth, minority groups, and all value chain actors. A
grievance mechanism will also be put in place, with programme funds being used for awareness creation
and mobilization through stakeholder engagement meetings32. Additionally, ensuring the availability of
quality FL-RS and access to finance is vital to support long-term continuation.
32 The cost of the grievance mechanism itself will be covered by AGRA using AE Fees.

=== PAGE 41 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 39
B
Activity 3.1.1: Support the revision of policies in support of enabling FL-RS investments, including tax
exemptions, reduced import duties, certification and standards for FL-RS quality
127. Under this Activity, AGRA will collaborate with government entities to identify critical policy gaps related to
food loss, and revise and update key policies which are not responding to the current and future food
losses and climate challenge scenario. This will include engaging relevant government ministries and
agencies to review existing regulations and policies and proposing amendments to facilitate the
development of local markets and support to farmers for the uptake of FL-RS. This will include
introducing/strengthening national policies that provide financial incentives such as tax exemptions,
subsidies, or reduced interest rates for FL-RS investments, making it more attractive for private and public
sector investments33. Evidence generated and lessons learned in FL-RS from target communities and
counties will be used to develop capacity, influence policy and promote best practices at scale.
128. AGRA will also actively participate in policy advocacy initiatives, working with stakeholders to ensure that
proposed changes support the long-term success of FL-RS initiatives as part of the broader food systems
of the respective countries. In addition to support for policy revision, AGRA will support local entities in
implementing existing policies and regulations that have potential to support the uptake of FL-RS but are
currently not fully enforced or supported. These actions will result in a supportive policy environment that
enables the successful implementation of the RE-GAIN programme and widespread adoption of FL-RS
solutions.
Activity 3.1.2: Support institutional capacity building of key local entities to support the scaling of successful
FL-RS models.
129. Through this activity, AGRA will provide technical assistance to local and national government entities
engaged in crop value chains (directly or indirectly) to build their institutional capacity to support the scaling
of successful FL-RS models and policies in their countries. This will include actively involving central and
local government through the establishment of formal partnerships and facilitating regular meetings and
consultations to ensure alignment of the RE-GAIN programme with national and regional development
priorities. These partnerships will be complemented by technical assistance, including guidelines, targeting
tools and technology delivery mechanisms, to develop the analytical capacity of partner institutions on the
topics of climate change and the contribution of FL-RS to GHG reduction and food security — enabling
these metrics to be tracked and reported on in relation to the NDCs — as well as for the scenario-based
prediction of mycotoxins during post-harvest period under changing climate.
130. RE-GAIN will also work with governments and other development partners to promote private sector
investment in post-harvest infrastructure, including storage facilities, processing units and cold chains, as
well as supporting regulatory bodies to develop and implement quality standards and certification
processes for FL-RS products and services, ensuring they meet required levels of reliability and efficiency.
Support will also be provided to enhance the capacities of extension services, and climate advisory
services to monitor food loss in relation to climate conditions and the impact of FL-RS in reducing loss, as
well as identifying and reporting on opportunities for upscaling and replication.
131. Finally, the programme will leverage on other investments to create synergies with other players in building
institutional capacity of governments and the private sector in FL-RS, which may include enhancing
coordination among country actors and institutions dealing with post-harvest loss under changing climates.
Activity 3.1.3: Develop a business case for investments in FL-RS manufacturing, supply, service provision
and financing to promote successful FL-RS business models for scaling-up and replication
132. A key step in unlocking investments and creating sustainable markets for FL-RS in Africa revolves around
creating solid business cases that account for the needs of all actors – including manufacturers, suppliers,
financers, agro-dealers, service providers and farmers. RE-GAIN will, therefore, research and compile
case studies of successful FL-RS initiatives implemented through the programme, as well as other best
practices observed across the region and develop models and approaches for scaling and influence. This
research will form the foundation of the business case for FL-RS, detailing best practice and benefits of
33 Note: RE-GAIN will advocate at Government level to create a more enabling environment for FL-RS but will not directly cover the financing
on tax incentives or subsidies.

=== PAGE 42 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 40
B
various FL-RS related activities, including equipment selection, business model, profitability analysis, and
financing models. The business case will be packaged into various communication materials, including
reports, videos, and presentations, to share these models with potential investors, policy-makers, and
other stakeholders, encouraging scaling-up and replication.
Activity 3.1.4: Implement a comprehensive monitoring, evaluation and learning system for FL-RS
133. The programme will develop a robust monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) framework that includes
clear performance indicators, data collection methods, and regular reporting schedules. This system will
build on the foundation of existing MEL frameworks within the AGRA system to track the progress of
programme activities, assess the impact of interventions, and identify areas for improvement. The MEL
framework will also establish mechanisms for continuous learning by regularly reviewing M&E data,
conducting periodic evaluations, and gathering feedback from stakeholders. These insights will be used
to make informed adjustments to the programme, ensuring that it remains relevant and effective.
134. Through the MEL framework, the RE-GAIN Programme will foster knowledge sharing, improve
performance through a continuous feedback loop and collaboration among stakeholders. This will be
supported through the activities under Components 1 and 2 to encourage the formation of farmer groups,
cooperatives and networks where farmers, MSMEs and private sector actors can exchange experiences,
share best practices and collectively address challenges related to post-harvest management. The
knowledge generated will be also shared regionally through AGRA’s networks to promote the scaling of
the successful models across Africa and beyond. In the spirit of south-south cooperation, the programme
will also coordinate with other projects to create synergies, identifying areas of overlap and collaboration.
This will include developing joint action plans, sharing resources, and coordinating activities to maximize
impact and avoid duplication of efforts.
B.4. Implementation arrangements
PSAA Applicant
135. AGRA — an African-led institution with a vision of contributing to a food system-inspired inclusive
agricultural transformation across Africa, of reducing hunger, improving nutrition, and adapting to climate
change, and has been scaling agricultural innovations that help smallholder farmers towards increased
incomes, better livelihoods, and improved food security will serve as the PSAA Applicant for this project.
136. AGRA was founded in 2006, bringing 18 years of experience in a varied sectors related to food security,
including seeds systems, sustainable farming, policy and state capability and inclusive markets and trade.
Efforts across these main thematic areas are built on the foundation of several key cross-cutting themes,
particularly climate change, nutrition and gender responsiveness.
137. The organisation operates in 12 countries, employing over 300 staff, and managing an average annual
operating budget of USD 90 million. AGRA also hosts the Partnership for Inclusive Agriculture
Transformation in Africa (PIATA); a partnership between AGRA and five other major donors (BMGF,
Rockefeller, BMW, MCF, FCDO and USAID) with AGRA as the implementing partner. PIATA is focused
on working with public and private partners to support 12 countries in Africa to create and sustain Inclusive
Agriculture transformation with a budget of over USD 500M in the next five years.
138. As a continental institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA is well placed to undertake the planned
activities through its widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, having strong AGRA
country teams in the target countries. Using AGRA’s continental forums, the Africa Food Systems Forum
(AFSF) and Agribusiness Deal Room, where AGRA brings on board connections with governments,
partners and other stakeholders to enhance ownership and coordinate key actors, knowledge and
resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.
139. AGRA is known as a trusted broker by the governments it works with which will enable it to ensure the
programme is aligned with national climate change policies, strategies, plans and instruments. Using
AGRA’s ongoing projects across the continent as leverage, AGRA will drive a paradigm shift for building
the resilience of smallholder farmers and agricultural communities in the seven focus countries and across
Africa. The lessons learnt, especially on financing food loss reduction and its relationship with climate
change, will have the potential of generating new knowledge on ways of engaging MSMEs and smallholder

=== PAGE 43 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 41
B
farmers in reducing their harvest and post-harvest losses and thus increasing their productivity and
resilience to climate shocks.
140. The AGRA HQ senior leadership and technical leads will be responsible for the overall supervision and
coordination of the project, including ensuring: i) funds are effectively managed to deliver results and
achieve objectives; ii) the quality of project monitoring; and iii) liaison with the GCF. AGRA will also
leverage expertise from its wider technical leadership and support by AGRA’s Heads of Markets and
Trade, Inclusive Finance, Sustainable Farming, Private-sector Partnerships, Strategy, Policy and State
Capability, Monitoring and Evaluation and Knowledge Management. The AGRA HQ team will be the
primarily liaison with the GCF.
Executing Entity (EE)
141. The project will be executed directly by AGRA through Programme Implementation Unit (PIU)34. Through
this unit, AGRA will provide key resources, including Finance, Grant Management and Procurement
Officers who will provide financial and administrative management, overseeing financial, contractual,
procurement and logistics aspects for the project from the Nairobi Headquarters. The unit will oversee
planning and quality assurance; supervise programme monitoring, evaluation and reporting; ensure timely
realization of all programme deliverables; provide leadership and technical support to implementing
partners; and ensure smooth communication flow across all programme partners. This executing role will
be fulfilled both through the Nairobi-based headquarters, and AGRA’s country offices, and will report to
the AGRA senior leadership.
142. The EE is responsible for:
▪ Execution of the project,
▪ Procurement of services specifically (major procurement and contracting)
▪ Facilitating partnerships,
▪ Managing contracts, monitoring results,
▪ Annual reporting by country offices to the PIU
143. AGRA deploys a diverse set of delivery models to deliver its country and institutional strategy. It offers
services through its expert staff, placed at headquarters in Nairobi; at the East, Southern and West Africa
regional offices; as well as at country offices. AGRA staff work with downstream partners and local
organizations to implement specific components of a contracted programme area with the aim to improve
local organizations’ capacity, build institutional capacity and ensure long term ownership and sustainability
of its interventions. AGRA provides Technical Assistance (TA) in the form of short- to medium-term
expertise support (through consultants where needed) embedded within or seconded to mandated
national, regional and continental institutions (e.g., government ministries, regional economic
communities) to drive desired change, and in some instances consultants are hired to support specific
assignments that require skilled expertise. AGRA is a convener (brings stakeholders together around a
change agenda, e.g., the Africa Food Systems Summit) facilitating connections and interactions between
different actors and stakeholders within the agriculture and food systems sector. AGRA utilizes advocacy
and communication as key tools for change. The specific delivery models will be determined at the
implementation stage and will depend on each country context.
Responsible Units
144. The EE team at the Nairobi HQ will be supported by AGRA country offices in each of the seven target
countries who will serve as responsible units. These units will support on-the-ground coordination and
implementation, as well as being mandated for specific outputs/activities.
Programme Governance
Programme Advisory Group:
34 The PIU will fall under the same legal entity as the PSAA Applicant

=== PAGE 44 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 42
B
145. AGRA will establish a Programme Advisory Group (PAG) made up of senior representatives from AGRA’s
Integrated Programme Management (IPM) unit35 that will serve as the starting point to guide innovation,
impact scale and adaptive thought leadership to shape the partnership at the continental level. AGRA
envisions this Advisory Group will meet on quarterly as part of the IPM meetings.
Programme Implementation Unit
146. A central Programme Implementation Unit (PIU) will be established at AGRA’s Nairobi headquarters to
oversee implementation of the entire programme across all seven countries. This unit will report to the
PAG and be comprised of two sub-groups; a Programme Management Unit (PMU) and a Technical Expert
Group (TEG), as described below.
• Programme Management Unit
147. The Programme will establish a management unit that will be functional for the entire duration and be
responsible for day-to-day implementation of the project. The PMU will offer overall management,
implementation and general technical direction of the entire programme, ensuring an integrated vision
among different components. The PMU will consist of five full time positions: i) PMU Lead; ii) Senior
Finance Officer; iii) Procurement Officer; iv) Project Analyst; and v) M&E Officer. The PMU will be
based in AGRA Nairobi Headquarters, with in-country support from responsible units in the country
offices.
• Technical Expert Group
The TEG, also situated within the Nairobi Headquarters, will provide expertise to assist the PMU in
the technical implementation of the RE-GAIN programme. The TEG will include several full-time
positions, including: i) Program Officer — Gender, Youth and Inclusion; ii) Technical Advisor —
Inclusive Finance and BDS; iii) Technical Advisor — Extension and Value Chain Development. These
full-time roles will be supported by several part-time technical team members, including: i) Technical
Advisor — Inclusive Markets and Finance; ii) Lead — Sustainable Farming, Distribution and Youth in
Extension; iii) Technical Advisor — Livelihood Resilience and Climate Adaption; iv) Head: M&E; and
v) Technical Advisor — Food Loss Reduction Analytics.
Country-level Implementation Units
148. The PIU will be assisted in project implementation within each target country by a country-level
implementation unit (CIU) which will be established in each of the AGRA country offices36 and will be
comprised of country-office staff. The CIUs will be responsible for managing day-to-day operations in each
country, reporting directly to the PIU, as well as providing regular reports to the relevant Project Steering
Committee (see below). Specific responsibilities of the CIU for each project Output are outlined in the first
row of Table 6 below.
Programme Steering Committee
149. At the country level, the programme will be implemented under the overall guidance of a Programme
Steering Committee (PSC) co-chaired by a representative of the NDA, and AGRA country managers37.
The PSC will include representatives of other key government departments and agencies, the private
sector and civil society organizations. These partners will likely include Ministries of Agriculture and their
Departments for Land Resources Conservation, Crop Development, Agriculture Extension Services and
Agriculture Planning Services. The role of the PSC will be to: i) provide overall guidance and direction to
the project in country; ii) address project issues as raised by the advisory group; iii) review the project
progress and provide direction and recommendations to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced
satisfactorily and within the approved project framework; iv) review and approve annual work plan and
budget (AWPB) and provide necessary strategic guidance for its implementation; v) appraise the annual
project implementation report, including the quality assessment rating report; vi) make recommendations
for subsequent work plans to build on achievements and address any shortcomings; and vi) provide ad
35 Vice presidents, relevant business line or programme directors/heads, Lead of PMU , Head of MEL
36 Which fall under the same legal entity as the PSAA Applicant
37 The final composition and chairing of the PSC will be finalized at inception stage in collaboration with the NDA in each country.

hoc direction and advice for exceptional situations or when requested by the GCF, strategic advisory group
or PSC members.
150. Each national PSC will include representatives of private sector actors in addition to key government
institutions. A list of potential private partners is presented in Appendix 9 of Annex 2. The selection of
specific partners for each country will be led by AGRA and will be dependent on specific criteria as outlined
in Annex 2. At country level there will annual forums for feedback and policy dialogues that will be
organized by each county office. The lessons learned through the project monitoring, evaluation and
learning systems in each participating country will be shared to all other participating countries through
two approaches: i) Cross-country presentations at AGRA's internal Quarterly Performance Review
Meeting, where all country directors and program officers participate; and ii) an annual planning and review
session organized by the PMU in which all countries and partners participate to promote cross country
learnings, exposure and innovation. In addition, at continental level, the AFSF will organization special
sessions for cross country learning and feedback.
151. Each National PSC will convene in an interval of 3 months (quarterly) with a provision for additional
extraordinary meetings when required and to be called by the chair and co-chair or if requested by
members. The PSC will report to the NDA who oversees all GCF project in the individual countries.
Stakeholder Engagement
152. Across the different countries, AGRA will liaise with different governmental agencies during the
implementation of the different outputs to ensure that the RE-GAIN programme is aligned with country-
specific policies. A non-exhaustive list of these stakeholders is provided below and will be further updated
through engagement with the NDA’s selected representative in each country. Further information of the
key players working across the FL-RS adoption in the key markets is available in Annex 2, with a full list
of stakeholders already engaged during the design of the RE-GAIN programme in Annex 7’s Appendices.
153. The table below also outlines the key roles and responsibilities that AGRA will have in each countries in
the implementation across the different outputs.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request
156. Africa's food insecurity challenge has been exacerbated by climate change. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at
a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity for agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of
a rapidly growing population of 1.2 billion and the pressures of a changing climate38. The continent faces
significant development challenges including food insecurity, resource degradation, poverty, gender
inequality, and social exclusion. The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is
38 World Bank, 2023
Figure 9. RE-GAIN Programme flow of funds.

=== PAGE 49 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 47
B
evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to
climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. These challenges are being
exacerbated by climate change, which is impacting the full spectrum of food production system. Most
relevant of these to RE-GAIN is the expected increase in post-harvest losses in African food systems
resulting from increases in pests and diseases, unpredictable rainfall pattern, increased humidity, and
changes in precipitation patterns — all of which will affect the harvest and post-harvest food handling. The
effects of climate change are expected to be particularly severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and
respond to a changing climate is weak. Climate finance support from the global community is therefore
essential to shift development pathways in the post-harvest food systems in the seven targeted African
countries.
157. Low productivity, aggravated by harvest and postharvest loss, has been the major driver of climate
vulnerability as farming is expanding to fragile ecosystems, wetlands and mountains, causing
deforestation, land degradation and drying of water resources, exposing farmers to extreme events.
158. In response to these climate change threats on food security, the RE-GAIN programme aims to enhance
the climate resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholders by promoting the widespread adoption of FL-
RS in seven African countries. AGRA, as the lead agency, is requesting GCF grant funds to support the
roll out of a suite of physical and management interventions alongside capacity building and enhanced
financial and market access. The rationale for GCF concessional support – beyond the basic climate
change attribution described above – is presented below.
Barriers to public or private finance alternatives
159. Experiences to date show that countries rarely invest in climate change adaptation, less so in reducing
food loss. This is largely due to the constrained fiscal space in the public sector, which limits opportunity
to invest in catalytic programme such as RE-GAIN using public funds. Constraints on public finance are
further compounded by the already high levels of external debt, which prevents governments access to
loan finance without putting considerable strain on the national economy. Given these constraints and the
resulting high levels of external dependency, the target countries are reliant on external funding and
support for climate change adaptation initiatives. However, international aid and climate finance flows may
be insufficient or unpredictable, making it difficult for these countries to plan and implement long-term
adaptation and mitigation strategies.
160. Looking to the private sector as an alternative, it is highly challenging to secure finance from conventional
sources in the market for such resilience-building interventions unless market demand is created and
appropriate market linkages are put in place. The return on investment for such programmes is often seen
as unattractive for private sector investors, and the element of innovation that characterizes the necessary
climate adaptation initiatives is viewed by the market as untested, with the absence of a track record of
financial soundness translating to a high estimation of risk among potential investors. This challenge is
compounded by the fact that resource-poor smallholder farmers may not be willing to invest in the FL-RS
without a clear demonstration of the effectiveness of the solutions – particularly given that some of the
technologies are unaffordable and farmers need a quasi-guarantee about the viability of these solutions
before investing.
161. Based on the stakeholder mapping AGRA conducted during the country strategy development, existing
interventions and support focus mainly on last mile delivery, with few actors willing or able to invest in
unlocking the systemic challenges that would facilitate agriculture and food systems transformation. AGRA
sees this as a gap and a potential opportunity for creating significant impact if interventions incentivize the
markets inclusively, the right level of awareness and adoption is created and access to finance is de-
risked.
162. Building the necessary capacity to develop and implement effective adaptation plans and investments
requires substantial investments in education, training, and institutional strengthening, which, although
catalytic, are generally considered public goods with limited direct return. International aid is, therefore,
necessary to initiate a paradigm shift in the sector, supporting Africa-led solutions to tackle national
priorities and investment plans.
Consideration of alternative funding models.

=== PAGE 50 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 48
B
163. In the process of designing the RE-GAIN programme, numerous other alternative finance models were
considered. While each option has some merit, each faces considerable barriers that prevent these models
from working on their own. These alternative models are summarised below and have been factored into
the design of innovative finance models that the proposed programme will initiate through Component 2.
▪ Farmers pay cash for the hermetic technologies and for using the FL-RS services if they have savings
and or alternative sources of income. This is often not the case in most contexts and countries.
▪ Farmers can get the FL-RS on credit from the supplier of the technology (agro-dealers) and/or service
providers if these MSMEs have the liquidity to give credit to their customers. This is often not the case
in most countries and contexts. A mix of finance from vendors and suppliers, own contribution from
farmers and additional funding from GCF can solve for the access to finance.
▪ Service providers offering FL-RS could get equipment financing through wealthy individuals, family
and friends or through financial institutions or equipment vendors. However, there is inherent risk,
both for the lender and borrowers, with several barriers currently preventing access to finance.
Innovative finance models are needed to overcome this barrier.
▪ While credit options are possible, borrowers would need to offset the risk for lenders through co-
contributions as well as the provision of some form of guarantee or collateral.
▪ This option is further hindered by the high interest rates in some countries (particularly those with high
inflation) and transaction costs for accessing financing are high in rural contexts (documentation,
transport).
▪ There are risks for lenders (first time borrowers and their lack of collateral and transaction history) as
the financing of FL-RS in food crops have been minimal till date.
▪ The costs for lenders (loan origination, assessment and monitoring) are higher than for other sectors
and loan products
▪ There is also risk to potential borrowers (market risk, profitability risk), as the business case for many
of the FL-RS have not yet been proven in rural contexts for staple crops produced by small scale
producers.
▪ Extension services (for example the provision of information on weather and harvesting practices)
could be paid for with Government budget. However, most Governments are constrained in their
budgets for extension services (they are over-indebted and have many competing priorities), hence
the need for donor funds to complement the government budget.
▪ The proposed awareness activities could be financed by the suppliers of the FL-RS; however their
reach is limited, and they might not be able to pay 100% of the investment costs for creating
awareness in rural areas as the profit margins do not make up for that investment. Private sector does
invest in the activities that give the highest return on investment and that might not be on products
sold in rural areas to low-income small-scale farmers.
Rationale for concessional funding
164. AGRA is requesting concessional finance in the form of grants for the RE-GAIN programme. The need for
full concessionality is based on the nature of the investments, which require targeted capacity development
support combined with strategic conditional procurements to catalyse a paradigm shift in the post-harvest
value chain using innovative FL-RS in the seven targe countries. The specific details on the nature of
intervention, their suitability for concessional finance, and how the programme will transfer the benefits of
concessionality directly to farmers are described in more detail below.
▪ De-risk first time users to invest in new technology that remains unproven in their context. The “pay
for two, get one free” type of investment will make sure the concessional funding end up in farmers
pockets.
▪ Reduce the cost of distributing FL-RS to rural areas where demand is not yet confirmed (the
concessional funding will de-risk agro-dealer and distributors from remaining with unsold stock).
▪ Reduce the risk for FL-RS service providers to invest in equipment for which the business case is not
yet proven. The manufacturers will be paid, and the service providers will not be left with unpaid debts
in case the equipment is not able to be operated at profitable scale.
▪ De-risk investments in awareness creation by sellers and distributors of FL-RS (extension,
demonstrations, etc.) because awareness creation does not necessarily translate in adoption of FL-
RS and profit for the sellers of FL-RS.

=== PAGE 51 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49
B
▪ Incentivize farmers to adopt FL-RS by reducing the cost of the FL-RS and enhance the availability of
FL-RS at the right time.
165. The level of concessionality transferred to the end user through the ‘smart-subsidies’ will depend on a
number of factors which differ per country and per FL-RS. The less developed the local context is on any
of the following factors, the greater the subsidy relative to the co-contribution form the recipient. These
factors include:
▪ Is the FL-RS technology proven to be profitable in financial terms for all actors: farmers, agro-
dealers, service providers?
▪ Is the distribution network of FL-RS already in place and reaching all areas where the FL-RS is
needed?
▪ Are farmers already aware of the FL-RS and ready to adopt? Is there a ready market?
▪ Are manufacturers capable of forecasting demand and prefinance stock of FL-RS where the
demand is?
▪ Does the Government have the knowledge and tools to provide the FL-RS information in time and
with sufficient accuracy to help farmers harvest in the right time using the right practices?
B.6. Exit strategy
166. Long-term sustainability is at the core of the programme design, with GCF investments seen as catalysers
of change within local systems. A key aspect of this GCF intervention is having an exit strategy through
the creation of an enabling environment to shift away from the smart-subsidies, catalysing further
investments in FL-RS under Component 2. This approach to sustainability is centred on market
development and stimulating the local supply chains to bring FL-RS to smallholder farmers at affordable
rates — working through the private sector to foster innovation and commercial opportunities. To establish
sustainable supply chains, the programme will forge market-led relationships with value chain actors such
as off-takers, processors, aggregators, business development service providers and financial institutions,
deepening and strengthening the networks that connect farmers to market. When combined with the
demand-oriented actions of awareness, demonstration and market development under Outcome 1, the
programme will result in a balance of supply, demand, and finance opportunity that will enable these
relationships and commercial opportunities to persist within the target communities and expand to spill
over areas beyond initial GCF investment period. Strengthening a market-led system is particularly
important for the economic value that is being created through the adaptation components of RE-GAIN;
these will ensure that the farmers and the supporting value chain actors continue to see value in the
deployment of FL-RS even after the GCF project comes to an end. Further, the strengthening of an
enabling public policy and institutions will ensure continued promotion of FL-RS through extension systems
beyond the life of the project. Support for these systems among communities will be further strengthened
by the long-term adaptation support provided through the environmental and social co-benefits. Such
benefits, when combined with the strengthened awareness of climate risk, will further incentivise farmers
to continue with FL-RS beyond the project lifespan.
167. At the community level, the programme is expected to actively strengthen and grow agricultural MSMEs
(including farmer cooperatives, women’s and youth groups) through access to finance and business
development services to enhance development of FL-RS technologies, helping them attain a higher level
of self-sufficiency that is expected to increase the sustainability of the full agriculture value chain, especially
at post-harvest. It is also expected that by demonstrating a positive track record of successful direct
investments in rural MSMEs and farmer organization, as well as by funding financial intermediaries for the
creation or expansion of FL-RS investment portfolios, the GCF fund will incentivize the wider financial
sector to focus on the agriculture sector through the design and development of appropriate products,
thereby contributing to its increased sustainability of the programme. This will further enhance
opportunities for continued investment in FL-RS beyond the initial subsidised finance models.
168. To ensure balance in interventions and sustainability of the programme, an end-to-end process approach
will be used to for inclusion of stakeholders, from food system input processes, productivity, output
processes, post-harvest handling and marketing. This will be context-specific, and the depth of
investments will depend on existing AGRA or partners’ investments. The programme will take a market-
led approach to ensure sector sustainability, innovation and scale. Through the various capacity
development activities, the programme will invest in strengthening the capacity of VBA extension workers

=== PAGE 52 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 50
B
and farmers on the topic of climate-resilient agriculture and FL-RS. The programme will build and
strengthen a network of village-based advisors and agro-dealers who will build the needed extension
capacity within the farmer communities. They will be able to maintain and support other similar extension
interventions inside and outside the targeted areas within focus countries, beyond the programme lifespan.
169. The proposed programme is designed through the participation of a wide array of public and private
institutions, and civil society (including national and international and development actors) to promote
ownership of the programme during implementation, the activities will be executed in close collaboration
with relevant government departments in the focus countries, engaging with key stakeholders to enhance
their capacity and create the enabling environment for continued investment. The programmatic
monitoring, evaluation and learning activities will ensure that the lessons learned, documents produced,
and the activities implemented during the programs are synthesized, stored and disseminated to the
benefit of other programs and enable adaptive management of FL-RS initiatives.

C.3. Capacity building and technology development/transfer
C.3.1 Does GEF funding finance capacity building Yes ☒ No ☐
activities
C.3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology Yes ☐ No ☒
development
171. Capacity development is a key aspect of the programme design, cutting across all three project
components. Under Component 1, capacity development will focus on training smallholder farmers
on the use of improved climate-resilient FL-RS (USD 6M) and agricultural traders and processors on
complementary services for FL-RS (USD 1.4M). The capacity of extension workers and agro-dealers
will also be enhanced to further scale the provision of training support to farmers, traders and
processors, with specific training of trainers programme (USD 2M).
172. Under Component 2, similar capacity development support will be provided to train new FL-RS service
providers such as MSMEs, cooperatives, incl. women- and youth-led initiatives (USD 3.2M), including
business development support and market intelligence (USD 1.8M), as well as capacity and market
development for all supply-side market actors (USD 1.8M).
Under Component 3, the programme will provide institutional capacity development support to key
local entities to support the scaling of successful FL-RS models (USD 2.6M).
173. In terms of technology, the project will not develop new technologies but will invest in technology
transfers for existing FL-RS. This will be approached primarily through the development of innovative
finance mechanisms under Component 2, using conditional procurements to leverage private finance
through partnerships with financial service providers, agro-dealers, MSMEs, youth groups, and
smallholder farmers (USD 32.7M).

D
D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA
D.1. Impact potential
Adaptation Impact
174. The proposed programme will yield significant adaptation benefits for vulnerable smallholder farmers, their
families, and communities across the seven target countries. At the core of these benefits is the expected
reduction in climate change-induced food losses that are increasingly being experienced across Africa.
Such food loss reductions will have multiple positive impacts on the lives and livelihoods of these highly
vulnerable communities, mainly by enhancing the absorption capacity of smallholder farmers to seasonal
climate and other shocks, building their resilience in the face of climate change. At the most fundamental
level, reduced food loss will directly contribute to food security in the target communities — thereby
contributing to GCF Adaptation Result Area (ARA) 2: Health and well-being, and food and water security.
While climate change is known to impact the full spectrum of food systems — from production through to
consumption — the proposed programme’s focus on post-harvest food loss addresses a critical, but
somewhat under-served, component of food security. Reduced food loss through improved management
at harvest and postharvest phases will not only improve food security, but also increase farmers income
and farm-level profitability. Additionally, reduction of food loss also leads to preserving the integrity of
natural ecosystems that further has a positive impact on the resilience of farmers. The impacts of this
programme are, therefore, considered as complementary to the broader adaptation strategies for
agricultural resilience within each target country, filling a key gap in the broader landscape of food security.
However, despite the complementarity of this approach with others focused more on primary production,
the expected impacts on food security would remain even when viewed in isolation.
175. The nature of the approach – targeting smallholder farmers, with particular focus on women, youth groups,
and MSMES — means that the resilience benefits will apply to the most vulnerable people and
communities – thereby contributing to GCF ARA 1. In addition to the increased food security, these benefits
will include the increased resilience of rural livelihoods, with significant investments being made in
developing local markets and building up farmer cooperatives, youth and women’s groups, and MSMEs.
The resulting investments extend beyond just the initial GCF contribution, with catalytic grants creating
sustainable systems that will leverage partnerships with financial institutions and private sector actors to
mobilise significant investment beyond the proposed project.
176. Through these results, the project will directly benefit a total of 2.62 million people across the seven
countries, distributed across the even target countries as shown in Table 7. Further details on the
methodology for determining beneficiary numbers are presented in Supplementary Annex 1:Adaptation
Impact Estimation.
