D.2. Paradigm shift potential
177. The proposed programme has been designed to elicit a paradigm shift in the harvest and post-harvest
development pathways for the seven target countries and continentally in Africa. At the core of this shift is
the use of GCF grants as a catalyst for change, stimulating markets and mobilising investment at scale.
This approach will lean on AGRA’s convening power in Africa, drawing together key actors across the

D
post-harvest value chain — including farmers, agro-dealers, suppliers, off-takers and decision-makers —
to establish sustainable programmes that enable farmers to adopt improved FL-RS in a manner that is
financially viable and climate resilient. A clear focus on achieving scale and sustainability is embedded in
the programme design, both through the program’s continental reach and its ambition to work on the
ground within seven countries through employing tested and functional last mile delivery systems. This
commitment to achieving scale and sustainability is underpinned by the programme’s emphasis on
achieving transformational systems change, as described below.
Potential for scaling up and replication
178. The nature of the programme approach is such that it will stimulate local supply of affordable FL-RS options
that will enable the scaling of interventions beyond the scope of the GCF investment. Specifically, the
innovative finance mechanisms under Output 2.2, along with the MSME business development and market
support under Output 2.1, will catalyse the establishment of and/or strengthen local supply chains that will
bring FL-RS to smallholder farmers at affordable rates. A key feature of this approach is the notion of
working through the private sector to foster innovation and commercial opportunity. AGRA’s delivery model
will demonstrate and scale innovations that enable smallholder farmers to participate in both input and
output markets, and in doing so to forge commercial relationships with off-takers, processors, exporters,
commercial aggregators, business development service providers and financial institutions. This
ecosystem focus builds sustainability and strengthens resilience by deepening and strengthening the
networks that connect farmers to market. The FL-RS solutions will be disseminated at scale by integrating
best practices into major national and regional flagship programs and anchor firms. When combined with
the demand-oriented actions of awareness, demonstration and market development under Outcome 1,
the programme will result in a balance of supply, demand, and finance opportunity that will enable the
scaling of interventions in the target communities beyond initial GCF investment.
179. In addition to the scaling potential noted above, the programme is structured such that the approaches to
stimulating the FL-RS market can be replicated; not only within other communities in the target countries,
but also regionally across Africa. This replication potential is initially rooted in the demonstration effect the
project will have, which when combined with the convening power of AGRA across Africa will attract
additional market actors to engage in similar investment schemes. To further facilitate replication,
Component 3 of the project will establish an enabling environment for sustainable FL-RS markets (see
below). While these enabling activities will focus on replication at the national level, AGRA will facilitate
collaboration with continental and regional bodies and national governments, including African regional
climate institutions and Regional Economic Communities, to initiate the replication of the programme’s
innovative models for scaling FL-RS across the region.
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning
180. The innovations, especially on innovative financing and business models, within the programme have the
potential to generate new knowledge on ways of engaging MSMEs and smallholder farmers in reducing
their food losses and thus increasing their productivity and their resilience to climate shocks. Activities
under Component 3 will capture these lessons and develop knowledge products to share best practices
at the regional level. This knowledge sharing component will be fundamental to the replication potential
described above.
Contribution to the regulatory framework and creation of an enabling environment
181. Through the partnerships that will be established under the innovative finance and business models, the
programme will address key barriers that limit the scaling up of FL-RS. This will be complemented by the
policy focus with national governments in partner countries to help them realize their commitments to
tackling climate change and improving food security in their countries. This will include supporting national
governments to enact forward-thinking policies and regulations that seek to integrate climate-resilient FL-
RS into the agriculture sector, as well as advocating for the establishment of tax incentives, and the uptake
of certifications and standards for FL-RS.

D.3. Sustainable development
182. In addition to the core impacts described in D.1 above, the proposed programme will elicit multiple co-
benefits that will contribute to the sustainable development goals (SDGs) of the target countries. Key
contributions to the SDGs are listed in Table 8.

Environmental co-benefits
183. Programme interventions to reduce post-harvest food loss will have several environmental co-benefits.
▪ Reduced land degradation and improved water use efficiency: Reducing post-harvest food loss will
increase the net production of agricultural land, thereby reducing area of land and amount of water
needed to produce sufficient food to meet demand. FL-RS will, therefore, improve the efficiency of
land and water use in the agriculture sector, thereby reducing the impact of agriculture on land and
water resources. This includes curtailing some of the drivers of agricultural expansion associated with
the need to compensate for food loss. The resultant reduction in the rate of land use change will help
preserve natural ecosystems and the important services they provide.
▪ Decreased in net use of fertilisers and pesticides: The improved net-productivity of agriculture will
reduce the environmental impact of fertilisers and pesticides, as well as the related energy demands,
particularly as related to regrowing of lost food.
▪ Reduction in air pollutants from waste food disposal: Reduced food waste will limit the potential
environmental impacts of improper disposal of spoilt food – for example the release of air pollutants
when waste is burned, or the production of methane during decomposition (see mitigation benefits in
Annex 22).
▪ Increased regreening and improved land use: Reduced expansion of farming will release more land
to reafforestation, land rehabilitation and other services.
▪ Reduced loss of biodiversity: Land expansion into forests and fragile areas is the major cause of
biodiversity loss on the continent, which is threatening the livelihoods of current and future
generations.

Socioeconomic and health co-benefits
184. In targeting the livelihoods of highly vulnerable smallholder farming communities, the proposed programme
will yield multiple socioeconomic co-benefits. Some of these co-benefits arise as a direct result of the core
impact of improved food security, while others arise as a result of the actions to develop the FL-RS market
in target communities.
▪ Increased food quality and safety: Improved post-harvest processes that reduce food waste will also
improve the quality of food reaching consumers, which will enhance the nutritional value and reduce
the risk of food-related illnesses resulting from contamination by pests, molds, aflatoxins and other
pathogens. This will not only benefit individuals, but a more stable and safe food supply will enhance
public health systems through reduced incidence of nutrition-related illness.
▪ Job creation: By working with local suppliers and agro-dealers, while also supporting the business
development of MSMEs as service providers in the FL-RS value chain, the proposed programme will
generate employment opportunities that will extend beyond the initial investment. This will particularly
benefit vulnerable groups such as women and youth that will be actively targeted for support in
business development initiatives.
▪ Market stability: Reducing food loss can help stabilize food prices by ensuring a more consistent
supply. This can protect both producers and consumers from price volatility.
Gender-sensitive development benefit
185. The proposed programme has been designed with gender sensitivity at its core; with each component
carrying key gender-responsive actions to not only ensure equal participation of women, but also actively
empower women within the FL-RS value chain. This includes business development opportunities directly
targeted at women’s groups and women-led enterprises, recognising them as key actors in the market and
supporting these groups to establish robust, sustainable services in the FL-RS value chain. The delivery
mechanism of the project which primarily focuses on targeting farmer groups provide a platform that are
likely to promote gender and youth integration and therefore leads to development outcomes that are
gender sensitive.
D.4. Needs of recipient
186. RE-GAIN targets seven African countries that are all recognised as being highly vulnerable to climate
change, which impacts all sectors of society. Of particular concern is the highly vulnerable agriculture

sector, which employs between 40 and 80% of total population and contributes between 20 and 40% of
GDP across the target countries39. Given the high rates of employment and economic contribution of the
sector, climate change impacts on agriculture extend beyond the farmers themselves to the national
economies as a whole. In addition to their sensitivity to climate impacts, all seven of these target countries
are recognized as having low adaptive capacity40 and low readiness41 to adapt; highlighting their need for
support.
Table 10. ND-GAIN index for 2021
Country Adaptive Capacity Rank (out of 192) Readiness Rank (out of 192)
Burkina Faso 154 (low adaptive capacity) 158 (low readiness)

Uganda 147 (low adaptive capacity) 163 (low readiness)
Zambia 103 (low adaptive capacity) 141 (low readiness)
187. As noted in Section B.1, climate change impacts the full spectrum of food production systems in Africa,
from preparation of the land through the production and harvest, to the processing, storage and marketing
of the produce. While the impacts across the whole system are well documented, the lion’s share of
research and resources for resilience interventions in the agricultural sector have been focused thus far
on production, leaving a considerable gap in investments in the post-harvest stages of the process. In the
absence of innovative initiatives to develop post-harvest food loss reduction systems, the target
populations are left vulnerable to the impacts of post-harvest losses on their food security, which are being
exacerbated by climate change. Given the high levels of poverty, weak markets, and low adaptive capacity
experienced in smallholder farming communities, farmers are left unable to adopt climate-resilient FL-RS
without initial external support. Moreover, the national governments of each country, while prioritising
climate-resilient agriculture development, lack the financial, institutional and technical capacity to establish
sustainable markets for FL-RS, with the majority of limited resources being directed towards production.
188. The RE-GAIN programme targets this vulnerable stage of the value chain that, despite the urgent need,
has remained relatively underdeveloped at the global scale – particularly when considering smallholder
farmers. By targeting local suppliers and agro-dealers, while also supporting the business development of
MSMEs as service providers in the FL-RS value chain, RE-GAIN will build resilience directly within the
target populations, integrating the solutions into local systems that meet the individual needs of each
community.
189. More details on the needs of each country and the tailoring of interventions to each local context are
provided in the country-specific Feasibility Studies appended to Annex 2.
D.5. Country ownership
190. The design of RE-GAIN directly emerged from a national-driven process that reflects country ownership
by seven target countries — with significant effort applied throughout development to ensure country
ownership of this programme. AGRA co-developed and submitted project idea notes and summary
concept notes to NDAs and the various ministries in the respective country for demand creation,
commenting on relevance and content and have jointly developed the potential, context-specific solutions
together with the respective ministries and key stakeholders of the respective countries. This including
having established demand by conducting initial consultation with key government institutions, followed by
multiple local and national stakeholder workshops. Hence, the focus on post-harvest loss reduction is
aligned with key agriculture and adaption strategies, as outlined in various policy documents and

development strategies in each of the seven focus countries (Table 11). Further, the programme is fully
aligned with national policies, programmes in the various thematic areas of climate change, food and

project will further leverage on existing national and partner programmes to ensure complementarity of
programmes. These factors will enhance national and local ownership of the programme.
Table 11. Key alignment with national policies and priorities for climate change and agriculture in each of the target countries.
CC Strategies and Policies
Burkina Faso
Burkina Faso’s National Sustainable Development Policy (PNDD) includes a key focus on reducing post-harvest
losses through improved storage, processing, and distribution techniques to enhance food security and promote
sustainable agricultural practices. This is complemented by the National Economic and Social Development Plan
II 2021-2025 which highlights improving the handling, storage, and processing of agricultural products to reduce
post-harvest losses as part of the strategy to restore security and peace, strengthen national resilience, and
transform the economy for inclusive and sustainable growth.
The National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNA) also addresses the need for resilience in agricultural
systems amid climate variability and change, underscoring adaptive agricultural practices and sustainable
methods to secure food and nutritional needs. The National Agricultural Investment Program further reinforces
these efforts, providing strategic guidance to the agricultural sector to reduce post-harvest losses and improve
food security by addressing aflatoxin-triggered losses, among other issues. Similarly, the National Strategy for
the Development of Agricultural Entrepreneurship by 2025 includes focus on reducing post-harvest losses
through improved storage facilities, processing techniques, and investments in modern infrastructure and
training for farmers, while the Agricultural Value Chain Development Strategy 2019-2023 focuses on increasing
productivity and reducing harvest and post-harvest losses through disseminating conservation technologies,
building capacity on good practices, and constructing storage facilities. This approach is also promoted in the
Agro-sylvo-pastoral Production Sectoral Policy 2018-2027.
Finally, Burkina Faso’s revised its Nationally Determined Contribution for the period 2021–2025 sets ambitious
targets for reducing greenhouse gas emissions and adapting to climate change —including through the
promotion of regenerative agriculture and food systems.
Ethiopia
Ethiopia’s Agricultural Transformation Agenda focuses on improving productivity and reducing post-harvest
losses through infrastructure development and training. The Postharvest Management Strategy in Grains (2018)
and the Working Strategy for Strengthening Ethiopia’s Teff Value Chain (2015) also both focus on reducing post-
harvest losses and enhancing value chains.
In addition to these policies aimed specifically at post-harvest, numerous national policies and strategies identify
agriculture as a key sector for sustainable and climate resilient development. This includes the Ten-Year
Development Plan (2021-2030), the Homegrown Economic Reform Agenda, and Climate Resilient Green
Economy Strategy which focus on agriculture and private sector involvement as a driver to reach middle-income
status by 2025. The programme also aligns with investment roadmaps for prioritizing agricultural investments
laid out in the National Agriculture Investment Plan (2022) and the Agricultural Sector Policy and Investment
Framework (2010-2020). Ethiopia’s updated NDC highlights the mitigation potential in land use and forestry,
targeting significant GHG reductions by 2030. The National Adaptation Plan (2017) focuses on agriculture and
industry, aiming to reduce food losses and increase resilience. Additionally, the Agricultural Extension Strategy
(2017-2030) integrates best practices for effective extension services, and the Nutrition Sensitive Agriculture
Strategy (2017-2021) aims to mainstream nutrition in agricultural programs.
Kenya
Kenya’s Vision 2030 aims to transform the country into a newly industrializing middle-income nation by 2030,
focusing on modernizing agriculture and achieving a 10% annual economic growth rate. The National Climate
Change Action Plan (2018-2022) strengthens the path towards sustainable, climate-resilient development,
addressing agricultural emissions and enhancing productivity and resilience. Kenya’s Agricultural Sector
Development Strategy (2010-2020) and the National Agriculture Investment Plan (2019-2024) promote market-
oriented production and post-harvest loss reduction. Kenya’s Nationally Determined Contribution (2020-2030)
seeks to abate GHG emissions by 30% by 2030, with the agriculture sector playing a key role. The National
Adaptation Plan (2015-2030) enhances climate resilience towards Vision 2030, promoting climate-smart
agriculture and efficient food systems. The Agricultural Sector Transformation and Growth Strategy (2019-2029)
emphasizes increasing incomes, output, and food resilience, with specific targets for SME development and
market access. The Climate Risk Management Framework (2016) harmonizes climate change and disaster risk
policies. The Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Implementation Framework (2018-2027) provides guidance for
innovative and transformative initiatives, focusing on productivity, resilience, and emissions reduction.

D
Malawi
The programme approach aligns with the priorities of the two core development agendas for Malawi, namely
Malawi 2063 and the Malawi Growth Development Strategy, which both include focus on long-term agricultural
development and economic transformation. The need for addressing climate change impacts and post-harvest
food losses are recognised in the National Agriculture Policy (NAP 2016-2020), which propose measures that
include the use of CIEWS and community-based storage. This was further highlighted in the Vision 2020 - The
National Long Term Development Perspective, which highlighted the need for clear policies and technologies to
reduce post-harvest losses. Malawi’s updated NDC (2021) also identifies agriculture as the largest contributor to
GHG emissions, emphasizing mitigation and adaptation measures to enhance food security and resilience.
Tanzania
Tanzania’s comprehensive policy framework addresses agricultural productivity, climate resilience, and post-
harvest loss management. The Tanzania Development Vision 2025 and the Long-Term Perspective Plan (2011-
2021) set long-term development goals, emphasizing food security and sustainable development. The National
Strategy for Growth and Reduction of Poverty II and the Third National Five-Year Development Plan focus on
post-harvest loss management and agricultural productivity. The Agricultural Sector Development Programme
Phase II (2017/18-2027/28) and the Agricultural Sector Development Strategy (2015/2016-2024/2025) aim to
modernize agriculture, improve infrastructure, and enhance market access.
The National Climate Change Strategy (2012-2018) and the updated Nationally Determined Contribution (2021)
target GHG emission reductions and climate resilience in agriculture. The National Postharvest Management
Strategy (2019-2029) and its Implementation Plan (2019-2024) focus on reducing crop losses through improved
storage, technology adoption, and market systems. Tanzania’s policies also include the Agriculture Climate
Resilience Plan (2014-2019) and the National Agriculture Policy (2013) to promote sustainable agricultural
practices and resilience. The ASDP II Communication Strategy (2020-2028) identifies key information needs for
smallholders and strategic interventions to reduce post-harvest losses. The National Environment Policy (NEP
2021) and the National Climate Change Response Strategy (2021-2026) outline environmental and climate
change goals.
Uganda
Uganda’s policy landscape includes long- and mid-term strategies focused on agricultural development, climate
resilience, and post-harvest loss reduction. Uganda Vision 2040 aims to transform Uganda into a competitive
upper-middle-income country by 2040, emphasizing agricultural development and reducing post-harvest losses
through expanded market infrastructure. The Green Growth Development Strategy (2017/18-2030/31) ensures
sustainable development in agriculture, natural capital management, and climate resilience, focusing on income
enhancement, job creation, climate change adaptation, and resource use efficiency. The Third National
Development Plan (2020-2025) prioritizes reducing post-harvest losses to enhance food security and increase
agricultural productivity, with a focus on agro-industrialization. The National Agriculture Policy (2013) aims to
promote food security and household incomes through sustainable agricultural productivity and value addition. It
emphasizes reducing post-harvest losses through improved storage, processing, and value addition. The
National Cooperative Policy (2011) seeks to improve governance, production, value addition, and marketing
capacities of cooperatives, addressing challenges like poor storage facilities and high post-harvest losses. The
National Agricultural Extension Strategy (NAES 2016/17-2020/21) aims to establish a sustainable farmer-centred
agricultural extension system, enhancing capacity in production, agribusiness, value addition, and post-harvest
management. The National Grain Trade Policy (2015) targets improved grain handling and storage to reduce
post-harvest losses, which range between 26% to 37%. It focuses on enhancing pre- and post-harvest practices,
standardizing warehouses, and improving market access.
The National Climate Change Policy (2015) promotes climate-resilient and low-carbon development, with
adaptation strategies to enhance agricultural systems' resilience and improve food storage and management.
Uganda’s updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 2022) prioritizes adaptation in agriculture, aiming to
reduce post-harvest losses from 37% to 12% by 2025 and to 3% by 2030. The National Organic Agriculture
Policy (2020) emphasizes enhancing post-harvest handling and value addition to improve market opportunities
and job creation. It promotes access to appropriate agricultural technologies for post-harvest management.
Zambia
Zambia has a comprehensive policy framework aimed at enhancing agricultural productivity, reducing post-
harvest losses, and promoting climate resilience. The 8th National Development Plan (2022-2026) focuses on
improving living standards, reducing poverty and inequality, and promoting economic transformation through
agricultural development. It emphasizes increasing production, productivity, and value addition, along with
enhancing storage and logistics for small-scale farmers. The Second National Agricultural Policy (2016) supports
sustainable agricultural diversification, commercialization, and private sector participation, targeting high post-
harvest losses by constructing and rehabilitating storage facilities and providing fumigation services. The revised
National Agriculture Policy (2012-2030) guides sustainable agricultural growth, focusing on reducing post-
harvest losses through better storage and handling practices. The National Food and Nutrition Policy includes

 | Malawi | 
The programme approach aligns with the priorities of the two core development agendas for Malawi, namely
Malawi 2063 and the Malawi Growth Development Strategy, which both include focus on long-term agricultural
development and economic transformation. The need for addressing climate change impacts and post-harvest
food losses are recognised in the National Agriculture Policy (NAP 2016-2020), which propose measures that
include the use of CIEWS and community-based storage. This was further highlighted in the Vision 2020 - The
National Long Term Development Perspective, which highlighted the need for clear policies and technologies to
reduce post-harvest losses. Malawi’s updated NDC (2021) also identifies agriculture as the largest contributor to
GHG emissions, emphasizing mitigation and adaptation measures to enhance food security and resilience. |  | 
 | Tanzania | 
Tanzania’s comprehensive policy framework addresses agricultural productivity, climate resilience, and post-
harvest loss management. The Tanzania Development Vision 2025 and the Long-Term Perspective Plan (2011-
2021) set long-term development goals, emphasizing food security and sustainable development. The National
Strategy for Growth and Reduction of Poverty II and the Third National Five-Year Development Plan focus on
post-harvest loss management and agricultural productivity. The Agricultural Sector Development Programme
Phase II (2017/18-2027/28) and the Agricultural Sector Development Strategy (2015/2016-2024/2025) aim to
modernize agriculture, improve infrastructure, and enhance market access.
The National Climate Change Strategy (2012-2018) and the updated Nationally Determined Contribution (2021)
target GHG emission reductions and climate resilience in agriculture. The National Postharvest Management
Strategy (2019-2029) and its Implementation Plan (2019-2024) focus on reducing crop losses through improved
storage, technology adoption, and market systems. Tanzania’s policies also include the Agriculture Climate
Resilience Plan (2014-2019) and the National Agriculture Policy (2013) to promote sustainable agricultural
practices and resilience. The ASDP II Communication Strategy (2020-2028) identifies key information needs for
smallholders and strategic interventions to reduce post-harvest losses. The National Environment Policy (NEP
2021) and the National Climate Change Response Strategy (2021-2026) outline environmental and climate
change goals. |  |

provisions for reducing post-harvest losses to ensure food security. Zambia's Climate-Smart Agriculture
Investment Plan promotes practices that increase productivity, enhance resilience, and reduce GHG emissions,
emphasizing strategies for reducing post-harvest losses and the need for market infrastructure and training on
improved post-harvest management.
The National Policy on Climate Change (2016) provides a framework for coordinating climate change programs
to ensure climate-resilient and low-carbon development towards Zambia's Vision 2030. It includes measures to
strengthen early warning systems, control climate-related pests and diseases, and promote climate-smart
agricultural technologies. Zambia’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 2015-2030) pledges to reduce
GHG emissions by 25% (20,000 Gg CO2eq) by 2030 under a Business As Usual (BAU) scenario, with a
potential reduction of 47% (38,000 Gg CO2eq) with substantial international support. The NDC emphasizes
sustainable agriculture, forest conservation, and waste management, including food loss reduction as key areas
for mitigation. Zambia’s revised NDC (2021) broadens the scope of mitigation and adaptation actions, focusing
on sustainable agriculture, productive infrastructure, capacity building, and research.
191. The design of the proposal has been built on a strong foundation of consultations with communities as
well as local and national governmental and non-governmental entities. These consultations, together with
substantial desktop research and the extensive local experience of AGRA in the target countries, were
used to establish a strong baseline for the project, identifying key technical, institutional and market gaps
that need to be filled to build resilience of the target smallholder farmers. This process will continue through
the project implementation, ensuring that the solutions are tailored to the individual needs of each target
community. In particular, the innovative finance and catalytic grant programmes under Component 2 will
be specifically tailored to the context and needs of local stakeholders.
192. While the project will be implemented by AGRA, the co-development process with the target countries,
underpinned by the consultative process noted above, ensures that the project retains a strong sense of
country ownership. In addition to the consultation held specifically for the development of this proposal,
the foundation of the approach arose from extensive engagements AGRA has conducted in each of the
target countries during the development of its five-year country strategies (2023-2027). These
engagements resulted in a gap analysis across the agriculture and food system sector of each country,
and the region more generally, identifying areas for collaboration and complementarity to enhance a food
system transformation. AGRA prioritizes collaboration with government – through its policy and state
capacity strategic support that involves alignment with national climate change strategies, policies, plans,
and instruments as mentioned earlier in this concept note. Interventions in this program support national
priorities, visions and plans, fostering a natural ownership by the government. For more details on country
stakeholder analysis, please refer to Annex 7.
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Financial Structure – adequacy, reasonableness and return on investment
193. Affordability, access to finance, market access, financial literacy, and consumer awareness are key
challenges for smallholder farmers seeking to adopt practices that reduce food loss in the agricultural value
chain. Key to viably addressing these challenges is RE-GAIN's programmatic approach that combines
demand-side, supply-side, and enabling environment components.
194. The combination of both financial and non-financial approaches effectively ensures market failures
resulting from both the supplier and consumers side of the agricultural value chain are addressed.
195. The two key financial models—1) catalytic grants for smallholder farmers and 2) catalytic grants for
agricultural MSMEs—use concessional funding to incentivize private sector manufacturers and financial
institutions (FIs) to
▪ Provide physical solutions that are within the affordability envelope of farmers while remaining
commercially viable to private sector suppliers.
▪ Provide tailored financial products that are affordable to farmers, while mitigating credit risk to financial
institutions.

=== PAGE 64 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62
D
196. By focusing on a 5-year period, the program aims to demonstrate long-term market viability to suppliers
(both physical and financial), build the necessary credit history required to access financial products, and
encourage adoption of physical solutions by farmers.
197. Non-financial interventions drive adoption of these solutions from farmers by focusing on building capacity
and awareness through training, educational materials, and village-based agent (VBA) demonstrations,
addressing information gaps and promoting financial literacy. These interventions are critical to creating
linkages between private sector manufacturers, MSMEs and Small hold farmers.
198. This combined approach of addressing both supply and demand side constraints is expected to mobilize
US$ 144.95 million worth of physical solutions and reach 553,900 smallholder farmers households over
the next 5 years. Given the programme cost of US$ 105 million this represents a catalytic effect or return
of 1.38 times the original investment from the GCF. The financial return on investment exceeds 1 time the
original investment across each of the seven countries. This return excludes the long-term impact of the
programme’s ability to demonstrate the market viability of these physical solutions to both users (farmers)
and suppliers (manufactures and FIs), which will catalyse new private sector markets while addressing
food-loss and associated climate related challenges.
Economic Efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project/programme and return
199. The proposed programme is efficient and effective, with a total investment of US$ 105 million expected to
generate significant climate adaptation outcomes. By reducing post-harvest food losses, the programme
contributes directly to increased food availability, with co-benefits of reduced greenhouse gas (GHG)
emissions; projected reduction of around 0.1 gigatonne of CO₂-equivalent emissions. This mitigation co-
benefit is coupled with economic and social benefits such as enhanced food security, improved household
incomes, and better health outcomes through reduced aflatoxin exposure, improved food safety and
availability.
200. An economic analysis for the programme was undertaken using a cost-benefit analysis approach.
Programme costs and benefits were assessed over a 10-year period and using country-specific social
discount rates as presented in Annex 3. The analysis weighed up the total programme costs against the
benefits associated with the uptake of equipment by smallholder farming households. These benefits
included the income gains associated with the value of food that would otherwise have been lost in the
absence of the programme interventions, the health benefits from reductions in aflatoxin exposure, the
improved nutritional status (particularly of infants) from avoided food losses, and the climate benefits
associated with reduced greenhouse gas emissions from food production. The results of the economic
analysis suggest that, under the current set of assumptions, the total package of interventions is cost-
beneficial, with the total programme investment generating an economic Net Present Value (ENPV) of
US$ 69 million, with a benefit to cost ratio (BCR) of 8:1 and an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR)
of around 56%. On this basis, there is a clear economic case for implementing the proposed programme.
Additional analyses were conducted to test the sensitivity of the findings to changes in the underlying
assumptions, including around the choice of discount rate, the shadow price of carbon, and the proportion
of existing food production for which the quality (and safety) improves as a result of programme
interventions. The results of these analyses are presented in Annex 3 and show that, at a programme
level, the economic summary statistics are robust to changes in the input values.
201. At a country level, both the ENPV and BCR is positive across all seven countries. The highest net benefits
are experienced in Burkina Faso and Ethiopia and can be explained, at least in part, by the choice of crops
used to illustrate the impacts of interventions, and which have comparatively higher prices; the relatively
high prevalence of aflatoxin-related illnesses in Burkina Faso
Application of best practices
202. An evaluation of proposed physical food loss reduction solutions (FL-RS) was conducted across all seven
target RE-GAIN countries to identify those with the highest potential to reduce postharvest food losses
and protect the harvests against growing impacts from climate hazards. Stakeholder engagements in all
seven countries provided critical nuances including advantages, disadvantages, and barriers for use,

=== PAGE 65 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 63
D
particularly for smallholder farmers, while a detailed assessment of best practices was used to underpin
the assessment.
203. In addition to the above-mentioned prioritizations following the climate rationale, the final selection of
solutions considered additional prioritization factors to ensure the success of the RE-GAIN programme
and achieve lasting systemic changes in all target countries. Those factors included solution’s estimated
potential in reducing food losses, estimated level of environmental pollution/ GHG emissions during the
use of the solutions, current level of awareness of the farmers about the solution’s proper use and
maintenance, availability of selected FL-RS in the country, and potential for their supply scalability and job
creation through locally produced or assembled solutions and improving market linkages. The results of
the evaluation, including major climate hazards for each country, a package of proposed solutions,
together with their food loss reduction potential and existing barriers for their increased adoption is
presented in Annex 2.
204. Based off the above-described analysis, basket of options approach was selected for the RE-GAIN
programme FL-RS adaptation strategy – adopting bespoke combinations of FL-RS tailored to the context
of each country. The key best practice innovations for FL-RS identified include mechanical multi-crop
threshers and shellers (preferably solar-powered) combined with moisture meters for monitoring the level
of moisture in the target crops, and communal storage structures, with the FL-RS uses on the individual
farm level, such as tarpaulins and plastic sheets for drying crops, hermetic storage technologies (hermetic
bags, silos) used for storage of the crops, and storage protectants and control agents, preferably biological
origin.
205. In addition to the technical FL-RS designs described above, the overall project design considers AGRA’s
experience and lessons learnt from previous projects. These lessons and best practice are described
below.
▪ AGRA’s last mile delivery model through training and supporting VBAs (village-based advisors) and
agro-dealers in the target countries, who are largely in place to take the FL-RS solutions to farmers
and their institutions.
▪ AGRA has multiple and largescale projects working on food security and resilience in the target
countries, which will allow this project to leverage at a scale and have a jump start.
▪ AGRA has been working on multiple financial models, which could be used to move this project
forward.
▪ AGRA has a well fledged infrastructure and country teams in the target countries that will ensure a
jump start and successfully delivery.
▪ It was working with the governments of these target countries on major food security, climate and
market issues, which this project will be leveraging both the political good will but also the
infrastructure.
▪ Farmer Education is a requisite. Partnerships with input providers and loss reducing technology
manufacturers can help in training smallholder farmers to promote the sustainability of loss reducing
efforts in the value chain.
▪ Financial products should consider the production cycle and set requirements for repayment that are
informed by the crop season.
▪ Financing models should include risk sharing strategies for different market actors in the value chain
▪ Partnerships and brokering agreements between financial institutions and strategically positioned
market actors with the aim of unlocking barriers to accessing finance and encourage the development
of tailor-made financial products that enable actors to innovate, invest for the expansion of operations
and increase equipment acquisition for light to medium processing.
▪ Create incentives for SMEs to invest in food loss reduction technologies.
▪ Technical assistance to assist value chain actors to develop loan proposals and business plans for
quick processing of loans.
▪ Smallholder farmers must be convinced of the return on their investment requiring linkages to a
reliable market that will provide a fair price for the product’s quality.
▪ Promoting technologies for multiple crops to increase uptake of post-harvest technologies that are
introduced through a program.
▪ The involvement of technology distribution companies leads to faster adoption by farmers.

Core Indicator 5: Degree to
which GCF investments
contribute to strengthening
institutional and regulatory
frameworks for low
emission climate-resilient
development pathways in a
country-driven manner | Development of national policies, strategies,
frameworks and implementation plans
aiming to address agricultural sector and
climate change challenges has been a
priority for many of the African governments
in the recent years. Despite this, there are
still considerable gaps in the policy and
regulatory environment across the target
countries that impede the mainstreaming of
sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector.
This gap is underpinned by several factors.
First, there is limited or no use of the
scientific evidence base in the formulation of
policies and strategies on post-harvest loss
reduction. Second, policies generally lack
coordination and harmonisation across
sectors, limiting opportunities for integrated
approaches to building resilience in the
agriculture sector. Third, where policies or
regulatory frameworks are in place, they are
often constrained to agricultural input
subsidies — mainly for fertilizer and seeds
— with no subsidy on post-harvest
technologies. This further extends to the
focus of information and extension services,
which are generally geared towards primary
production, and less so to post-harvest
processes. Similarly, existing taxation
regimes do not favour or incentivise
investments in storage and processing
technologies for food in the smallholder
system. | low | Policies and incentives are in
place to facilitate the development
of local markets and support to
smallholder farmers for the uptake
of FL-RS in each of the seven
target countries. Manufacturers,
importers and suppliers are
incentivised to establish local
markets through tax exemptions,
subsidies and affordable access to
finance. | The RE-GAIN programme will support the
revision and operationalization of policies in
support of enabling FL-RS investments,
including tax exemptions/incentives, certification
and standards for FL-RS quality, and promote
successful FL-RS business models for scaling-
up and replication. This will include collaborating
with government entities to revise and update
key policies which are not responding to the
current and future food losses and climate
challenge scenario. RE-GAIN will engage
relevant government ministries and agencies to
review existing regulations and policies and
proposing amendments to facilitate the
development of local markets and support to
farmers for the uptake of FL-RS. This will
include introducing/strengthening national
policies that provide financial incentives such as
tax exemptions, subsidies, or reduced interest
rates for FL-RS investments, making it more
attractive for private and public sector
investments. | Multi-
countries
Core Indicator 6: Degree to
which GCF investments
contribute to technology
deployment, dissemination,
development or transfer
and innovation | The deployment of FL-RS in the target
countries is extremely limited, particularly
among smallholder farmers. This includes
extremely limited penetration of suppliers
into rural markets. There are also few
innovative financial mechanisms that can
unlock local markets for FL-RS. | low | FL-RS technologies are
disseminated in the local
communities, both directly to
farmers (for smaller, low-cost
solutions) and through MSME
service providers for larger
solutions that can be shared
among farmers. Innovative finance
mechanisms are in place in the
target communities linking
manufacturers, suppliers, agro-
dealers, financiers, service
providers and farmers. | FL-RS technologies will be transferred to
smallholder farmers, MSMEs, farmer
cooperatives and youth groups through
innovative finance models and smart subsidies.
The RE-GAIN programme will facilitate
partnerships for technology transfer through the
innovative finance models, using the smart
subsidies to derisk investments for suppliers
and financiers. Beneficiaries will also be trained
on the effective use of FL-RS, including the
operation of service provision businesses. | Multi-
countries

Core indicator 7: Degree to
which GCF Investments
contribute to market
development/transformation
at the sectoral, local, or
national level | Local markets for FL-RS are
underdeveloped, particularly among
smallholder farming communities. Limited
awareness and financial capacity of farmers
constrains demand for FL-RS, which in turn
constrains interest among manufacturers
and suppliers to develop the local market.
This problem is exacerbated by a lack of
innovative financial mechanisms that can
catalyse investment in local market
development. | low | The local market is unlocked,
encouraging vendors of FL-RS
equipment to invest in distribution
channels, marketing, maintenance
capacity and other needed
infrastructure to make the
equipment available and
serviceable in a sustainable
manner. This shift will lead to
reduced prices and increased
affordability, creating a self-
sustaining system that will enable
the phased out of programme
subsidies. | RE-GAIN will build the capacity of local MSMEs
to provide access to FL-RS, either through
improving local supply or offering services to
farmers. MSMEs and youth groups will be
trained on technical topics related to FL-RS
service provision, including: i) proper service
management and process optimization; ii) the
safe, effective, efficient and sustainable
operation of the equipment; iii) compliance with
international and local quality standards for
post-harvest services; and iv) the management
of the fee-for-service model. This will be
complemented by the development of
innovative financial mechanisms that will
catalyse investments in FL-RS through
partnerships and smart subsidies. | Multi-
countries
Core indicator 8: Degree to
which GCF investments
contribute to effective
knowledge generation and
learning processes, and
use of good practices,
methodologies and
standards | While farmers are distinctly aware of the
impacts of food loss on food security, many
do not fully understand the specific causes
of food loss, or the potential solutions
available to reduce food loss. This includes
limited understanding of basic harvesting
practices that can reduce risk of food loss,
or the related post-harvest practices and tool
available. Moreover, many farmers are not
fully aware of the impacts that CC has on
food loss and the consequent need to
change post-harvest practices. | low | Farmers are aware of the need
and options available for FL-RS,
thereby enhancing demand for,
and adoption of, FL-RS in the
target communities. This adoption
of FL-RS will ultimately reduce
food losses and result in the core
benefits of the programme for
smallholder farmers – namely
improved food security, increased
resilience to climate shocks, and
reduced GHG emissions. | RE-GAIN will provide direct support to
smallholder farmers to not only know what FL-
RS options are available, but also to understand
the need for adopting such options – particularly
when considering the impacts of a changing
climate – as well as how to optimise the use of
FL-RS. Moreover, RE-GAIN programme will
identify and train local MSMEs and cooperatives
to develop FL-RS service provision operations
in the target communities. | Multi-
countries

Activity 1.1.7: Provide technical
assistance to food processors
to facilitate a shift to whole-
grain flour production | In addition to the FL-RS approaches that will be promoted above, the programme will
provide technical assistance to promote the transition from refining grains to
wholegrain flour production. As depicted in Figure 4, wholegrain production can
produce almost 50% more bread from the same quantity of unprocessed wheat,
helping reduce the “hidden food loss” associated with over processing. The
intervention will target food producers – millers in particular – providing technical
assistance along several themes, including: i) developing MSME capacity; ii) raising
awareness of market opportunities for wholegrains; iii) support for technology
transfers; and iv) facilitating access to finance.
The activity will form part of a broader initiative that is promoting wholegrain food for
use in school meal programmes, which the RE-GAIN programme will leverage and
complement. | ▪ Develop awareness raising

service providers (MSMEs,

youth-led initiatives) | The RE-GAIN programme will identify and train local MSMEs and cooperatives to
develop FL-RS service provision operations in the target communities. This training
will prioritise women- and youth-led initiatives to maximise the social benefits and
build resilience among these vulnerable groups. The programme will adopt a rigorous
process of scouting and in-depth assessment using standardized tools of the
potential service providers and food processors complemented by intensive vendor
support to make sure operations and maintenance skills for the equipment are
transferred. The identification of suitable entities will be supported by government
(decentralised and local) authorities as well as through digital platforms, social media,
industry players where potential service providers are connected or enrolled, and via
the scoping exercises under Activity 1.2.1.
Once service providers are identified and enrolled in the programme, a rigorous
assessment of their capacity and skills will be conducted, before organising intensive
“boot camps” to train these entities on the FL-RS service provision. These training
camps will cover several technical topics related to FL-RS service provision,
including: i) proper service management and process optimization; ii) the safe,
effective, efficient and sustainable operation of the equipment; iii) compliance with
international and local quality standards for post-harvest services; and iv) the
management of the fee-for-service model. In addition to the technical training on FL-
RS service provision, entities will be supported to develop their business into a
sustainable entity. This will include training on basic business practices (such as
financial literacy, cash flow management, record keeping, and risk management) and | ▪ Host scouting missions to identify

under Output 2.2.

though VBAs and other last mile delivery channels. This model is not only sustainable
in the long run but also creates an incentive for end users to scale up the technology
in the very initial stages. Exchange visits will also be arranged to share experiences
between service providers operating in nearby villages.

E
E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements
206. The M&E structure for RE-GAIN has been developed to be in-line with AGRA’s existing M&E structure,
to ensure a cohesive approach, as well as with GCF’s policy requirements such as Monitoring and
Accountability Framework and Integrated Results Management Framework. It has also been designed
to target the GCF-supported project Outcome and Output key performance indicators (KPIs; listed
above) and the broader programme performance, respectively. A baseline survey will be conducted
during the inception phase of the programme, and AGRA will update the performance indicators where
applicable based on the results alongside the baseline results as part of the inception report. The
performance monitoring will be conducted on an ongoing basis by a dedicated M&E officer with close
oversight from the AGRA M&E Team and Board. The GCF-supported project KPIs will be monitored
during the implementation period as a subset of the broader performance monitoring of the
programme over the long-term. The responsibilities of the M&E officer will include:
• Data collection and performance measurement.
o The M&E officer will work closely with each project intermediary, to aggregate data on the
non-financial fund metrics obtained directly from the intermediaries. This will include data to
support the development of site-specific baselines; Economic activity; Market share;
Production; Sales volume and value. Additional data will be periodically and systematically
aggregated by intermediaries during implementation relating to production, sales volumes
and value, human resources, among others.
o Periodic site visits will be conducted to assess and verify the results presented by the
intermediaries.
o When appropriate and deemed necessary, external consultants may support the data
collection and measurement process.
• Performance assessment and recommending corrective actions: Based on the predefined output
and outcome targets and metrics, corrective actions may be defined by engaging with the
intermediary.
• Reporting: Performance against the Outcome and Output KPIs are reported to the GCF on an
annual basis.
207. The monitoring and reporting on the Outcome and Output KPIs will be integrated into the broader
performance monitoring system for AGRA. AGRA has a comprehensive MRV system that extends
from grantees, to country, region, corporate level review and verification processes supported by an
online Management Information System (MIS). AGRA also has standard Data Quality Assurance
(DQA) mechanism to ensure quality performance data are collected and reported. AGRA will integrate
RE-GAIN into the existing MRV system. The M&E system for RE-GAIN will be validated and finalised
by the AGRA Board, in collaboration with the AGRA M&E Team, during the programme’s inception
and will continue to be updated as the programme progresses. The AGRA Board and M&E Team will
work with the M&E officer to evaluate the interplay between project indicators and results to assess
the effectiveness of the M&E system and amend it where appropriate.
Evaluation
208. The performance will be independently evaluated against the Outcome and Output KPIs at the mid-
term and final stages of the project implementation period, specifically in years 3 and 5, as per GCF
evaluation policy. These evaluations will be conducted by an independent evaluation specialist, under
the oversight of AGRA.
Financial Reporting
209. AGA will track the program’s finances and will produce semi-annual reports that summarizes material
developments of these. AGRA will develop valuation processes and procedures for the underlying
portfolio investments and ensure these are done in accordance with Accounting Standards
Codification (ACS) 820-10 on a semi-annual basis on June 30th and December 31st.

=== PAGE 81 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 79
F
F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT
F.1. Risk factors and mitigation measures
Selected Risk Factor 1
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Low Medium
Description
Lack of programme ownership by beneficiaries and target governments leads to weak uptake and/or sustainability of FL-RS
Mitigation Measure(s)
Detailed stakeholder engagement has been conducted throughout the programme design stage, and will continue throughout
implementation to ensure that interventions are fully owned by and meet the individual needs of the target countries. This
consultative process will ensure that the probability of this risk remains low.
Selected Risk Factor 2
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium High
Description
Financial crisis caused by unstable macro-economic variables impacts the market development and increases the risk profile
of financial mechanisms.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The approach to designing the financial models was built on a foundation of a detailed analysis of local markets and
economic fundamentals, ensuring that the mechanisms are robust to withstand fluctuations in the macro-economic situation.
Multiple derisking approaches have been integrated into the market development models, which will provide a buffer to
reduce the potential impact of economic fluctuations on the effectiveness of the programme.
Selected Risk Factor 3
Category Probability Impact
Governance Medium Medium
Description
Political instability in some target countries leads to disruptions to the implementation of project interventions, or
ineffectiveness of enabling environment actions.
Mitigation Measure(s)
During times of political instability, AGRA will increase engagement with national stakeholders to ensure investments and
programmes are not disrupted, reducing the potential impact of political instability on programme outcomes.
Selected Risk Factor 4
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium High
Description
Lack of willingness from end users to adopt the FL-RS reduces market transformation potential and long-term impacts of the
project.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The RE-GAIN programme will engage in extensive awareness campaigns and capacity development to ensure that farmers
fully understand the threats posed by climate change on post-harvest loss, the benefits of FL-RS for reducing losses, and the
financial viability of FL-RS solutions. Combined with supply-side investments that increase availability and affordability of FL-
RS, these actions will reduce the probability of this risk to low.
Selected Risk Factor 5
Category Probability Impact
Credit Medium Medium
Description
Credit default and end beneficiaries’ failure to repay the loans and meet their contractual obligations may reduce the
effectiveness of financial models and erode trust in the system.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The credits are administrated by financial institutions with excellent knowledge of their customers and the market within which
they operate. The financial models have also been designed with multiple levels of derisking, including smart subsidies to
reduce burden on end beneficiaries (which will increase likelihood of being able to pay), as well as broad market development
in agriculture value chains and capacity development of farmers and MSMES. Assessments will also be conducted by
AGRA to ensure alignment of credit providers with policies and procedures on compliance and AML
Selected Risk Factor 6
Category Probability Impact
Governance Low Medium
Description
Regulatory constraints impede the implementation of the project interventions in individual countries.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The interventions have been designed to account for legal frameworks and internal policies to ensure that the activities align
with regulations in each country, ensuring the project can be implemented within the programme timeline.
