G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
G.1. GCF Policies and social risk assessment
Risk Categorisation
210. The programme is assigned a Category B risk rating as per the GCF Revised Environmental and Social
(E&S) Policy definition. The rationale for the risk rating is as follows:
▪ The Programme will target established smallholder farms and MSMEs and as such will not be
associated with any greenfield development or land acquisition;
▪ The Programme is designed to facilitate the adoption of FL-RS solutions by smallholder farmers
and MSMEs through existing supply chains in the countries. As such, the Programme will not
directly be involved in the manufacturing, transportation and/or installation of the FL-RS solutions;
▪ The E&S risks associated with the Programme are mainly associated with the regions targeted by
the Programme in each in-scope country, the contextual circumstances of the existing smallholder
farms targeted to participate in the interventions and the in-country partner(s) chosen to facilitate
the interventions; and
▪ The risk assessment conducted on the Programme highlighted limited adverse E&S risks and
impacts that are few (see Programme E&S Risk Assessment) and can be readily addressed
through actionable mitigation measures (see Programme EandS Risk Management Framework).
Programme E&S Risk Assessment
211. In order to meet the GCF EandS due diligence requirements expected on a Category B type project, AGRA
conducted two desktop assessments, namely a country and region contextual EandS risk analysis for
each in-scope country and focus crops and an intervention level EandS risk assessment taking into
account the envisaged Programme activities. The key EandS risks stemming from these assessments are
summarised as follows:
Overall Programme E&S Risks
212. i) AGRA procures the services of local NGOs and/or private sector actors to support the building of the
intervention ecosystem (i.e., to build the connections between manufacturers, agro-dealers, and the end
recipients such as smallholder farmers and MSMEs). Reputational risks may exist associated with the
Programme partners chosen due to poor E&S performance and/or reputation.
213. ii) AGRA will preferentially identify and partner with locally available manufactures of FL-RS technologies
for inclusion into the Programme. Reputational risks associated with manufactures due to EandS related
risk factors such as:
▪ Sub-standard labour and working conditions including potential for poor occupational health and
safety practices;
▪ Limited pollution control measures and waste management; and/or
▪ Road transport health and safety risks associated with transporters (internal/external) of FL-RS
technologies to agro-dealers.
214. iii) AGRA will partner with locally available financial institutions to provide financing at preferential rates to
either manufacturers or smallholder farmers / MSMEs. Reputational risks associated with financial
institutions due to poor and/or unethical lending processes (especially those related to microfinance
institutions) which may lead to smallholder farmer indebtedness.
Contextual E&S Risks
215. i) Conflict areas were identified in certain regions in Burkina Faso and Ethiopia where the Programme is
intended to be implemented may lead to challenges in meeting Programme objectives including potential
health, safety and security risks to Programme personnel.

=== PAGE 83 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 81
G
216. ii) Depending on the country, maturity and oversight of labour regulations, reputational risks associated
with labour and working conditions may exist on the target smallholder farms and/or MSMEs. These may
include the presence of child and/or forced labour practices on these farms, poor health and safety
practices, non-compliance’s with labour laws and non-alignment with the requirements of international
standards such as those set out in the ILO and IFC PS 2.
217. iii) AGRA conducted a desktop Indigenous Peoples (IPs) screening and vulnerability assessment of the
Programme. Of the seven countries assessed, no recognized IPs were identified in Zambia and Malawi.
However, potential IPs were identified in specific regions within Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Tanzania,
and Uganda. There are no circumstances in which the Programme will result in any form of displacement
of IPs, affect access to land or natural resources, nor will the Programme interventions be likely to include
activities that significantly alter the physical or social context of the local areas in which the interventions
will be implemented. However, the identification of the IPs underscores the need for careful and proactive
consideration of any risk that might arise during program implementation and requires tailored approaches
to address the unique circumstances and challenges of indigenous communities in the identified areas. In
this regard, the Programme will proactively work with IPs and their representatives to ensure that the
programme (i) does not cause any significant harm to their way of life and livelihoods and (ii) enhances or
complements their existing livelihoods. Further, using appropriately translated ESS and programme
information to local languages, IPs will also be continuously engaged before and during programme
implementation, with feedback and suggestions from leaders and representatives used as proactive
measures to ensure a successful implementation of the programme.
Intervention Level E&S Risks
218. i) The Programme includes the introduction and promotion of novel FL-RS technologies. The innovative
technology introduced to small holder farmers and/or MSMEs may lead to unintended health, safety and/or
environmental risks such as injury or increased pollution generation as a consequence of recipients not
being adequately trained on their correct use and/or waste disposal.
219. ii) AGRA procures NGOs to create awareness and demonstrate the FL-RS in collaboration with
manufacturer and agro-dealers. These trainings are conducted at selected smallholder farms. NGO staff
may not have adequate technical competencies to provide training on unintended health, safety and/or
environmental risks associated with the FL-RS technologies. NGOs may deliver training that is not
culturally suited to the target audience and may be biased from an inclusion and gender perspective.
Programme E&S Risk Management Framework
220. AGRA has in place a centralised Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) that will be
applicable to all interventions initiated in AGRA’s focus countries. The ESMS is designed to ensure that
potential negative E&S impacts of AGRA interventions are minimized, while positive effects on
communities and the environment are enhanced. It follows the guidance of IFC Performance Standard 1
and includes an EandS policy and procedures, identification of risks and impacts, management programs,
organisational capacity and competency, emergency preparedness and response, stakeholder
engagement, grievance management, monitoring, review and reporting.
221. In addition, AGRA has developed a Programme E&S Action Plan (ESAP), which provides a framework for
E&S action across the entire RE-GAIN Programme. Once the specific communities and beneficiaries have
been selected, the Programme ESAP will be adapted where appropriate to be specific to the E&S risks
and impacts associated with the individual country, region, crop and combination of FL-RS applicable to
the specific intervention envisaged. The Programme ESAP sets out mitigation measures for each of the
risks identified in the E&S risk assessments conducted.
222. Full details on Programme E&S risk identification and mitigation are provided in Annex 6 of the Funding
Proposal.

=== PAGE 84 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 82
G
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan
223. To ensure that the RE-GAIN Programme is designed, financed, implemented and monitored in a gender-
responsive and socially inclusive manner, a comprehensive Gender Assessment and Gender and
Inclusion Action Plan has been developed (Annex 8). These outputs comply with the GCF’s Gender Policy,
as well as AGRA’s own Gender and Inclusion Strategy, ESMS Manual and Safeguarding of Vulnerable
Persons and Youth Policy.
224. RE-GAIN’s Gender Assessment considers the various factors (including those exacerbated by climate
change) that influence gender inequality in each of the seven target countries’ agriculture sectors and
identifies opportunities for women, men and youth to contribute to inclusive resilience building within post-
harvest value chains. These opportunities are captured in a Programme-wide Gender and Inclusion Action
Plan, which defines activities, indicators and targets specific to women and youth in their capacities as
smallholders, cooperative networks, MSMEs and FL-RS vendors.
225. The Gender Assessment combines extensive literature review, carried out on a country-by-country basis
on the key contextual factors that enable and / or impede gender equality progress at a national -level,
with primary findings drawn from 28 national and local stakeholder engagements spread evenly across
the seven countries.
226. Using these information sources to examine the disparities (and underlying drivers) between women, men
and youth within the context of agriculture and climate change in each of RE-GAIN’s countries, several
clear, common and consistent themes on gender and inclusion emerge: Improved, inclusive access to
finance for women and youth is a fundamental enabler for increased and more equitable participation in
FL-RS. The upfront cost of many solutions is a primary barrier to adoption by women and youth. It is
therefore critical that inclusive and diverse finance options and advisory services are made available to
these groups. Inclusive and accessible training and information on FL-RS for women and youth is
necessary to empower and capacitate them as farmers and business owners. This includes both technical
and financial advisory support on: FL-RS tools, equipment and structures; the benefits of changing current
practices or preferences; and on financial management and marketing – including options available,
budgeting, saving, repayment processes, pricing, market quality standards and control etc. Such training
must be designed in a gender sensitive and highly context specific manner. This must be coupled with
sensitization of male community members, to overcome persistent disparities between women and men’s
access to financial, technical, infrastructural and capacity building resources. The deliberate and targeted
appointment of extension service delivery partners is fundamental to achieving many of these
recommendations, meaning the selection of RE-GAIN’s implementation partners must incorporate a
specific technical competency on demonstrated experience of working with women, youth and other
marginalised groups. Adopting a dual top-down bottom-up approach to amplify women and youth’s voices
on FL-RS is needed to create sustainable shifts in the way women and youth engage in, and benefit from,
RE-GAIN’s support. This means that on-the-ground, community centred efforts must be coupled with more
inclusive, enforceable FL-RS policies. These recommendations, together with RE-GAIN’s overarching
goal, outcomes and outputs, were used to develop the Gender and Inclusion Action Plan which includes
both qualitative and quantitative targets over the five-year period. Implementing the measures set out in
this Plan will result in 1,284,321 women (~50% of the total beneficiaries) benefitting from RE-GAIN’s
support. A dedicated RE-GAIN Gender Expert will oversee the delivery and M&E of the Gender and
Inclusion Action Plan, and ensure continued compliance with related processes, including application of
the grievance mechanism, safeguarding protocols and ESMS. The cost of this expert has been budgeted
for the full five years, along with related expenses required to deliver all activities.
G.3. Financial management and procurement
227. AGRA has a comprehensive financial management system designed to ensure accountability,
transparency, and efficient use of resources. Below is an overview of AGRA’s financial management
structure, including financial monitoring systems, financial accounting, auditing, and disbursement

=== PAGE 85 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 83
G
methods. All the structures listed below will be applied throughout the implementation of the RE-GAIN
programme.
1. Financial Monitoring Systems
228. AGRA transitioned from Navision to an integrated system - Oracle system. It employs robust financial
monitoring systems to oversee the management and utilization of funds. These systems are designed to
ensure that funds are used effectively and in line with AGRA’s strategic goals. Key components include:
▪ Budget Monitoring: AGRA uses a budgetary control system that monitors expenditures against
approved budgets. This allows for real-time tracking of financial performance and ensures that any
variances are identified and addressed promptly.
▪ Financial Reporting: AGRA’s financial monitoring includes regular financial reporting. These reports
provide detailed insights into the financial health of the organization and are used by management to
make informed decisions. The reports are typically produced on a quarterly basis and include budget
variance analysis, fund utilization reports, and financial forecasts.
▪ Grant Management System (AMIS): For managing funds disbursed through grants, AGRA uses a
dedicated grant management system-AMIS. This system tracks grant disbursements, monitors
compliance with grant terms, and ensures that grantees are using the funds as intended.
2. Financial Accounting
229. AGRA’s financial accounting practices are designed to ensure accuracy, consistency, and compliance with
international accounting standards. The key elements include:
▪ Accrual and Modified Cash Accounting: AGRA uses both accrual and modified cash accounting
system, which records financial transactions when they are incurred and ins some cases its only when
cash is exchanged as this is a requirement for some of our donors. This method provides a more
accurate picture of the organization’s financial position.
▪ Internal Controls: AGRA has a set of internal controls in place to prevent and detect errors or fraud.
These controls include segregation of duties, authorization of transactions, and reconciliation of
accounts.
▪ Financial Software: AGRA uses Oracle financial management software to record and manage its
accounting activities. This software integrates various aspects of financial management, including
accounts payable, accounts receivable, general ledger, and fixed assets management.
3. Auditing
230. AGRA places significant emphasis on auditing to ensure the integrity of its financial management. The
auditing process includes:
▪ Internal Audits: AGRA conducts regular internal audits to assess the effectiveness of its internal
controls, compliance with policies and procedures, and the accuracy of financial records. The internal
audit function reports directly to the Audit Committee of the Board to maintain independence.
▪ External Audits: In addition to internal audits, AGRA is subject to annual external audits conducted
by independent audit firms. These audits assess the accuracy of AGRA’s financial statements,
compliance with relevant regulations, and the effectiveness of its financial management systems. The
external audit reports are shared with stakeholders, including donors and board members, to ensure
transparency.
▪ Audit Committee: AGRA has an Audit Committee that oversees both internal and external audit
processes. The committee is responsible for reviewing audit findings, ensuring that management
takes corrective actions, and making recommendations to the Board on matters related to financial
governance.

=== PAGE 86 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 84
G
4. Disbursement Structure and Methods
231. AGRA’s disbursement structure is designed to ensure that funds are distributed efficiently and in
accordance with donor agreements and organizational objectives. The key elements include:
▪ Procurement: AGRA provides support through the procurement of various partner
organizations/implementing agencies, including governments, research institutions, and NGOs.
AGRA follows stringent procurement processes to ensure value for money. All procurement activities
are conducted in accordance with AGRA’s procurement policy, which includes competitive bidding,
vendor evaluation, and contract management. Project payments are typically made in tranches,
contingent upon the meeting of specific deliverables and reporting requirements. This ensures that
funds are used effectively and for their intended purposes.
▪ Direct Payments: For certain projects and operational costs, AGRA makes direct payments to
vendors or service providers. These payments are processed through AGRA’s financial management
system, ensuring proper documentation and authorization.
▪ Banking and Treasury: AGRA’s treasury management includes maintaining relationships with
banking partners, managing cash flows, and ensuring that funds are available to meet the
organization’s financial obligations. AGRA uses secure and reliable banking systems to process
disbursements and manage its funds.
AGRA Procurement Process Overview.
232. AGRA has a fully resourced procurement unit with clear policies and procedures that meet best practices.
The procurement policy and procedures manual are approved by the board and clearly outlines the
detailed processes to be followed and the approval matrix.
233. As a policy requirement, we follow competitive processes in sourcing goods and services for most of our
needs. The procurement process is fully automated starting from budget approval all the way through to
contracting.
Key steps in the procurement process
▪ Planning and Budgeting - Initiated by user units through annual budgeting and procurement
planning.
▪ Vendor Management - Handled by the procurement unit and includes vendor due diligence,
prequalification, registration and performance management.
▪ Requisitioning - User units initiate requests through AGRA’s automated SCM management system.
▪ Sourcing – Procurement initiates sourcing through AGRA’s automated SCM management system.
This includes evaluation of proposals/quotations by the Technical Evaluation Panels, negotiations and
review of proposed awards by the Procurement Committee, Executive Committee and the Board as
per the approval matrix.
▪ Contracting – this involves drafting of contracts, sharing with proposed consultants/vendors and
signing of the agreements. This is also done through AGRA’s automated SCM management system.
▪ Contract management – this includes management of all contracts by the requesting units, vendor
payments, and performance management.
Escrow Accounts
234. During the implementation of the financial models under Output 2.2, escrow accounts will be used to hold
funds until specific conditions have been met. In these instances, AGRA will open an account either at the
bank of the supplier, at the partner financial institution, or at its own bank (depending on the specific
agreement reached with the supplier and FI). This will be a USD denominated deposit account to which
AGRA is a signatory. Depending on the agreements reached, the other parties (suppliers, financiers) will
be included as co-signers as needed. Funds can only be released if the trigger in the agreement has
materialized.

=== PAGE 87 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 85
G
Governance and Oversight
235. The roles and responsibilities are clearly outlined in the procurement policy and procedures manual. The
Board is responsible for reviewing and approving the policies. The President appoints the Procurement
Committee comprising of nine members including the Chair, Vice chair, Secretary and Members who are
responsible for providing oversight and ensuring that the procurement system is fair, transparent,
competitive, and cost-effective. Internal Audit Function provides independent review and assurance on
risk management, controls, and governance processes.
Procurement Risk Assessment and management
236. AGRA has an elaborate Risk Management Framework that is overseen by the Enterprise Risk
Management Unit. AGRA defines procurement risk as the possibility that an event may impact its ability
to achieve it’s objectives, either adversely or favorably. To address this, AGRA's risk management process
involves identifying, assessing, measuring, and managing procurement risks to ensure they remain within
acceptable tolerance levels. This proactive approach aims to enhance transparency, accountability, and
stakeholder confidence in the procurement process. The procurement unit and other units have risk
registers that are updated on quarterly basis.
Other policies
237. AGRA has several other policies that complement the procurement policy. These include Human
Resources Policy, Code of Conduct Policy, The Whistle-blower Policy, Anti-Fraud Policy, and Anti-Money
Laundering and Counter Terrorism Financing policy. To gain further insight into our procurement
procedures, please refer to our procurement policy document embedded below.
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal
☒ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its
annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its
annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence.
Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding
proposal, including all annexes:
• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly,
together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for
confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and
• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential
information.

The RE-GAIN Food Loss Reduction Programme offers a comprehensive solution that aligns
with Zambia's climate priorities. By minimizing food waste, the programme contributes to
mitigating climate change through reduced greenhouse gas emissions. Additionally, it supports
adapting to climate change by ensuring food security and promoting resilient agricultural
practices.

I. Entity capacity assessment summary
11.1.1.1. BInatsrico idnufocrtmioanti oann adb ionust ttihteu ptiroonjeaclt aanssde tshsem enetnitty
3.
AGRA (formerly the Alliance for a Green Revolution in Africa), established in 2006 by
the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and the Rockefeller Foundation, is a pan-African institution
headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya. AGRA’s vision is to contribute to a food system-inspired
inclusive agricultural transformation across Africa, focusing on reducing hunger, improving
nutrition and adapting to climate change. AGRA’s strategic objectives include empowering and
building the resilience of smallholder farmers and supporting the development of inclusive
markets and finance to strengthen agricultural systems, as well as strengthening the capability
of African countries to sustain agricultural transformation. AGRA collaborates with both public

in undertaking the entity capacity assessment review.

4.
In 2023 AGRA’s total revenues were USD 128.3 million, up from USD 80.3 million in
2022. AGRA is applying for a USD 75.6 million grant from GCF for a five-year programme, RE-
GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa (hereinafter referred to as the RE-GAIN
programme), which falls into the category of medium projects (up to USD 250 million). The
annual volume of the proposed programme is less than 15 per cent of the average realized
annual revenues in 2022 and 2023. The programme is assigned a category B risk rating as
defined in the GCF revised Environmental and Social Policy.
1 .2 Methodology
5.
AGRA passed the Climate Investment Committee endorsement step (known as CIC2) on
11 October 2023 and submitted the first set of materials for review round #1 (completeness
check) on 17 September 2024 using the customized application form and material request. Two
further review rounds took place from September to November 2024. The external review team
(ERT) conducted a comprehensive review of the submitted materials and assessed AGRA’s
policies, procedures, capacities, track record and human resources against the GCF accreditation
st andards and checklist.
6.
Reference check interviews were conducted with AGRA’s two existing donor partners:
the German Development Bank Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), which has supported
AGRA since 2017; and the United Kingdom Foreign & Commonwealth Development Office,
which has supported AGRA since 2019. The referees were selected on the basis of their earlier
contributions to interventions similar to the RE-GAIN programme under the Partnership for
Inclusive Agricultural Transformation in Africa, and their mandate as public government
donors. The interviews confirmed that no issues related to the fiduciary role or other capacities
of AGRA had been identified during the long-term donor relationship.
7.
In addition to the desk review, the ERT conducted a site visit to AGRA’’s headquarters
(HQ) in Nairobi, during which the ERT validated the desk review findings, walked through
processes, and engaged in interviews and presentations across all areas of the assessment. A
total of 26 representatives from AGRA participated in the discussions, which provided further
ev idence of the capacity and procedures within AGRA.
8.
The applicant was assessed in accordance with the GCF policies and standards to the
ex tent applicable to accreditation below:
(a)
“Updated Strategic Plan for the Green Climate Fund 2024–2027”;
(b)
“Matters related to the accreditation framework” regarding the accreditation process
(decisions B.24/13 and B.26/01);
(c)
“Guiding Framework and Procedures for Accrediting National, Regional and
International Implementing Entities and Intermediaries, Including the Fund’s Fiduciary
Principles and Standards and Environmental and Social Safeguards” (decision B.07/02);
(d)
“Policy on Prohibited Practices” (decision B.22/19);
(e)
“Anti-Money Laundering and Countering the Financing of Terrorism Policy” (AML/CFT
Policy) (decision B.18/10);
(f)
“Policy on the Protection of Whistleblowers and Witnesses” (decision B.BM-2018/21);
(g)
“Environmental and Social Management System: Environmental and Social Policy”
(decision B.19/10);

1 .3 Contribution to the mandate of the GCF
9.
The RE-GAIN funding proposal outlines AGRA’s contributions to the GCF focus areas:
forests and land use; livelihoods of people and communities; and health, food and water
security. The proposed programme aims to enhance climate adaptation by supporting
innovative practices in the harvest and post-harvest value chain across seven African countries.
It also seeks to improve food security by addressing climate change impacts on post-harvest
food losses through the widespread adoption of food loss-reduction solutions (FL-RS) by
smallholder farmers. In addition, the funding proposal includes initiatives to raise awareness
among farmers about the effects of climate change on post-harvest losses and the benefits of the
FL-RS available in the market. Beyond the proposed programme, AGRA’s overall strategy and
mission align with the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024–2027 and contributes to the
mandates of the GCF.
1. 4 Track record and implementation of similar projects
10.
As evidence of its track record, AGRA referenced four earlier or ongoing interventions
that demonstrate its capacity to manage and deliver similar sized programmes in the proposed
regions. AGRA has a track record of both developing and leading continuous activities regionally
and of managing country-specific earmarked donor-funded interventions under different
bu siness lines with defined timelines, targets, activities and reporting requirements.
11.
The four referenced interventions are the multi-country Partnership for Inclusive
Agricultural Transformation in Africa (over USD 400 million), the Kaduna Consortium and the
Niger Consortium (two separate initiatives aimed at enhancing farmer resilience in Nigeria,
each with a funding of USD 1.5 million) and the Strengthening Regenerative Agriculture in
Kenya project (USD 4 million). These examples use grants as the primary financial instrument
and demonstrate AGRA’s track record of working with programmes involving agricultural
productivity, climate resilience and market development. Each intervention incorporates
practices aimed at addressing the impacts of climate change and promoting broader resilience
in food systems.
12.
Globally, AGRA has a network of donor partners ranging from international foundations
to government donors, including the founding partners Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and the
Rockefeller Foundation, the MasterCard Foundation, the United States Agency for International
Development, German Cooperation (KfW/BMZ) and the United Kingdom Foreign &
Commonwealth Development Office, demonstrating a strong funding base.
1. 5 Legal status, registration, permits and licences
13.
AGRA was registered in 2006 in the United States of America as a non-profit
organization under the US Internal Revenue Code (para. 501(c)(3)).

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 111
14.
AGRA has established country offices in 12 African countries and is physically
headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya. In Kenya, AGRA operates and is registered with the Registrar
of Companies as a branch of the AGRA US registration under section 366 of the Companies Act,
Cap 486 in 2006. This registration grants AGRA legal status and certain rights and obligations as
governed by the Kenyan Companies Act. AGRA will be both the accredited entity (AE) as the
contractual party with GCF and the executing entity (EE) of the RE-GAIN programme through
the programme management unit and the country offices.
15.
AGRA has established legal entities in six countries (Ghana, Kenya, Mali, Mozambique,
Rwanda and United Republic of Tanzania) to implement its country strategies and operations as
well as ensuring legal compliance. The country offices are extensions of AGRA and operate
under the supervision of AGRA HQ. In countries where AGRA is not registered, administrative
op erations are managed through hosting arrangements with partner organizations.
16.
In addition, several African governments (Burkina Faso, Ghana, Kenya, Malawi, Mali,
Rwanda and United Republic of Tanzania) have granted operational privileges and exemptions
through host country agreements.
II. Accreditation assessment
22.1.1.1. BKeays iacd mfidinuisctiraartyiv es taanndd fianradnsc i al capacities
17.
AGRA’s global board of directors includes members from the Rockefeller Foundation,
the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and independent directors, supported by board committees
dedicated to audit and risk, finance and human resources, nomination and governance, and
programme, whose task is to oversee key functions. In addition, AGRA has established five
oversight committees governed by their respective policies: procurement, grants, ethics, data
protection and safeguarding. Procedures for appointment, termination and remuneration are
de fined.
18.
AGRA’s organizational structure is documented, outlining responsibilities and reporting
lines. The process for setting objectives involves a five-year strategy cycle and annual reviews,
wi th key performance indicators and a corporate scorecard guiding the tracking of progress.
19.
Monitoring and reporting mechanisms are detailed in a quarterly management review,
with assigned roles and reporting frequencies. External evaluations are conducted at closure of
the larger programmes and at other strategic periods.
20.
AGRA’s financial statements are prepared as per the United States Generally Accepted
Accounting Principles, and include detailed statements of financial position, performance, cash
flo ws, changes in financial position, and notes and disclosures.
21.
AGRA uses an enterprise resources planning system for financial management. The
system is designed to support compliance with accounting principles and to manage fund
accounting. AGRA has a financial operations policy and its Procedures Manual covers financial
rep orting and donor fund management.
22.
The finance team, led by the chief financial officer and consisting of 15 people at the
headquarters and eight in-country finance officers, is responsible for maintaining financial
reporting systems. The IT infrastructure, which includes the AGRA Management Information
System, supports financial tracking and grants management.

=== PAGE 112 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05

23.
On the basis of AGRA’s track record, this assessment concludes that AGRA has
demonstrated that it is capable of managing and reporting financial activities aligned with its
str ategic framework and has operational capacity to handle international payments.
24.
AGRA has an audit and risk committee that oversees both internal and external audit
functions. The internal audit function is organized through a four-person internal auditing unit
(IAU), which operates under a formally approved charter. The IAU adheres to international
standards. The IAU reports functionally to the chair of the audit committee and administratively
to the AGRA President. Reports are also shared with senior management, who participate in the
pr ocess of addressing recommendations by the IAU.
25.
AGRA appoints an independent external audit firm for the audit process through a
competitive selection process, and audits are conducted in line with International Standards on
Auditing. The external auditor issues annual audit opinions. Management letters from the
auditor are reviewed by the audit and risk committee, which monitors internal controls and
fin ancial reporting.
26.
AGRA’s control framework is documented through various policies and manuals that
govern key operational areas, including governance, human resources and administration, grant
management, finance, procurement, risk management and internal audit. Internal policies and
procedures support regulatory compliance. The accuracy of financial data and reporting is
supported by the built-in controls within the enterprise resources planning system, such as
acc ess controls, data validation controls and segregation of duties.
27.
AGRA’s risk management processes involve a risk register and proactive measures, with
updates to the management and the board. Risks are assessed at multiple levels, and actions to
enhance mitigation measures are developed and documented. A comprehensive strategic risk
rep ort covering seven key risk areas is prepared quarterly for the board.
28.
AGRA has detailed procurement guidelines, including a procurement policy and a
Procurement Procedure Manual, outlining procurement actions, requirements and
responsibilities. AGRA has mechanisms for regular oversight of procurement, including risk
ass essments, audits, due diligence, key performance indicators and segregation of duties.
29.
AGRA uses an e-procurement system, which helps with streamlining workflows and is
designed to improve financial control and compliance with procurement policies. In addition,

AGRA’s transparency and accountability policy framework is built on its ethics policy

aw ard letter confirming their commitment to AGRA’s ethical standards.

34.
AGRA has structured its operations around thematic areas or business lines to achieve
its mission of transforming agriculture in Africa. Under the purview of the Vice-President –
Center of Technical Expertise, AGRA has thematic units aligned with the business lines for policy
and state capability, seed systems, climate adaptation, sustainable agriculture and resilience,
and inclusive markets, trade and finance. Under the purview of the Vice-President – Programs
Delivery, AGRA has the regional structures and country offices, as well as units for private sector
pa rtnerships, and gender, youth and inclusiveness.
35.
AGRA implements projects through a variety of instruments and partners, including
grant-making to local non-governmental organizations, farmer organizations, research
institutions, extension service providers and so on. All projects and interventions are aligned
with broader strategic goals as well as regional and country priorities. Grantees are the primary
implementing partners who execute interventions contained in the AGRA strategy. AGRA has a
comprehensive Grants Management Policies & Procedures Manual that outlines policies and
pr ocedures for managing the entire grant lifecycle.
36.
AGRA has a clear monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) framework and a
comprehensive MEL plan aligned with the AGRA Strategy 3.0. The MEL plan outlines the
principles, procedures, tools and responsibilities to execute the MEL framework. AGRA’s overall
corporate theory of change and results framework expand into detailed business line-specific
theories of change. The Director of Strategy & Monitoring & Evaluation and Learning reports
directly to the President and oversees the operational units for strategy and analytics, and
monitoring and evaluation (M&E). There are regional M&E officers for West Africa and for
So uthern and East Africa, as well as in-country M&E officers in eight countries.
37.
AGRA has demonstrated its experience in project identification, design and management
throughout the project cycle. There are fiduciary policies for quality control and oversight
mechanisms to monitor budgets and risks in project implementation phase. AGRA has broad
programme management and MEL capacity with defined procedures to adhere to established

2. 3 Environmental and social safeguards
41.
AGRA’s Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) is mandated by its
highest decision-making body and based on the IFC Performance Standards (PS). The key ESMS
document includes an environmental and social policy and separate sections covering (1)
identification of E&S risk and impacts, (2) ESMS management and organization, (3) ESMS
screening, (4) environmental and social impact assessments, (5) stakeholder engagement
(including a grievance mechanism) and (6) collaboration, information sharing and reporting.
AGRA’s ESMS is comprehensive and is supported by a set of detailed implementation manuals,
gu idelines and templates.
42.
AGRA does not have an Indigenous Peoples policy, but risk screening and monitoring
procedures for matters relating to Indigenous Peoples are covered in the ESMS implementation
manual/annexes, and sample reports provide evidence of implementation. The project-specific
Indigenous Peoples screening and vulnerability assessment annexed to the RE-GAIN funding
pr oposal further indicates AGRA’s capacity to comply with PS7 on Indigenous Peoples.
43.
AGRA’s ESMS requires and guides the identification and assessment of E&S risks and
impacts. The process for screening projects is described in the manuals, and the impacts are
assessed against PS1–PS8 using the environmental and social screening checklist. An E&S risk
cat egorization guideline provides guidance for assessing the E&S risk category.
44.
Most of AGRA’s interventions in the agriculture sector are classified as risk category C or
B (i.e. low to medium risk). The proposed RE-GAIN programme is classified as category B
because of identified potential exposure to social risks related to labour conditions and child
labour in post-harvest activities and work safety with agriculture machinery. The
environmental risks identified in the RE-GAIN programme are minimal, due to the focus on the
po st-harvest stage in the agricultural value chain.
45.
AGRA has incorporated a system in the delivery model for managing the E&S risks in the
grant projects and other activities. All grantees sign an undertaking (comprising a grant award
letter and a grant commitment checklist) that commits them to screening and mitigating
identified risks. AGRA’s grantees are required to document the E&S risks in a project-specific
risk register. AGRA is in the process of integrating the ESMS elements in the institution-wide
AGRA Management Information System where grant processing, reporting and results
mo nitoring are managed.
46.
The roles and responsibilities related to AGRA’s ESMS are defined in the ESMS Manual.
The information provided by AGRA demonstrates its capacity and expertise on environmental
and social matters at the HQ level and at the country level. A senior resilience specialist, sitting
in the Adaptation, Sustainable Agriculture and Resilience Unit, oversees the implementation of
the ESMS and has been closely engaged in the design of the RE-GAIN programme and its

=== PAGE 115 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 115
environmental and social components. Regional environmental and social officers, programme
officers and safeguarding officers in country offices support and review AGRA grantees’ E&S
risk screening. Country offices monitor and report performance to HQ. AGRA’s ESMS was
developed with an external consultant supported by earmarked KfW funding, and external
support for E&S risk matters is contracted as needed. AGRA’s ESMS training is mandatory both
for staff in environmental and social related positions and for grantees and other implementing
pa rtners.
47.
AGRA adopted the ESMS in 2020 and it has been rolled out in three West African pilot
countries: Burkina Faso, Ghana and Nigeria. AGRA has provided evidence of its track record of
implementing the ESMS with local grantees in those countries (E&S risk screening, development
of environmental and social action plans, monitoring and performance reporting). However, the
ERT assessment noted that, with the exception of Burkina Faso, the ESMS and required
capacities have not been fully established in six out of the seven countries targeted by the GCF-
funded RE-GAIN programme (Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania and
Zambia). AGRA has indicated that according to its training plan, all country training scheduled in
RE -GAIN countries would be completed by March 2025.
48.
AGRA has provided evidence of information disclosure and stakeholder consultation
both in terms of past track record and in the context of the current funding proposal. AGRA has
a whistle-blowing mechanism and other channels for receiving communications on E&S risk
matters. Guidance and templates for setting up a project-level grievance redress mechanism are
embedded in the ESMS and the annexed E&S Risk Assessment Toolkit. According to AGRA,
complaints related to E&S risk are rarely escalated from the beneficiary level to AGRA; rather,
the y are typically handled locally by the grant partner.
49.
The ERT assesses AGRA’s approach to environmental and social risk management as
appropriate and sufficient for category B. At the HQ level and in selected countries, AGRA has
policies, procedures and capacity to screen, monitor and manage E&S risks and impacts, but the
roll-out of the ESMS in all target countries of the RE-GAIN programme has not been completed.
AGRA has processes for independent reporting and grievance redress mechanisms at the global
an d country level and capacity to investigate alleged E&S risk violations.
50.
AGRA has zero-tolerance on Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) which is
covered in the human resources and administration policy, ethics policy and safeguarding of
vulnerable persons and youth policy. The applicant has identified potential SEAH risks for the
programme that include incidents involving staff or programme partners and backlash against
women in non-traditional roles. Additionally, subprojects will also be screened for SEAH risks.
Mitigation measures to manage these risks include training on AGRA safeguarding policy for
staff and programme partners, including regular mandatory training on SEAH/GBV awareness
and mitigation, integrating SEAH risk management into staff performance reviews and
programme planning as well as SEAH awareness sessions in onboarding for new staff. In
addition, the programme will establish community gender dialogues to address power
dynamics and promote positive masculinity as well as include men in household financial
management training to reduce tensions and offer support to survivors and victims of SEAH.
Furthermore, the applicant will ensure that access to grievance mechanisms for SEAH incidents
is provided to stakeholders, The applicant’s ESMS includes safeguarding officers at the country
level for undertaking SEAH assessments and training.
2. 4 Gender
51.
The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the
updated gender policy of the fund. The RE-GAIN programme's gender assessment highlights
significant challenges and barriers to gender equ ality in the agricultural value chains of target

=== PAGE 116 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 116
countries. Women face restricted access to critical resources such as land, credit, and climate-
resilient technologies, exacerbating disparities in productivity and income compared to men.
Sociocultural norms perpetuate unequal responsibilities, with women disproportionately
shouldering unpaid care work, limiting their participation in economic and decision-making
activities. Women are also underrepresented in leadership roles and formalized agricultural
structures, and they lack adequate training and access to extension services tailored to their
needs. Climate change further amplifies these gaps by intensifying women’s vulnerability due to
their reliance on natural resources and exclusion from climate resilience planning and
interventions. Institutional barriers, such as inadequate sex-disaggregated data and weak
im plementation of gender-sensitive policies, continue to impede progress toward equality.
52.
The gender and inclusion action plan for the RE-GAIN programme focuses on addressing
gender disparities through targeted and actionable measures. The gender action plan defines
activities, indicators, and targets for women's participation and benefit with corresponding
timelines, budget and gender expertise. During the programme start-up phase country-level
gender assessments and action plans will be developed. Key activities include implementing
capacity-building activities to enhance women's technical skills, leadership abilities, and access
to climate-resilient agricultural practices. To strengthen women’s participation in decision-
making, the plan includes strategies to establish women-led cooperatives and community-based
networks. It also supports awareness campaigns to challenge sociocultural norms that limit
women's roles in agriculture and land governance. Additionally, the action plan incorporates
provisions for training women in post-harvest handling technologies and facilitating their
access to markets through value chain enhancements, as well as finance by identifying relevant
credit schemes and financial instruments.
III. Conclusions and recommendations
3. 1 Conclusions
53.
On the basis of this assessment, AGRA has demonstrated high capacity to implement the
proposed RE-GAIN programme and manage the GCF-funded activities in alignment with the
relevant GCF policies. In addition, AGRA possesses the relevant legal status to implement the
RE -GAIN programme as stipulated in the PSAA pilot framework requirements.
54.
AGRA substantially meets the standards for the GCF basic fiduciary standards,
specialized fiduciary standards, environmental and social standards, and Gender Policy. The
assessment identified some deviations from the requirements as described in the GCF guidance
no tes, but the ERT does not consider these deviations as material gaps.
(a)
The applicant meets the requirements of the GCF basic fiduciary standards, the GCF
Policy on the Protection of Whistleblowers and Witnesses, the GCF Policy on Prohibited
Practices and the GCF AML/CFT Policy, and the specialized fiduciary standard for
project management; to the extent applicable to PSAA and the funding proposal;
(b)
The applicant meets the GCF revised Environmental and Social Policy, GCF interim
environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards and the GCF Information
Disclosure Policy on disclosure of E&S risk information in relation to the E&S risk
category B/I-2 (activities with potential mild adverse environmental and/or social risks
and/or impacts that are few in number, generally site-specific, largely reversible and
readily addressed through mitigation measures) and to the extent applicable to PSAA
accreditation;
(c)
The applicant has demonstrated that it has policies, procedures and competencies in
order to implement its gender approach, which is found to be consistent with the

=== PAGE 117 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 117
Updated GCF Gender Policy to the extent applicable to PSAA and the funding proposal.
The applicant has demonstrated that it has experience in gender considerations in the
context of climate change.

II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2. 1 Programme background
62.
Many African countries face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often
shortening the period suitable for agriculture and limiting the viability of farming in marginal
areas. Projections indicate that crop yields in some countries could decline by up to 50 per cent
by 2030, and net revenues for crops may fall by as much as 90 per cent by 2100, with
smallholder farmers being the most affected. Food security, however, is affected not only by
reductions in crop production but also by changes throughout the crop value chain, including
post-harvest. There is, therefore, a crucial need to address the impacts of climate change on the
full agriculture value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, processing and
dis tribution.
63.
To address these challenges, the programme aims to build the climate resilience and
adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers by promoting the wide-scale adoption of FL-RS, and
thereby increasing awareness, capacity and demand for, improving access and the availability
of, and enabling environment support for wide-scale adoption of these solutions. These
activities will contribute to achieving the targets of the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024–
2027: Target 2 (new direct access entities); Target 4 (food systems); Target 9 (adaptation and
locally led action); and Target 10 (local private sector early-stage ventures and MSME
inn ovation).
64.
The total financing for this proposed programme is USD 105 million with the total GCF
funding of USD 75,623,754 in grants, which leverages USD 29.3 million in grants in co-finance
by AGRA. The project is rated as ESS category B. The programme will directly benefit 2.6 million
vulnerable people and indirectly benefit a further 2.7 million vulnerable people (1.72 per cent of
the regional population) across seven countries: Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda,
Un ited Republic of Tanzania and Zambia.
65.
This initiative is the first PSAA multi-country programme in the African region,
supporting a direct access entity (regional). The PSAA applicant, AGRA, serving as both the AE
and the EE, has over 15 years of experience, operations in 11 countries and a team of over 200
professionals. AGRA’s experience and proven track record make it well positioned to lead this
large-scale PSAA initiative, with its extensive network including partnerships with
go vernments, local agribusinesses and civil society organizations in the agriculture sector.
66.
This funding proposal was developed with support provided by the Project Preparation
Facility.

Increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers
67.
Outcome 1 demonstrates several technical strengths, particularly in its comprehensive
approach to increasing the adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers. By integrating multiple
strategies, such as awareness-raising, training, technology transfer and market linkages, the
programme effectively addresses the demand sid e of FL-RS adoption.

68.
The extensive engagement strategy further strengthens the programme’s potential
impact. By leveraging village-based advisers and local institutions, the project aims to reach
over a million smallholder farmers, ensuring widespread awareness and participation across
seven countries. In addition, the project’s commitment to gender responsiveness and youth
inclusion is commendable, with specific modules designed to empower women and youth in
ag ricultural value chains.
69.
Another key strength lies in its market-oriented approach. By structuring agriculture
value chains and fostering linkages between smallholder farmers and cooperatives, MSMEs and
potential buyers (retailers, processors and exporters), the programme creates incentives for
quality compliance and increased adoption of FL-RS. Practical, on-the-ground demonstrations of
FL-RS provide an added layer of support, connecting technical training with real-world

70.

that combines business development and financial innovation, the programme builds the
capacity of local MSMEs, creates inclusive opportunities for women and youth, and fosters a
sustainable market ecosystem to ensure widespread access to FL-RS for smallholder farmers
an d MSMEs.
71.
The programme strengthens MSMEs by providing targeted training on operations and
maintenance, as well as sustainable business models, such as shared equipment use. Engaging
FL-RS vendors and incentivizing them to expand distribution channels and after-sales services
creates a self-sustaining system that extends the programme’s benefits beyond its five-year
du ration.
72.
Innovative financial mechanisms – designed for both smallholder farmers and
cooperatives or MSMEs – incorporate smart subsidies, pre-financing partnerships and the
integration of FL-RS into agricultural input packages. These approaches help to lower
affordability barriers for farmers while de-risking investments for stakeholders. This localized
approach ensures interventions are tailored to community needs, with scoping exercises and
ma rket intelligence guiding implementation for maximum relevance and impact.
73.
The programme’s significance lies in its focus on strengthening local markets, reducing
dependency on external suppliers and fostering economic growth within target communities.
Its scalable design enables replication across regions; meanwhile, empowering women and
youth fosters social equity and resilience. By reducing food loss, improving farmer incomes and
promoting climate-resilient technologies, outcome 2 aligns with broader development and
environmental goals, creating a de-risked investment environment that encourages sustained

III. Assessment against investment criteria
Scale: High
3. 1 Impact potential
77.
The proposed programme delivers transformative adaptation benefits by addressing the
vulnerabilities of smallholder farmers and communities across seven African countries. By
reducing food losses exacerbated by climate change, the programme strengthens food security
and enhances farm-level profitability. It prioritizes vulnerable groups such as women, youth and
MSMEs, empowering them through capacity-building, market development and social cohesion.
These efforts strengthen community resilience and directly support broader climate adaptation
ob jectives at both the regional and the national level.
78.
The programme also complements traditional agricultural adaptation strategies by
addressing critical gaps in the food value chain. Its emphasis on preserving harvested crops
alleviates pressure on land and water resources, promoting environmental sustainability while
mi tigating greenhouse gas emissions from compensatory agricultural production.
79.
Through catalytic grants and partnerships with the private sector, the programme
creates a foundation for sustainable markets and attracts additional investments, ensuring
benefits extend beyond the initial funding. Its scalable and replicable design further amplifies its
impact, positioning it as a model for regional and global food security initiatives.
Scale: High
3. 2 Paradigm shift potential
80.
By leveraging GCF grants, the programme catalyses a paradigm shift in post-harvest
development pathways through market stimulation, private sector engagement and scalable FL-
RS, addressing climate-induced challenges in the agricultural value chain. The programme
builds resilient agricultural ecosystems by empowering smallholder farmers with accessible,
climate-resilient food loss-reduction systems. It integrates multi-stakeholder engagement and
proven last-mile delivery systems, and it aligns with national and regional frameworks to
ensure long-term sustainability. These efforts create robust linkages between farmers, markets
an d financial systems.
81.
The programme’s design means there is potential for significant scaling and replication.
Strengthening local supply chains, fostering private sector innovation and embedding successful
models into national flagship programmes bring the potential to extend its reach. Partnerships
with regional climate institutions amplify its impact, facilitating adaptation across Africa.
Complementing this is a focus on knowledge-sharing, which leverages best practices and
innovative financial models to influence broader strategies.

=== PAGE 122 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 122
82.
By mobilizing private sector engagement, the programme multiplies the impact of GCF
grants, unlocking significant additional investment. This approach incentivizes innovation and
builds long-term market viability for FL-RS, empowering farmers while strengthening supply
ch ains.
83.
Lastly, the programme prioritizes resilience-building for smallholder farmers, with a
strong emphasis on inclusivity. Targeting women, youth and MSMEs ensures equitable benefits,
fostering economic empowerment and social equity while enhancing food security and climate
adaptation.
Scale: High
3. 3 Sustainable development potential
84.
The proposed programme extends its impact far beyond reducing food losses by
delivering a range of co-benefits that advance global goals (SDGs 2, 5, 8, 13), environmental
conservation and socioeconomic well-being. Its integrated design fosters systemic change at the
loc al, national and regional level, ensuring long-term sustainability and resilience.
85.
The programme generates substantial environmental co-benefits by reducing the need
for compensatory agricultural production, which conserves land and water resources, prevents
land degradation and protects ecosystems. Efficient agricultural practices decrease dependency
on fertilizers and pesticides, reducing the agricultural environmental footprint. In addition,
minimizing food waste lowers greenhouse gas emissions and prevents habitat destruction,
pr eserving biodiversity and supporting ecosystem sustainability.
86.
The programme’s mitigation efforts are significant, with greenhouse gas emission
reductions estimated at over 1 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over 15 years. These
reductions stem from decreasing compensatory food production emissions and mitigating land-
use changes. This aligns with the climate commitments of target countries, reinforcing their
pr ogress towards global climate goals.
87.
The programme enhances food quality and safety by reducing contamination risks,
improving nutrition and lowering food-borne illnesses, which positively impacts public health
systems. It generates employment through FL-RS value chains, creating opportunities for
women and youth, and fostering economic inclusion. Stabilizing food markets reduces price
volatility, benefiting both producers and consumers, while providing more predictable income
an d affordable food.
88.
Designed with gender sensitivity at its core, the programme ensures women’s equal
participation and leadership, fostering equity and leveraging their pivotal role in agriculture.
Inclusive strategies targeting women-led enterprises and youth integration ensure equitable
benefits, strengthening social and economic resilience.
Scale: Medium to high
3. 4 Needs of the recipient
89.
The target countries rely heavily on agriculture, a sector that employs 40–80 per cent of
their populations and contributes 20–40 per cent of their gross domestic product. However,
climate change poses severe threats to agricultural productivity, food security and economic
stability, exacerbated by low adaptive capacities and limited institutional readiness. The
programme directly responds to these challenges by focusing on reducing food losses and
building resilience in post-harvest systems – a crucial yet underdeveloped stage in the
ag ricultural value chain.
90.
Despite significant investments in improving production systems, post-harvest
processes remain neglected, leaving smallholder farmers – the backbone of these economies –

=== PAGE 123 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 123
particularly vulnerable to losses from spoilage, contamination and inefficiencies. RE-GAIN
ad dresses this gap by introducing FL-RS that improve storage, processing and market access.
91.
The programme is uniquely tailored to local contexts, working with community-level
suppliers, agro-dealers and MSMEs to integrate FL-RS sustainably into existing systems. This
localized approach not only fosters ownership and capacity but also complements the financial
and institutional constraints of national governments, whose limited resources are often
co ncentrated on production rather than post-harvest systems.
92.
A core strength of RE-GAIN lies in its focus on empowering vulnerable populations,
particularly smallholder farmers, women and youth. These groups face significant barriers to
adopting climate-resilient technologies owing to financial constraints and systemic inequalities.
The programme addresses these barriers by providing affordable FL-RS, building market
infrastructure and creating targeted business development opportunities for marginalized
groups. This inclusive approach promotes equitable development and enhances community
cohesion.
Scale: High
3. 5 Country ownership
93.
The RE-GAIN programme exemplifies a model of country-driven, context-specific
development, reflecting a strong commitment to aligning interventions with national priorities
while fostering local ownership. Through close collaboration with national designated
authorities, ministries and stakeholders, AGRA has co-developed project concepts and solutions
that address local challenges. This approach involved extensive consultations, including initial
government engagements, stakeholder workshops and iterative feedback loops. As a result, the
programme integrates with existing initiatives across climate change adaptation, agriculture
development, MSME support, financial systems and food security. A participatory design
process further strengthens the programme’s responsiveness. Extensive consultations with
communities, governments and non-governmental entities identified critical gaps and shaped
interventions that directly address the realities of smallholder farmers. This process continues
throughout implementation, allowing the programme to remain flexible and adaptive to
ev olving local contexts.
94.
The programme also leverages AGRA’s expertise and its established presence in the
target countries. Drawing from five-year country strategies, comprehensive gap analyses and
strategic partnerships, the programme builds on AGRA’s foundation to deliver systemic change.
Collaboration with governments ensures alignment with national climate strategies and
str engthens local capacity through active engagement of agro-dealers, MSMEs and others.
95.
This emphasis on government and local ownership is central to the programme’s
sustainability. By embedding interventions within national systems and fostering collaboration
among governments, private sector actors and development organizations, the programme
ensures its long-term viability. Local ownership is further reinforced by integrating
interventions into existing frameworks and systems, ensuring scalability and institutional
commitment.
Scale: Medium
3. 6 Efficiency and effectiveness
96.
The financial structure exemplifies a strategic and innovative approach to addressing
the barriers faced by smallholder farmers in adopting FL-RS while ensuring long-term market
sustainability. By integrating catalytic grants, concessional funding and capacity-building
initiatives, the programme creates a holistic framework that addresses critical challenges on
both the supply and the demand side of the agric ultural value chain.

=== PAGE 124 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 124
97.
At its core, the programme tackles the issues of affordability and access to finance,
which are significant obstacles for smallholder farmers. Through concessional funding and
grants, private sector manufacturers are incentivized to offer affordable FL-RS while financial
institutions are encouraged to develop tailored credit products that mitigate risks. This
approach ensures farmers gain access to sustainable solutions while suppliers and financial
institutions see reduced risk, fostering a market ecosystem that can thrive beyond the
pr ogramme’s duration.
98.
The programme’s economic efficiency and resource mobilization are particularly
noteworthy. The financial returns are positive across all target countries. Beyond immediate
returns, the programme lays a foundation for long-term market viability, unlocking additional
private sector investments and amplifying its systemic impact. In addition to financial returns,
the programme delivers significant economic and climate benefits.
99.
The programme incorporates best practices from AGRA’s extensive experience, such as
leveraging last-mile delivery systems through village-based advisers and agro-dealers, tailoring
financial products to crop cycles and promoting multi-crop food loss-reduction technologies.
These practices ensure interventions are relevant, scalable and adaptable to local contexts,
fostering systemic change and long-term resilience.

. AGRA has zero tolerance of
SEAH, which is covered in the AGRA human resources and administration policy, its ethics
policy and its policy on safeguarding vulnerable persons and youth. The applicant has identified

safeguarding policy for staff and programme partners, including regular mandatory training on
SEAH and gender-based violence awareness and mitigation; integrating SEAH risk management
into staff performance reviews and programme planning; and SEAH awareness sessions in
onboarding for new staff. In addition, the programme will establish community gender
dialogues to address power dynamics and promote positive masculinity as well as including
men in household financial management training to reduce tensions, and offering support to
survivors of SEAH. Furthermore, the applicant will ensure that access to grievance mechanisms
for SEAH incidents is provided to stakeholders, The applicant’s ESMS includes safeguarding
officers at the country level for undertaking SEAH assessments and training.
4.2 Gender policy
110.
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy.

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 126
111.
The RE-GAIN programme’s gender assessment highlights significant challenges and
barriers to gender equality in the agricultural value chains of the target countries. Women face
restricted access to critical resources such as land, credit and climate-resilient technologies,
exacerbating disparities in productivity and income compared with men. Sociocultural norms
perpetuate unequal responsibilities, with women disproportionately shouldering unpaid care
work, limiting their participation in economic and decision-making activities. Women are also
underrepresented in leadership roles and formalized agricultural structures, and they lack
adequate training and access to extension services tailored to their needs. Climate change
further amplifies these gaps by intensifying women’s vulnerability due to their reliance on
natural resources and exclusion from climate resilience planning and interventions.
Institutional barriers, such as inadequate sex-disaggregated data and weak implementation of
gen der-sensitive policies, continue to impede progress toward equality.
112.
The gender and inclusion action plan for the RE-GAIN programme focuses on addressing
gender disparities through targeted and actionable measures. The gender action plan defines
activities, indicators and targets for women’s participation and benefit, with corresponding
timelines, budget and gender expertise. During the programme start-up phase country-level
gender assessments and action plans will be developed. Key activities include implementing
capacity-building activities to enhance women’s technical skills, leadership abilities and access
to climate-resilient agricultural practices. To strengthen women’s participation in decision-
making, the plan includes strategies to establish women-led cooperatives and community-based
networks. It also supports awareness campaigns to challenge sociocultural norms that limit
women’s roles in agriculture and land governance. In addition, the action plan incorporates
provisions for training women in post-harvest handling technologies and facilitating their
access to markets through value chain enhancements, as well as finance by identifying relevant
credit schemes and financial instruments.

programme is co-financed with USD 29.38 million from AGRA, leveraging a total project cost of
USD 105 million. This programme covers seven countries in the Africa region. This is the first

4.4 Fiduciary
125.
The RE-GAIN programme will be implemented by AGRA as both the AE and the EE.
AGRA will leverage its extensive network of country offices and partnerships across seven
African countries to coordinate programme execution. A dedicated programme implementation
unit within AGRA will oversee programme activities, financial management and compliance
with GCF policies and standards. The unit will oversee planning and quality assurance;
supervise programme monitoring, evaluation and reporting; ensure timely realization of all
programme deliverables; provide leadership and technical support to implementing partners;
and ensure smooth communication flow across all programme partners. This executing role will
be fulfilled through both the Nairobi-based HQ and AGRA’s country offices, and the EE will
rep ort to the AGRA senior leadership team.
126.
A central programme steering committee, co-chaired by AGRA and representatives of
the national designated authority, will provide strategic guidance and review project
performance annually. The committee will ensure that programme activities are consistent with
GCF objectives and national development goals, reinforcing accountability and governance
mec hanisms.
127.
AGRA will establish a robust financial management framework, utilizing its internal
financial systems to segregate and track GCF funds. Separate project accounts will be
maintained for accurate financial reporting. AGRA’s financial management system aligns with
international standards, ensuring compliance with GCF fiduciary requirements.

128.
Periodic financial reports will be submitted to the GCF, detailing expenditures and
progress against budgeted activities. External audits will be conducted annually by an
independent auditor to validate financial reports and ensure adherence to established financial
controls. AGRA will also provide capacity-building support to local partners on financial
man agement and reporting, to uphold accountability at all implementation levels.
129.
Procurement activities under the RE-GAIN programme will adhere to AGRA’s
procurement policy, which emphasizes transparency, competitiveness and value for money.
Procurement plans will be developed annually, specifying timelines and mechanisms for
acquiring goods and services. The programme will prioritize local procurement to support
regional markets, with AGRA’s country offices ensuring compliance with national and
international procurement standards.
4.5 Results monitoring and reporting
130.
As detailed in the theory of change, three interconnected programme outcomes will
contribute to achieving longer-term adaptation outcomes for food and nutrition security and
livelihood resilience. The mitigation impact expected from the programme is classified as a co-
benefit. The theory of change has appropriately articulated the goal statement to focus on the
adoption of food loss-reduction technologies, improving the resilience of farmers, MSMEs and
rur al communities to climate shocks.
131.
The programme’s logical framework follows the GCF Integrated Results Management
Framework and will measure adequate core and supplementary indicators mapped to two GCF
result areas: livelihoods of people and communities; and health, food and water security. The
enabling environment and paradigm shift potential indicators selected by the AE are consistent
with the requirements and allow for comprehensive results monitoring. The project-specific
indicators deal with context-specific results from the output level, ensuring that programme
performance is adequately measured. The measurement of a few of the programme-specific
indicators will be informed by the baseline survey to be undertaken by the AE at the inception
stag e.
132.
The monitoring and evaluation of the programme will leverage the AE’s existing M&E
framework and data management systems. The monitoring and evaluation plan submitted as
part of the funding proposal package describes how the measurement and reporting against the
logical framework will be conducted. The implementation monitoring will be undertaken at the
country level, which will be aggregated at the programme level to prepare the annual
performance report in accordance with the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework. The
programme will be commissioning an interim and final evaluation through independent
evaluators in line with the GCF Evaluation Policy.
4.6 Legal assessment
133.
This funding proposal is being presented under the PSAA. If the funding proposal is
approved by the Board, the GCF Secretariat and the applicant will negotiate a funded activity
agr eement for the proposed programme.
134.
The applicant has provided a legal certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal
app rovals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.
135.
The proposed programme will be implemented in seven host countries. The GCF has
signed bilateral agreements on privileges and immunities with two of these host countries,
namely Burkina Faso and Zambia. In the case of the remaining five countries, namely Ethiopia,
Kenya, Malawi, Uganda and United Republic of Tanzania, GCF is not provided with privileges

=== PAGE 130 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 130
and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or
expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability
of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in such countries may be hindered
due to the absence of privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.
136.
Therefore, it is recommended that the Board consider whether disbursements of GCF
proceeds should be made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation
and expropriation in the countries, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and
imm unities for GCF and its personnel.
137.
To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:
(a)
Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the
GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
(b)
Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of

Proposal name: RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa
