I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1.
This funding proposal for a medium-sized, public sector adaptation programme in
environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by AGRA through the GCF project-
specific assessment approach (PSAA). The RE-GAIN programme will operate over five years in
seven African countries: Burkina Faso and six contiguous countries – Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi,
Uganda, the United Republic of Tanzania and Zambia (see figure 1). AGRA, a continental
institution in agriculture and food systems, will be both the PSAA entity and the executing entity
for the programme, which will be executed through the AGRA Programme Operational Unit and
utilize the AGRA Africa Food Systems Forum and Agribusiness Deal Room, allowing close
wFiogurkrein 1g Prreiloartiiotyn cslhimipas twe icthha gnogvee-vrunlmneernatbs,l ep arertgnioenrss aanndd otatrhgeert sctraokpesh foolrd tehres .seven participating
countries1
2.
The total cost of the programme is USD 105 million, of which USD 75.6 million is
proposed as GCF grant finance and USD 29.4 million as AGRA co-finance. The programme goal is
“to build smallholder climate resilience and adaptive capacity by promoting the wide scale
1
Figure 10 and table 4 from the funding proposal.

=== PAGE 132 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 132
2
adoption of food loss reduction solutions (FL-RS) in the seven participating countries” . The
programme will work by increasing demand and absorption capacity for FL-RS by smallholder
farmers, enhancing supply and affordability of the solutions, and strengthening enabling
environments for scaling them up. This will result in reducing exposure and vulnerability of key
grain and legume crops that are grown by smallholders and that are strategic for food security,
in regions identified as vulnerable to climate hazards – especially unpredictable rainfall with
heavy downpours, high temperatures and flooding (with drought of secondary importance).
These hazards are already increasing with climate change and are predicted to intensify,
worsening food losses during harvesting, post-harvest handling and storage, and processing,
transport and logistics – through mould, rotting, decay, heat damage, pests, rodents and
af la/mycotoxin contamination.
3.
The programme outputs and intended outcomes, under three components, are as shown
iTna tbaleb l1e P1r. ogramme structure for RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa
Abbreviation
: MSMEs = micro, small and medium enterprises.
4.
In addition to extensive work in Component 1 on awareness and training for
smallholders and value chain actors to build demand, as well as support to governments in
Component 3 to enhance enabling environments, the biggest proportion of the budget (54 per
cent of GCF funds) will go to Component 2 to develop the supply side for technical solutions to
food loss. Output 2.1 will build partnerships for market linkages and provide training to
suppliers and service providers. At the heart of the programme is Output 2.2, in which
temporary financial mechanisms will be put in place to support smallholders and MSMEs to
adopt FL-RS. According to the detailed programme budget (annex 4 to the funding proposal), a
total of USD 20.42 million of GCF funds will be used to subsidize tarpaulins, plastic sheets, metal
and plastic silos, and hermetic bags for smallholders farmers to protect harvested crops (in
Activity 2.2.1), and USD 12.34 million from GCF will be used to subsidize larger items for which
service providers take loans – mechanical multi-crop threshers, moisture meters, communal
storage structures, storage protectants and control agents (in Activity 2.2.2). This is to be
achieved through two innovative business and financial models, which can be summarized as
follows:
2
This wording on the project objective is from Slide 5 of the PowerPoint slides presented to iTAP on the funding
proposal by the Secretariat on 2 December 2024, and is aligned with the content of paragraph 49 of the funding
proposal (which does not include an explicitly stated single-sentence project goal or objective. overall project
objective).

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 133
Model 1
(a)
will promote the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional
procurements to effectively subsidize interventions at smallholder farmer level,
facilitated by 5,350 village-based advisers across the 35 priority regions. This model will
enable agrodealers to provide FL-RS to approximately 540,000 smallholder farmers at a
discounted rate, with GCF funds used to procure one item for every two items procured
and sold by an agrodealer. The subsidy will be passed down to the smallholder farmers
as a discount of 33 per cent on the purchase price (see paragraphs 95–105 of the
funding proposal for a detailed explanation of the carefully designed model, including
use of an escrow account to manage risk), also explained by the PSAA entity as a “Buy
3
two, get one free” promotional offer. The aim of this model is to make these
interventions more affordable for farmers, and to stimulate production and
m
M
a
o
n
d
u
e
f
l
a
2
cturer capacity by placing pre-emptive orders.
4
(b)
will match MSMEs and cooperatives with suppliers and financial institutions,
facilitating the signing of multi-stakeholder agreements to allow the MSMEs to purchase
more expensive equipment for post-harvest handling (such as solar-powered multi-crop
threshers and shellers, moisture meters, and communal storage structures). The MSMEs
will become service providers, operating the equipment on a fee-for-service basis with
smallholder customers. Under each agreement, AGRA will commit to an upfront co-
payment of 30 per cent of the purchase price, with the MSME taking a loan from the
financial institution to pay the other 70 per cent out of its service fee earnings. In the
event of a successfully completed loan repayment, the equipment is owned by the
MSME. If the MSME defaults, the supplier is obliged in terms of the agreement to buy
back the equipment, accounting for depreciation. The establishment of the service
operations will be facilitated through the business development support under Output
2.1. The aim of this model is to reduce borrowing costs to MSME service providers,
through subsidies on the purchase price, and to reduce credit risk to local financial
institutions.
5.
The subsidy mechanisms have been carefully designed to achieve these effects, ensuring
that the benefits of the subsidies do reach the targeted smallholder farmers without leakages,
that systems are in place to ensure agrodealers’ compliance with subsidy conditions, and that
contingencies are in place to deal with market price fluctuations or supply chain disruptions. In
5
response to a query from iTAP about the possibility of renting-to-own, AGRA confirmed that
variations on the model will be possible, using the equipment as collateral for the loans (asset
finance, buy-back guarantees by the suppliers, rent-to-own, etc.) – to be specified in the four-
way agreement. The programme has already engaged the following financial partners:
AgLeaseCo in Zambia, Equity for Africa (EFTA in Tanzania and EF-KEN in Kenya), and Coris
Bank in Burkina Faso, the last of which AGRA has previously implemented an equipment finance
scheme.
Scale: High
1 .1 Impact potential
6.
The programme will target two of the GCF results areas for adaptation, aiming to
enhance the resilience of (a) the most vulnerable people and communities and (b) health and
well-being, and food and water security, in the seven participating countries.

Responses received by the iTAP on 6 January 2025 from AGRA to questions it received from the iTAP on 18

=== PAGE 134 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 134
Climate change.
7.
The funding proposal highlights the vulnerability of all seven countries
to climate change, with negative impacts experienced and anticipated – not only on crop
production and yields but also on volumes of crops that remain of sufficient quality for
consumption or sale at harvest and beyond. Historical trends are shown, based on data from the
Wo°rld Bank Climate Change Knowledge Portal, with temperature indicators (such as annual
mean surface temperature and the number of days with extreme heat – i.e. temperatures over
35 C) displaying an increasing trend over time, and the rate of increase accelerating in recent
decades. Historical rainfall trends display far greater variability, and for some countries a
measure of uncertainty regarding the direction of the trend. The perceived historical increase in
th e frequency of intense rainfall events is not shown as a key climate variable.
8.
Data from the same portal, using two future emissions scenarios, indicate that future
projections of rainfall (such as annual mean precipitation, the number of consecutive wet days,
and – as a corollary – the number of consecutive dry days) suggest a projected rise in rainfall
levels and rainfall days across most of East Africa, likely to increase flooding. The exception is
Zambia, where projections display a slight drying signal, consistent with regional trends for
southern Africa. Continued trends of increasing variability in rainfall patterns are expected, as
are increasing heat extremes. AGRA acknowledges that the impact of climate change on post-
harvest food loss has not been well studied, either in terms of observed or projected impacts,
but suggests that “[o]f the range of climate hazards that affect harvest and post-harvest value
chains of cereal crops and legumes, scholarly publications and country experts pointed to
variable, erratic, and unpredictable rainfall (including an increase in heavy rainfall events), high
te mpera

t

), and flooding as the priority hazards of concern”.
9.
The programme aims to yield significant adaptation benefits for
vulnerable smallholder farmers and their households across the seven countries, through
reducing climate change-induced food losses. This will enhance farming communities’ resilience
to climate change by improving management at harvest and post-harvest stages, maintaining
higher levels of food availability for consumption and sale than would be the case without the
GCF–AGRA investment. The intended impacts of the programme are considered complementary
to the broader adaptation strategies for agricultural resilience within each target country, which
tend to be focused more on primary production. The programme will target vulnerable
members of society, aiming to increase the resilience of rural livelihoods through investing in
developing local markets and building up farmer cooperatives, youth and women’s groups, and
MSMEs, leveraging partnerships with financial institutions and private sector actors to mobilize
sig nificant investment beyond the proposed programme.
10.
Through these results, the programme aspires to directly benefit over 2.6 million people
and indirectly benefit a further 2.7 million people, distributed across the seven target countries
as shown in table 7 of the funding proposal (see methodology and detailed calculations in
supplementary annex 1 to the funding proposal). The 35 priority regions, shown in detail in
maps in annex 16, were selected on the basis of crop profiles and vulnerability to identified
climate hazards. Smallholder farmers adopting new climate-resilient practices with FL-RS will
be direct beneficiaries, experiencing improved food and nutrition security as well as increased
food quality and market connections that improve their incomes. MSMEs and agrodealers will
experience improved livelihoods as a result of increased business through the programme
subsidy and stimulation of demand. The indirect beneficiaries include local buyers, whose
access to affordable and good-quality grains and legumes will improve as a result of the
programme interventions, and market players such as suppliers and processors, who will
be nefit from improved market linkages.
11.
At GCF outcome level (see funding proposal section E.3), the programme will measure
the number of beneficiaries reached, the number of farmers adopting innovations that
strengthen climate change resilience, the number of people with new climate-resilient
livelihood options, and the number of vulnerable farm households with improved food security,

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 135
using farmer surveys employing the Food Insecurity Experience Scale. Since the innovations
and livelihood options in question are predefined and relate to specific FL-RS, these key
indicators should deliver credible tracking of results. Although food losses before and after
programme interventions will not be quantified, a co-benefit indicator of the percentage of
produce meeting quality standards will measure the percentage of grain rejected due to quality
6
concerns associated with afla/mycotoxins, with the aim being to halve the rejection rate.
Overall, it can be anticipated that food loss reductions will be significant, at least during the
programme implementation period (i.e. while the subsidies are in operation), improving
resilience through better nutrition, greater income from crop sales and enhanced business
inc omes along the value chains for technical solutions.
12.
Overall, impact potential is rated as high.
Scale: Medium
1. 2 Paradigm shift potential
13.
Paradigm shift relates to the degree to which the proposed programme can successfully
catalyse impact beyond the GCF one-off investment of USD 75.6 million over five years, together
with the AGRA co-finance. The iTAP recognizes the hard work and careful thought that have
gone into designing the programme in a manner that intends to elicit a paradigm shift in the
harvest and post-harvest development pathways for the seven target countries and
continentally in Africa. A clear focus on achieving scale and sustainability is embedded in the
programme design, both through the comprehensive value chain approach to removing barriers
to scaling of FL-RS and through the work with governments on country-level enabling
environments in Component 3. The design of the business and financial models, however,
relying as it does on a large subsidy that will be abruptly withdrawn at programme end, does
not seem optimal for achieving sustained demand or long-term market transformation, as
dis cusse

nment for scaling up.
14.
Component 3 of the programme seeks to create
an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS. This will involve working with governments
on new policies, such as stimulating tax exemptions, subsidies or reduced interest rates for FL-
RS investments. RE-GAIN will provide technical assistance to local and national government
entities to build their institutional capacity for scaling FL-RS through partnerships, platforms,
and support on tools, guidelines and monitoring. RE-GAIN will also work with governments and
other development partners to promote additional private sector investment in post-harvest
infrastructure, including storage facilities, processing units and cold chains, as well as helping
regulatory bodies to develop and implement quality standards and certification processes for
FL-RS products and services. Support will also be provided to enhance the capacities of
extension services and climate advisory services to monitor food loss in relation to climate
conditions and the impact of FL-RS in reducing loss. Although the RE-GAIN investments aim to
prolong grain shelf life in the absence of good rural road access and renewable-powered cooling
systems, engaging with wider government adaptation and development initiatives will enable

17.
The funding proposal states: “Long-term sustainability is at
the core of the programme design, with GCF investments seen as catalysers of change within
local systems. A key aspect of this GCF intervention is having an exit strategy through the
creation of an enabling environment to shift away from the smart-subsidies, catalysing further
investments in FL-RS under Component 2” (paragraph 166). Once the programme ends,
however, there will be nothing in place to manage this transition away from the subsidy. Prices
are likely to go up at least 30 per cent as soon as the subsidy is removed, which may lead to a
fall-off in purchasing and thus lower adoption rates for the FL-RS. The iTAP wonders whether a
subsidy that was smaller, more gradually phased out, or both, would be less disruptive, and
enable continued growth in sales and loans. In sharing examples of successful market
9
stimulation , AGRA made no mention of other similar price subsidy programmes. Arguably,

1. 3 Sustainable development potential
21.
The programme is expected to have a number of social, economic and environmental co-
be nefits

d health co-benefits.
22.
In targeting highly vulnerable smallholder
farming communities, the proposed programme will yield multiple socioeconomic co-benefits.
Reducing food loss can help stabilize food prices by ensuring a more consistent supply, helping
to protect both producers and consumers from price volatility. Improved post-harvest
processes that reduce food waste will improve the quality of food reaching consumers,
enhancing the nutritional value and reducing the risk of food-related illnesses resulting from
contamination by pests, moulds, aflatoxins and other pathogens. Not only will this benefit
individuals but also a more stable and safe food supply will reduce public health costs.Working
with local suppliers and agrodealers, and supporting business development of MSMEs as service
providers, the proposed programme will generate employment opportunities that should
extend beyond the initial investment, with the intention for these opportunities to be of
pa rticul

uch as women and youth.
23.
On the customer side, the fee-for-service business
model of the programme has significant potential for breaking down barriers to access by
women farmers to climate adaptation technology. This model allows small-scale farmers,
including female-headed households, to rent equipment, labour for services, or both, that they
would otherwise find difficult to access. An interesting example is provided by previous
12
programmes, where AGRA has tested and proven the deployment of “Uber for tractors” digital
solutions, resulting in more women getting access to mechanization services. The digital service
requests are anonymous, and the service provider deploys the equipment on a first-come, first-
ser ved basis, so cannot discriminate against female requesters.
24.
On the operator side, although AGRA hopes to prioritize women- and youth-led
enterprises in supporting 2,000 MSMEs and cooperatives to take loans and start fee-for-service
businesses, inclusivity is more challenging, given the (reasonable) eligibility criteria set out in
annex 2 of the funding proposal. The qualifying groups must have a registration certificate and
constitution, preferably a documentary track record as a service provider (in extension,
aggregation of produce, selling of inputs or provision of mechanized services), and preferably
qualified staff or members that have experience in operating, repairing and servicing the
machinery. Given that, in many of the rural communities to be engaged in the programme, these
are customarily seen as men’s areas of business, it seems unlikely that there will be many
women-led businesses or cooperatives that will have sufficient baseline experience to qualify.
The funding proposal does not suggest any small steps towards engagement of women’s
cooperatives – for example, in providing auxiliary services, such as bookkeeping or keeping
machinery clean and oiled. Such options could be explored through Activity 1.1.3 (on training
for complementary services).

25.
On youth groups, however, AGRA has suggested an innovative sub-model which takes
into consideration that the eligibility criteria will in practice likely also exclude most youth
groups (despite the characterization of the target MSMEs and cooperatives in the funding
proposal, somewhat confusingly, as “youth groups”). RE-GAIN will work with formalized MSMEs
to partner with local youth groups. The established MSME will take the lead in entering into the
multi-stakeholder agreement (including taking a loan to acquire machinery) and taking
accountability for the management of the agreement. The MSME will then recruit and work with
an informal youth group from the community to operate the equipment, sharing the income and
in the process developing the group’s capacities. Through this MSME-supervised approach, the
informal youth groups will have the opportunity to build a track record, demonstrating their

26.
The RE-GAIN programme interventions to reduce post-
harvest food loss will have some environmental co-benefits. Reducing post-harvest food loss
will increase the net productivity of agricultural land, improving the efficiency of land and water
use and preventing their wasteful use in producing food that is never eaten. The need to
compensate for food loss can be a driver of agricultural expansion, and AGRA posits this as a
major environmental co-benefit – avoiding land degradation and biodiversity loss (also used in
estimating emissions avoided). In the view of the iTAP, however, increased market access and
improved grain quality might in fact cause some farmers to expand the land under production
to take advantage of additional income-generating opportunities, potentially causing
deforestation. So this co-benefit is not a given, and there is nothing in place to test these
assumptions about non-expansion. The same applies to the argument that avoiding “regrowing
13
of lost food” will reduce the environmental impact of fertilizers and pesticides, and related
energy demands. A clear benefit is the reduction in air pollutants from burning of waste food,

27.
GHG emission reductions, as a co-benefit of the programme, will
be achieved through three pathways (with the emphasis on the first two in funding proposal
section D.3): (a) reduction of emissions arising from the production of compensatory food (i.e.
food produced to compensate for food lost in harvest and post-harvest without considering land
expansion); (b) reduction of food loss as a driver of land-use change linked to compensatory
agricultural expansion; and (c) reduction of emissions from wasted food left to rot, causing
methane and carbon dioxide emissions. Combined, it is estimated that these pathways could

about the accuracy of the assumptions on avoided agricultural expansion, which could

28.
The iTAP asked why AGRA chose not to utilize the most
obvious choice for estimating emissions reductions, i.e. the Verra VM0046 Methodology for
Reducing Food Loss and Waste, choosing instead to base the estimations on a single publication,
and queried whether the resultant estimates could be considered as suitably conservative. In a
14
written response, AGRA explained that this methodology was initially considered but that it
can only be effective in data-rich environments, which are not present in the programme
context of informal and smallholder value chains, while gathering new data would be a big
challenge in 14 value chains (two crops in each of the seven countries). The response explained:
“In light of these constraints and considerations, the team went back to the first principles and
developed an alternative approach that would make optimal use of the data available. The
calculations are considered con servative in that they: (i) were focused only on those portions of
13
See paragraph 183 of the funding proposal

the crop value of interest to the programme; (ii) did not consider additional emissions (such as
those related to transport or storage/packing materials); (iii) made use of estimates for food
loss supported by published studies that represent the average rates of loss, rather than very
low or very high losses; and (iv) made use of emissions factors derived from a robust global-
lev el study.”
29.
In the view of the iTAP, having the anticipated GHG emissions reductions as a co-benefit
justifies using a simplified approach to calculating their anticipated values. However, using
average figures and not estimates at the lower end, and using only one literature source, is not
deemed to constitute a conservative calculation approach. As a result, the figures given for the
proposed programme’s anticipated mitigation results could overstate the actual impact that can
be achieved.
30.
Sustainable development potential is rated as medium to high.
Scale: High
1. 4 Needs of the recipient
31.
RE-GAIN will target seven African countries, six of which are classified as least
developed countries and all of which are recognized as being highly vulnerable to climate
change. Of particular concern is the highly affected agriculture sector, which employs between
40 and 80 per cent of the total population and contributes between 20 and 40 per cent of gross
domestic product across the target countries. In addition to their sensitivity to climate impacts,
all seven of these target countries are recognized as having low adaptive capacity and low
15
readiness to adapt, which highlights their need for support. Most of these countries have a
high level of sovereign indebtedness, and need grant funding to help address the challenges
faced by poor, small-scale farming households, which are frequently highly exposed and
vu lnerable to negative impacts of climate change.
32.
Climate change-driven (or -exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-
production stages of agricultural value chains compound productivity and yield losses already
16
being experienced by these countries during cultivation, as a result of the changing climate.
This results in a double blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising
temperatures, higher air moisture levels, more frequent or intense heavy rainfall events, and
more frequent flooding. Increasing post-harvest losses with climate change reduce the output
that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize smallholder livelihoods,
perpetuating vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses as well as prolonging the cycle of
persistent poverty. Farmers experiencing increasing losses during harvesting, field-drying and
on-farm storage may choose to keep less of their reduced output for domestic consumption,
worsening household food insecurity in countries that already have markedly high levels of
17
undernourishment . All of these reduce adaptive capacity and must be ameliorated to ensure
the resilience of smallholder farmers in the seven countries.
15
According to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) Index for 2021, where “adaptive capacity” is
the ability of society and its supporting sectors to adjust to reduce potential damage and to respond to the negative
consequences of climate events, and “readiness” refers to the ability to make quick, effective use of investments for
adaptation.
16
In response to an iTAP question about whether these losses in the target countries have been quantified, the PSAA
entity explained (in responses received by the iTAP on 6 January 2025 from AGRA to questions it received from the
iTAP on 18 December 2024): “There is currently an absence of robust quantitative/quantified evidence on the
current or projected impact of climate change on post-harvest food loss. The climate change linkage in the post-
harvest value chain stages is increasingly being investigated, but the body of knowledge on this is relatively
nascent and has yet to yield a modeled estimate or quantification that is tied directly to climate projections. For
example in Chapter 5 on Food Security of the 2019 IPCC Special Report on Climate Change and Land
https://www.ipcc.ch/site/assets/uploads/sites/4/2022/11/SRCCL_Chapter_5.pdf.”
17
See funding proposal paragraph 27

=== PAGE 140 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 140
33.
Given the high levels of poverty, weak markets and low adaptive capacity experienced in
smallholder farming communities, farmers are frequently unable to adopt climate-resilient FL-
RS without initial external support. Moreover, the national governments of each country, while
prioritizing climate-resilient agriculture development, lack the financial, institutional and
technical capacity to establish sustainable markets for FL-RS, with the majority of the limited
resources being directed towards production. The RE-GAIN programme would target this
vulnerable stage of the value chain, which, despite the urgent need, has remained relatively
underdeveloped at the global scale – particularly when considering smallholder farmers. By
targeting local suppliers and agrodealers, while also supporting the business development of
MSMEs as service providers in the FL-RS value chain, RE-GAIN will build resilience directly
within the target populations, integrating the solutions into local systems that meet the
ind ividual needs of each community.
34.
The iTAP notes that specific country needs and measures to tailor the interventions to
each local context are provided in the country-specific feasibility studies appended to annex 2 of
the funding proposal.

The needs of the recipient are rated as high.
Scale: Medium to high
1. 5 Country ownership
36.
The proposed programme arose from discussions between AGRA and GCF, as well as
extensive engagements AGRA has conducted in each of the target countries during the
development of its five-year country strategies (2023–2027) – engagements resulting in a gap
analysis across the agriculture and food system sector of each country. As a continental
institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA has widespread infrastructure and
partnerships on the continent, with strong country teams in the target countries. Using its
continental forums, the Africa Food Systems Forum and the Agribusiness Deal Room, AGRA will
bring on board connections with governments, partners and other stakeholders to enhance
ownership and coordinate actors, knowledge and resources that are fundamental to take food

37.
AGRA co-developed the programme together with the
seven participating governments. Following initial consultations with the lead ministry
(agriculture) and GCF national designated authority for each country, draft concept notes were
shared to enable joint development of context-specific solutions. This was followed by multiple
local and national stakeholder workshops (detailed in annex 7 of the funding proposal), which
were combined with desktop research and experience in the target countries to establish a
baseline identifying technical, institutional and market gaps that need to be filled to build the
resilience of the target smallholder farmers. This process of stakeholder engagement will
continue throughout the programme’s implementation. At the country level, the programme
will be implemented under the overall guidance of a programme steering committee, which will
meet quarterly. Each such committee will be co-chaired by a representative of the national
designated authority and the AGRA country manager, with representatives of other key
government departments and agencies (ministries of agriculture and departments for land
resources conservation, crop development, agriculture extension services and agriculture

1. 6 Efficiency and effectiveness
44.
The PSAA applicant AGRA, whose permitted fiduciary functions for this GCF programme
do not include non-grant instruments, is seeking 100 per cent concessionality for the
programme. With a total budget of USD 105 million, AGRA is seeking USD 75.6 million, or 72 per
cent of the total, and would itself provide USD 29.4 million, or 28 per cent of the total, in grant
co -finance.
45.
The AGRA request for full concessionality is argued based on the nature of the
investments, which “require targeted capacity development support combined with strategic
conditional procurements to catalyse a paradigm shift in the post-harvest value chain using
innovative FL-RS in the seven target countries. The two key financial models – 1) catalytic
grants for smallholder farmers and 2) catalytic grants for agricultural MSMEs – use concessional
funding to incentivize private sector suppliers and financial institutions (FIs) to provide
physical solutions that are within the affordability envelope of farmers, while remaining
commercially viable to private sector suppliers; and to provide tailored financial products that
are affordable to cooperative and MSME service providers, while mitigating credit risk to

46.
In response to a query from iTAP about the risk of
crowding out existing private sector suppliers, AGRA responded that “all interested and eligible
suppliers (to be sourced through an open call following the eligibility criteria detailed in annex
2 of the funding proposal) will benefit from the subsidies to build and kick-start the market. The
programme seeks to build markets where they are non-existent and incentivizes all suppliers
26
that want to discover new markets and new client segments.” The programme addresses the
current market failure of FL-RS not being available or affordable in specific targeted
geographies or for specific segments because of a range of barriers related to information,
capacity, technology and finance. The intention is to grow the market and crowd in private
se ctor players by derisking the rollout of new technologies. RE-GAIN will thus:
(a)
De-risk first-time users investing in new technology that remains unproven in their
context. The “pay for two, get one free” type of investment will make sure the
concessional funding ends up in farmers’ pockets;
(b)
Reduce the cost of distributing FL-RS to rural areas where demand is not yet confirmed
(concessional funding will de-risk agrodealers and distributors from remaining with
unsold stock);
(c)
Reduce the risk for FL-RS service providers investing in equipment for which the
business case is not yet proven. The manufacturers will be paid, and the service

47.
AGRA expects the RE-GAIN programme’s combined
approach of addressing supply- and demand-side constraints while improving enabling
environments to mobilize USD 144.95 million worth of physical solutions over the next five
years, representing a catalytic effect or return of 1.38 times the original investment of USD 105
million by GCF and AGRA. This anticipated financial return on investment excludes the long-
term impact of the programme’s ability to demonstrate the market viability of FL-RS to both
users (farmers) and suppliers (manufacturers and financial institutions), which AGRA hopes
will catalyse new private sector markets while addressing food loss and associated climate-
rel ated challenges.
48.
AGRA undertook an economic analysis for the programme using a cost–benefit analysis
approach. Programme costs and benefits were assessed over a 10-year period and using
country-specific social discount rates as presented in annex 3 of the funding proposal. The
analysis weighed up the total programme costs against the projected benefits associated with
the uptake of equipment by smallholder farming households. These benefits included the
income gains associated with the value of food that would otherwise have been lost in the
absence of the programme interventions, the health benefits from reductions in aflatoxin
exposure, the improved nutritional status (particularly of infants) from avoided food losses, and
the climate benefits associated with reduced GHG emissions from food production. The results
of the economic analysis suggest that, under the current set of assumptions, the total package of
interventions is cost-beneficial, with the total programme investment generating an economic
net present value of USD 69 million, with a benefit-to-cost ratio of 8:1 and an economic internal
rate of return of around 56 per cent. On this basis, and with a sensitivity analysis conducted to
show that these statistics are robust to changes in input values, there is a clear economic case
for implementing the proposed programme.
49.
Efficiency and effectiveness are rated as medium to high.
II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel
50.
This proposed programme – RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa (brought
to GCF through the PSAA) – is expected to make a significant contribution in helping the
smallholder farming sector in Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, the United
Republic of Tanzania and Zambia to reduce their exposure and vulnerability to staple grain and
leg ume crop losses that are worsening as a result of intensifying climate change hazards.
51.
The iTAP retains a concern that the significant subsidies to be provided to reduce the
costs of food loss reduction solutions, derisking the scale-up of these solutions in rural markets,
might not have a long-lasting effect, given the subsidies’ large and short-term nature. There may
be the unintended consequence of a marked drop-off in demand following the subsidies’
withdrawal. Nonetheless, the iTAP believes that the programme can have a major impact during
the five years of its implementation alone, reducing the vulnerability and enhancing the
adaptive capacity of this highly affected sector in vulnerable priority regions of the participating
countries.

=== PAGE 144 ===
GCF/B.41/02/Add.05
Page 144
52.
The iTAP recommends that AGRA closely track the impacts of the subsidies intended to
stimulate market demand in the 35 priority regions of the seven countries – both the subsidy on
small-scale food loss reduction solutions being purchased by farmers, and the subsidy on larger
technologies for which MSMEs and cooperatives are taking loans in order to set up fee-for-
service businesses. Since AGRA will remain engaged in these countries during the programme
lifespan of 10 years, it should be feasible to use AGRA resources to track the development of
these markets in the five years following programme closure. Although attribution is complex,
and other factors will be at play, an evaluation can be made, based on this market tracking, of
the extent to which these temporary subsidies, together with the programme’s value chain and
en abling environment strengthening activities, have been able to create sustained demand.
53.
The iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

1 Introduction
1.1 PROGRAMME BACKGROUND
A great deal of attention has been paid in recent decades to the impacts of climate change on crop production, i.e., on growing
risks to agricultural productivity. Scholarly investigations and public and private research have invested heavily in identifying
and – where feasible – quantifying the ramifications of climate change on crop yields, yield stability over seasons, and in
exploring plausible management options for the emerging challenges (CGIAR, 2023). As governments and societies look at
how to minimize the risks of climate change, the impact of these changes on food production is increasing, fuelling concerns
about food security and livelihoods for current and future generations.
Food security, however, is affected not only by changes in crop production but by changes occurring throughout the crop
value chain, including during post-harvest phases (Akoth, 2020). It is therefore crucial to examine the impacts of climate
change on a crop’s value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, transportation, processing, and distribution. Each
stage comprises several sub-processes, and climate change may plausibly affect many or all of the sub-processes too.
With the lion’s share of research and resources for resilience interventions in the agricultural sector having been focused on
production, the RE-GAIN project is an effort to give dedicated focus to harvest and post-harvest stages of the value chain –
specifically, harvesting, post-harvesting handling and storage, processing, transportation, and logistics. As summarized in
Table 1-1, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) report highlights a range of climate change concerns in
the post-production stages of value chains and potential adaptation interventions that could increase resilience against such
climate change concerns (IFAD, 2015).
Table 1-1 - Illustrative climate change risks and climate change risk management interventions in post-production value chain processes (adapted from

AGRA is a continental institution working in 15 African countries addressing food systems focussing on smallholder farmers’
production, marketing and nutrition. In the countries where AGRA operates, which are highly diverse in terms of climate, soils,
crop choices and institutional capacity, neither all of these climate-related concerns may be applicable, nor all of these
potential interventions possible. Even within the range of what may be applicable, this programme is likely to look at a subset
of risks that may be viable to address, and – given resource constraints – only a limited number of high-priority resilience
interventions may be feasible to design and deploy. RE-GAIN is an effort to identify the most salient risks, select the most
impactful solutions, and implement the priority interventions through a well-structured, strategic, multi-country programme.
1.2 BRIEF PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION
There is a clear gap in knowledge, data and interventions designed to target the impacts of climate change at the harvest
and post-harvest stages of the value chain, despite the mounting evidence of the ramifications on food loss and the impact

=== PAGE 154 ===
this has on land use changes and associated climate change mitigation. The majority of the current programmes designed
to tackle climate-induced food loss focus on the pre-harvest stages of the value chain.
To address the pressing need for broader implementation of solutions aimed at reducing climate-related harvest and post-
harvest food loss, the proposed programme is designed to raise awareness and build capacity to promote the adoption of
Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS). It will do this by creating institutional capacity, facilitating the uptake of FL-RS by end
users and service providers, increasing options of solutions’ availability, and enabling practical application through policy
interventions. This will include enhanced financial access for farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs),
empowering them to invest in climate-friendly FL-RS and incentivising vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers of climate-
adapted FL-RS, fostering a robust market ecosystem.
A key focus is on strengthening the capabilities of countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest infrastructure, both
through providing physical solutions alongside capacity building along the value chains. This includes investing in strategic
frameworks and implementation plans, including a regulated quality-based pricing system and tax exemptions on imports,
for reducing food loss. By enhancing access to markets, the programme will encourage farmers to adopt FL-RS products and
services, thereby boosting their climate and economic resilience.
Target Countries Overview
During the 2023–2027 period, AGRA plans to target 28 million farmers across 15 Sub-Saharan African countries, 40% of
which will be women. The RE-GAIN Programme focuses on AGRA’s activities in seven target countries, as shown in Figure 1-1
below. The RE-GAIN Programme is designed to combat food loss during the post-harvest stages and to boost climate resilience
by fostering awareness and by building capacity for the adoption of Food Loss Reduction solutions (FL-RS). The programme
aims to transfer these solutions to end users and service providers for practical application while facilitating financial access
to farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) to invest in climate-resilient FL-RS. The programme plans to
incentivize vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers to adopt these solutions and enhance the capacity of countries to develop
climate-resilient post-harvest food handling infrastructure.
8 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

=== PAGE 155 ===
Figure 1-1 Focus Geographies for AGRA (2023-2027)
Crop selection
Key crops were identified by major stakeholders in the respective countries and expert assessments, supported by AGRA and
the National Designated Authority (NDA) of each target country. Two major crops per target country were selected, based on
area coverage, importance for food security and income, and climate vulnerability, to ensure that sufficient resources would
be available for the crafting and execution of targeted solutions. Selected crops are representative of the agricultural
dynamics of each country and aligned with the specific needs and strategic agricultural goals of the nation. In addition, these
crops hold substantial importance to the country’s food security and/or experience particularly high rates of loss within the
value chain. Finally, these crops are produced in large parts of the respective countries by a significant number of smallholder
farmers. The key crops, therefore, reflect the agronomic and economic realities of each country and provide opportunities for
targeted enhancement of food security and sustainable agricultural practices. Additionally, the improved management of
these crops is also expected to significantly reduction of GHG emissions contributing to the NDC targets of the countries
involved. Figure 1-2 highlights the key crops selected for each of the countries within the programme.

Harvesting and Post Harvesting Definition
For the RE-GAIN programme, the key value chain stages considered are shown in Figure 1-2.
Harvesting Post-harvest Processing, transportation
processes handling and storage and logistics
Including harvesting processes and Including threshing, cleaning, sorting,
Including packaging and distribution,
skills storage and primary processing
and impact on shelf life
Figure 1-2 Strategic value chain stages included in the RE-GAIN Programme
The harvesting process within this RE-GAIN Programme proposal is defined as the interval between the culmination of
agricultural production, marked by the crop reaching its maturity, and the initiation of post-harvest treatment. This process
encompasses the identification of the optimal harvesting time and is further delineated into four distinct stages:
1. Removal of contaminated seeds, heads or cobs of matured crops at harvest
2. Reaping, which involves cutting, pulling, or gathering the mature crops.
3. Threshing, the process of separating the grain from the rest of the plant.
4. Cleaning, such as winnowing, to remove chaff and other impurities.
5. Hauling, which entails the transportation of the harvested produce to storage or processing facilities.
The post-harvest handling and storage stage commences once the crop exits the field and is typically conducted on the farm1.
This stage encompasses several key operations, including:
1. Threshing, which can be performed manually or with mechanical threshing machines.
2. Drying, utilizing cribs, tarpaulins, and similar methods.
3. Cleaning and sorting, such as through winnowing, to remove impurities.
4. On-farm storage, which includes the use of granaries, hermetic bags, ordinary bags, stacks, metal silos, and plastic
silos.
5. In some instances, primary processing activities, such as grinding, hulling, pounding, milling, drying, and sieving,
are also conducted during this stage.
The processing, transportation, and logistics stage involves farmers selling their harvested crops either directly to traders,
who collect the produce from the farm, or to collection centres and processors. These market participants then undertake
the tasks of product accumulation, initial processing, quality control, grading, packaging, and transportation to wholesale
buyers.
1 In this instance, a field is where the crops are grown, and a farm consists of the whole small holding including the small
aggregation site.
10 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

=== PAGE 157 ===
1.3 REASONING FOR REQUESTED FUNDING
Africa's food insecurity challenge has been exacerbated by climate change. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with
an unprecedented opportunity for food systems transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5
billion and the pressures of a changing climate (World Bank, 2023) (Worldometer, n.d.). The continent faces significant
development challenges including food insecurity, resource degradation, poverty, gender inequality, and social exclusion. The
vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land
degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty.
The effects of climate change are expected to be severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing
climate is weak.
The impacts of climate change have increased over the past decades in Africa, manifesting in more frequent, intense, and
prolonged extreme weather events, such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, locust outbreaks, desertification, and sandstorms.
These extreme weather events have resulted in increased temperatures and humidity, shifts in precipitation patterns, water
stress, and soil erosion. Most African countries already face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often leading to
short growing periods reducing the viability of farming in marginal agricultural areas. Projected reductions in crop yields in
some countries could reach as much as 50% by 2030, and crop net revenues may fall by up to 90% by 2100, with smallholder
farmers being the most affected (IPCC, 2018).
Therefore, the RE-GAIN programme aims to enhance the climate resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholders by
promoting the widespread adoption of FL-RS in seven African countries. According to the World Bank estimates, a one percent
reduction in post-harvest losses in Sub-Saharan Africa could lead to economic gains of $40 million each year, and most of
the benefits would go directly to smallholder farmers (World Bank, 2011). Moreover, food loss and waste are the result of an
extremely inefficient use of resources and account for about 3.3 gigatonnes of greenhouse gas emissions globally (FAO,
2013). Large amounts of water and fertilizer also go into the production of food that never reaches human mouths.
Recovering the food that is lost during harvest and post-harvest handling some can help close that calorie gap in Africa while
strengthening livelihoods and improving food security— without imposing any additional environmental cost. Therefore,
facilitated by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) investment, RE-GAIN will roll out a suite of physical interventions alongside
capacity building and enhanced financial and market access. Not only will this benefit the respective countries as whole, but
it also has the potential to benefit the region and the wider planet.
1.4 PROGRAMME GOAL STATEMENT
IF the capacity of the target countries and communities to respond to climate-triggered food losses is strengthened through
improved and inclusive access to financing, promotion of context-specific and gender-responsive innovations to reduce food
losses, and better enabling conditions for public and private investments, THEN smallholder farmers will have enhanced food
security and livelihood resilience, BECAUSE the widespread use of food loss-reduction technologies will reduce food loss and
reduce the carbon footprint of food systems, while increasing household income and building the resilience of smallholder
farmers, MSMEs and rural communities to climate shocks.
11 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

=== PAGE 158 ===
1.5 PURPOSE AND STRUCTURE OF THE ANNEX
This Annex seeks to ensure that the RE-GAIN Programme is designed, financed, implemented and monitored in a gender-
responsive and socially inclusive manner. It has been developed in accordance with both the GCF’s Gender Policy, and its
Gender Assessment and Action Plan Templates. The Annex adopts the GCF’s three-pronged approach to mainstreaming
gender, by presenting a robust gender assessment that considers the various factors (including those exacerbated by climate
change) that influence gender inequality in each of RE-GAIN’s target countries’ agriculture sectors and identifies opportunities
for women, men and youth to contribute to inclusive resilience building within post-harvest value chains. These opportunities
are translated into gendered actions that promote gender equality and youth empowerment, and reduce disparities in the
sector, which are captured in a gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework (i.e. the Gender & Inclusion
Action Plan). This Annex is informed by a review of existing literature and secondary databases, extensive stakeholder
consultations and expert input.
This Annex sits within the context of the broader funding proposal and should be read as such.
• Chapter 2 provides an overview of the RE-GAIN programme’s three key components, outputs and outcomes. This framing
is particularly important when reviewing Chapter 5 of this Annex, given the need to ensure that all Gender and Inclusion
actions are appropriately embedded within the programme’s broader level of ambition and scope of delivery.
• Chapter 3 presents an overview of the status of gender equality in each of RE-GAIN’s seven countries. It focuses
specifically on two aspects of gender equality, which are of primary relevance to this project – namely, i) gender and
agriculture; and ii) gender and climate change. It also outlines the legal, policy and stakeholder landscape as it relates
to the intersection of gender, climate and agriculture in each country – providing an important framework within which
the Programme-specific gender interventions sit.
• Chapter 4 describes the approach, findings, and recommendations derived from Programme-specific stakeholder
engagement and literature review. It identifies preferences, notable barriers and potential opportunities to overcome
adoption of physical FL-RS, which in turn informs specific non-physical solutions that should accompany such support.
• Chapter 5 consolidates the recommendations from Chapter 4 into a Gender & Inclusion Action Plan, which contributes
to – and aligns with – the Programme’s broader Theory of Change.
• Chapter 6 concludes the Annex by outlining the overarching principles and approaches that will guide consistent, gender-
responsive and inclusive Programme delivery, all of which are aligned to AGRA’s existing institutional commitments and
protocols.

2 Programme Overview
The RE-GAIN programme is designed to address food loss in Africa through an integrated approach, taking into account the
growing need for climate adaptation in this space and the potential for mitigation of reducing food losses. The RE-GAIN
programme’s holistic approach to these growing challenges includes strengthening extension services, particularly as it
portrays to climate information, demand-side interventions, supply-side development, financial mechanisms, and creating
an enabling environment. Below is a comprehensive summary of the programme’s three components. Further information
on the programme design and process can be uncovered on the Feasibility Study (Annex 2) and the full Funding Proposal.
2.1 COMPONENT 1: FOOD LOSS-REDUCTION SOLUTIONS (FL-RS) DEMAND-SIDE
DEVELOPMENT
This component focuses on increasing the demand for and adoption of FL-RS among smallholder farmers to reduce post-
harvest food losses, enhance food security, and build resilience to climate change. The activities are centered around
raising awareness, providing training, and improving market linkages.
2.1.1 Output 1.1: Support to Smallholder Farmers:
o Awareness-Raising Campaigns: Targeted campaigns educate farmers about the impacts of climate change
on post-harvest losses and the benefits of adopting climate-resilient FL-RS.
o Training Programs through Extension Services: Farmers receive training on best practices for harvesting,
post-harvest handling, storage techniques, and the use of FL-RS. Training is tailored to local contexts and
emphasizes gender responsiveness and youth engagement.
o Demonstrations of FL-RS: Practical demonstrations showcase the effectiveness of FL-RS, helping farmers
understand and adopt these solutions in their local environments.
o Technical Assistance to Food Processors: Assistance is provided to facilitate a shift to whole-grain flour
production, which reduces food waste and improves profitability.
2.1.2 Output 1.2: Improved Market Linkages:
o Technical Assistance for Value Chain Structuring: The programme helps establish more structured
relationships between farmers, processors, and buyers, ensuring better quality compliance and reduced
post-harvest losses.
o Linkages with Institutional Markets: Farmers are connected to institutional markets like school feeding
programs, which provide reliable demand for high-quality produce, thereby encouraging the adoption of FL-
RS.
2.1.3 Outcome for Component 1
The main outcome of the component 1 is increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers.
2.2 COMPONENT 2: FL-RS SUPPLY-SIDE DEVELOPMENT
This component complements the first by improving the availability and affordability of FL-RS through support for local
businesses and the introduction of innovative financial mechanisms to stimulate supply of FL-RS and to improve the
13 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

=== PAGE 160 ===
affordability of physical FL-RS solutions that can improve climate resilience for smallholder farmers and micro, small, and
medium enterprises (MSMEs).
2.2.1 Output 2.1: Business Development Support for FL-RS Providers:
o Training for MSMEs and Cooperatives: Local MSMEs and cooperatives, especially those led by women and
youth, are trained to develop sustainable FL-RS service provision operations, with lease-to-own as an
example. This includes technical training on equipment use and business management.
o Market Intelligence and Networking: AGRA supports the development of market intelligence and networks
among local manufacturers, importers, and agro-dealers to boost the FL-RS market.
2.2.2 Output 2.2: Financial Mechanisms and Physical Solutions to Support the Adoption of FL-RS:
To stimulate supply and increase the affordability of FL-RS that can increase smallholder farmers and community-led
organisations’ climate resilience, the following physical FL-RS solutions have been selected given their impact on food loss
and its climate-impacted causes:
o Hermetic Bags, Metal and Plastic Silos, Tarpaulins, and Plastic Sheets: These are essential physical
solutions aimed at reducing post-harvest losses. They protect crops from pests, moisture, and
contamination, thus improving storage and prolonging the shelf life of produce.
o Harvesting Machinery and Mechanical Threshers/Shellers: These tools, including solar-powered options,
are designed to reduce labor intensity and improve the efficiency of harvesting and post-harvest
processing, minimizing losses due to improper handling.
o Moisture Meters and Storage Protectants: These devices help maintain optimal conditions for stored
crops, preventing spoilage and contamination. Moisture meters are crucial for monitoring and managing
crop moisture levels, which is vital in preventing mold growth and toxin production.
o Communal Storage Structures and Warehouses: These facilities offer shared storage solutions that help
smallholder farmers and aggregators store their produce in controlled environments, reducing the risk of
losses due to poor individual storage conditions.
To increase the affordability of these solutions in the market, the following financial models will be leveraged:
Model 1 promotes the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional procurements to effectively subsidise
interventions at smallholder farmer level (referred to hereafter as ‘smart-subsidies’). At its base, the smart-subsidy model
enables agro-dealers to provide FL-RS to smallholder farmers at a discounted rate by using GCF funds to procure one itemfor
every two items2 procured and sold by an agro-dealer. The subsidy is passed down to the smallholder farmers as a discount
on the purchase price3.
The interventions are typically focused on smallholder farmers, with lower individual ticket sizes to the end-users — i.e. for FL-
RS such as tarpaulins and plastic sheets, metal and plastic silos, and hermetic bags. The flow of funds will be facilitated
through a trust/escrow account at a local financial institution (FI) and released once proof of offtake by eligible smallholder
2 Note: the specific ratios will vary based on context of local markets in each country, with more developed markets
receiving a smaller subsidy than less developed markets. A three-for-two model has been used as a base for
demonstration purposes.
3 The maximum retail sales price and number of items per customer — as well as the eligibility criteria and geography
where the sales at reduced cost can be made — will be included as part of the agreement between RE-GAIN and the
suppliers as a condition for participation in the scheme.
14 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

=== PAGE 161 ===
farmers has been obtained. However, while funds will pass through an FI, it should be noted that no GCF funds will be
transferred to a financial institution as the end beneficiary. The aim of the model is two-fold:
• to stimulate production and manufacturer capacity by placing pre-emptive orders of FL-RS, however, maintaining control
over risk through a conditional release of funds to the manufacturer; and
• to reduce the cost of interventions at smallholder farmer level, additional profitability will drive additional demand and
facilitate knowledge sharing about the benefits of interventions.
The model has been designed with two variations to allow for administration of the model using the smart-subsidy alone, or
with the option of unlocking additional financing from partner financial institutions (FIs), with the latter offering greater
potential benefits at the cost of greater complexity.

Model 2 will use GCF grants to provide a unique approach to unlocking larger ticket items and food loss reducing equipment
to farmer collectives or small to medium sized enterprises. The term ‘youth groups’ is used in this model description to
highlight the priority given to this vulnerable demographic4; however, the model will also apply to other cooperatives, including

=== PAGE 162 ===
women’s groups and local MSMEs. The aim of Model 2 is to create multi-stakeholder agreements with suppliers, youth groups,
and financial institutions, with the goal of:
• Enhancing creditworthiness – through repurchase assurances from the suppliers that lower the loss given default.
• Reducing borrowing costs – Through a combination of the lowered credit risk (as per above) and subsidies on the
purchase price. The structure will ensure larger-ticket FL-RS become more affordable and thus accessible to youth
groups who provide services to smallholder farmers.
At the heart of Model 2 is the engagement of local youth groups that will be supported to act as service providers for FL-RS
that require more expensive equipment that can service multiple farmers — such as mechanical multi-crop threshers and
shellers (preferably solar-powered), moisture meters and communal storage structures. The establishment of the service
operations will be supported through the business development under Output 2.1, ensuring that the youth groups have the
necessary foundation to provide a reliable service. This approach leverages several key concepts to unlock the targeted
benefits:
• Collectivism provides benefits to smallholder farmers in economies of scale through cost sharing and increased
bargaining power with off-takers. These should promote further profitability and therefore additional demand for FL-
RS.
• Post-harvest handling will increase the quality and quantity of agricultural produce, allowing smallholder farmers to
capture more value, hence increased incomes.
The inclusion of financiers will further unlock access to finance in a typically underserved market. The structure aims to
reduce credit risk by providing a partial subsidy that will lead to lower borrowing costs (thanks to smaller loan size and interest
payments).

The main outcome of the component 2 is enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS.
2.3 COMPONENT 3: ENABLING ENVIRONMENT FOR FL-RS MARKET
SUSTAINABILITY
This component addresses policy-level constraints and builds institutional capacity to ensure the long-term sustainability
and scalability of the FL-RS market, ensuring the long-term sustainability of the RE-GAIN programme
2.3.1 Output 3.1: Enhanced Capacity of National Institutions:
o Policy Reform and Advocacy: AGRA collaborates with governments to reform policies that currently hinder
the adoption of FL-RS, including advocating for tax exemptions, reduced import duties, and the
establishment of quality standards. AGRA also supports national governments to formulate, enact and
implement new policies and regulations where gaps exist.
o Institutional Capacity Building: Technical assistance is provided to local and national government entities
to support the scaling of successful FL-RS models and policies. This includes strengthening the analytical
capacity of institutions to track and report on food loss and climate change metrics.
o Development of Business Cases for FL-RS Investments: Research is conducted, and evidence is gathered
to inform solid business cases for investing in FL-RS, which are then used to attract private sector
investments and promote successful business models for scaling and replication.
o Monitoring, Evaluation, and Learning (MEL) System: A comprehensive MEL framework is implemented to
track the progress of the programme, assess the impact of interventions, and ensure continuous
improvement and knowledge sharing.
2.3.2 Outcome of Component 3
The main outcome of the component 3 is strengthened enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS.

3 Country and sector-level analyses
This Chapter describes the status of gender equality in each of RE-GAIN’s seven countries. It presents an overview of the
progress, commitments, and impediments to closing gender gaps at a national scale, and then presents a high-level summary
of two aspects of gender equality that are of primary relevance to this programme – namely, i) gender and agriculture; and ii)
the impacts of climate change on gender equality. This follows with an outline of the legal, policy and stakeholder landscape
as it relates to the intersection of gender, climate and agriculture in each country – providing an important framework within
which programme-specific gender interventions will sit.
3.1 KENYA
3.1.1 National overview: status of gender equality
Kenya is a committed signatory to numerous international agreements that promote gender equality, ensuring the protection
and promotion of women’s rights. It ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women
(CEDAW) in 1984, and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPfA) in 1995 (UN Women Africa, 2023). Additionally,
Kenya is a participant in the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), specifically SDG 5, which aims to achieve gender
equality and empower all women and girls. Through these treaties and others, such as the Maputo Protocol (the Protocol to
the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa), Kenya has integrated international
gender standards into its national legal and policy framework, working towards the promotion of women's rights and gender
parity (UN Women Africa, 2023).
Box 1 Kenya’s National Gender Legislation
Kenya has made significant progress in advancing gender equality, through the enactment of laws, regulations and policies focussed on
increasing women’s participation in political spheres, their access to finance, and labour force participation (UN Women Africa, 2023). The
Constitution incorporates international treaties including CEDAW and BPfA, ensuring equality is embedded in national law with Article 27
guaranteeing equal treatment and opportunities for men and women. This has been complemented by gender-responsive budgeting efforts,
where the Kenyan government is considered at the fore of pioneering and implementing such approaches at a federal level (WEF, 2024).
Further to this, there a number of gender-sensitive policies including the National Land Policy (2009), which recognizes women’s equal
rights to own property alongside men. Other important policies include the National Policy for the Prevention and Response to Gender-
Based Violence (2014) and the National Policy for the Abandonment of Female Genital Mutilation (2019) (UN Women Africa, 2023).
Kenya is ranked above regional averages for the World Bank’s Women, Business and the Law (WBL) Index and the World
Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI). With a WBL score of 83.8/100, Kenya attained a perfect score for
mobility, workplace pay and marriage indicators. Its primary areas for improvement relate to entrepreneurship and
parenthood, where there remains a need to enact legislature that supports women’s ability to start and run a business, and
provide paid parental leave to allow women to more readily return to the workforce after having a child (World Bank, 2023).
This score has increased in recent years, due to reforms that resulted in more stringent laws and policies on domestic
violence, prohibition of gender discrimination in employment, and equal remuneration for work of equal value. Contrary to
this, Kenya’s GGGI rank of 75 out of 146 countries dropped by 2 places between 2023 and 2024 predominantly because of
its subindex score for educational attainment, where Kenya ranks in the bottom 15 worldwide. Despite this, Kenya still ranks

Barriers to effective implementation of policies, legislature and commitments
The differences in measures of progress between the WBL and GGGI can largely be attributed to several key factors, including
inadequate implementation of laws, insufficient financing for gender equality measures, poor accountability mechanisms and
overriding patriarchal gender norms, all of which have hampered tangible advancements in closing certain gender gaps (UN
Women Africa, 2023). In particular, religious and customary laws, attitudes and practices are still considered key
impediments to women’s empowerment, as illustrated by data from the Kenya National Bureau of Statistics (KNBS) which
indicated that only 29% of women were considered to be “empowered” in 2020 (Afrobarometer, 2023) (UN Women Africa,
2023). Notions around this concept of empowerment are linked to several important differences between women and men,
with notable gaps in higher education, asset ownership, and financial autonomy, where women trail behind men in each of
these areas (Afrobarometer, 2023). As a result, young women in rural areas are a particularly vulnerable group in Kenya.
Limited control over, use of and benefit from productive activities, poor access to land markets and other resources, exposure
to gender-based violence and the demands of unpaid care work collectively hinder a woman’s ability to participate in the
economy and perpetuate a cycle of overreliance on men (USAID, 2022). These issues are all pertinent within the context of
this Green Climate Fund application. The following subsections therefore explore them in more depth, through the lens of
agriculture and climate change.
Women in agriculture
Agriculture is the largest source of income for rural households in Kenya, and women account for 75 percent of the labour
force in the sector. This means that, in addition to their reproductive roles relating to water, energy and food security as well
as household health and care, they undertake the bulk of agricultural work for home consumption and sale (UNDP, 2020)
(IISD, 2018). Despite this, women earn significantly less than male counterparts, and operate largely in the informal
agricultural sector (with only 34 percent of women working in the formal sector), and they own a nominal percent of assets –
including land. Cultural norms dictate that land is inherited by first-born males, meaning that women generally conduct their
agricultural activities on the lands of men and are therefore beholden to the man’s decisions on use of income and activities.
With women owning as little as 1.62% of agricultural land, and land being an important form of collateral, they face major
difficulty in accessing credit (UNDP, 2020). Unfortunately, to date, national climate and environment plans do not place
explicit focus on securing women’s land ownership rights (IISD, 2018). Women also lack access to information, technologies,
extension support, financial services and marketing support – all of which are critical to building resilience and scaling up
agricultural operations (Box 2 highlights several additional barriers specific to Kenya’s seed system). Collectively, these
differences between women and men farmers have resulted in as much as a 30 percent yield gap between male and female
managed agricultural enterprises. And poverty is particularly pervasive among low-income earning women and female headed
households (UNDP, 2020).
Box 2 Snapshot of findings from AGRA’s Gender Gap Analysis in Kenya’s National Seed System
AGRA’s deep dive into the gendered differences in Kenya’s National Seed System was conducted using the SeedSAT. It highlights areas for
improvement, requiring effort by both the government and private sector actors – for example (AGRA, 2022):
• There is a need to more explicitly promote gender equality, and the inclusion of women and youth, in national seed systems through
reforms to policy, legislature and regulation. Such reforms should be informed by greater engagement with seed producers – focussing
specifically on small and micro producer units owned and managed by women.
19 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

--- Table 1 on Page 165 ---
Box 2 Snapshot of findings from AGRA’s Gender Gap Analysis in Kenya’s National Seed System

improvement, requiring effort by both the government and private sector actors – for example (AGRA, 2022):
• There is a need to more explicitly promote gender equality, and the inclusion of women and youth, in national seed systems through

• Women and youth’s accessibility of the government’s online and mobile-based platforms used by extension officers (i-Kilimo, National
Farmers Information Service (NAFIS) and Plant wise) needs to be improved.
• Data advancements are necessary to ensure that i) seed data is shared widely and accessibly, using gender responsive tools; and ii)
seed system data must be gender disaggregated where appropriate.
Climate change and gender equality
Both Kenya’s Updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and National Adaptation Plan 2015-2030 acknowledge
that climate impacts are not gender neutral; women are disproportionately affected by extreme weather events due to their
unequal access to key resources and services – such as land, finance, water, energy and education – which has major
implications on the economy, particularly in rural areas (Republic of Kenya, 2020) (UNDP, 2020).
As such, the intersection of gender and climate change was explored in depth as part of Kenya’s NDC review in 2020, which
highlighted critical contextual factors relating to water, agriculture and energy, and the impact that climate change has on
women due to their responsibilities and dependence on these sectors. Specifically, women’s reliance on the natural resource
base – both for productive and reproductive activities – makes them highly vulnerable to climate uncertainty. Prolonged
droughts have historically led to high rates of household food insecurity and malnutrition, while extreme flooding destroys
crops and infrastructure, increases incidence of waterborne disease and hinders access to key facilities (health, education,
markets etc.). All these outcomes affect women more than men, given the traditional domestic and care -related
responsibilities that Kenyan women shoulder. And within this broad social grouping, it is understood that pregnant women,
women heads of household, lactating mothers, elderly women and schoolgirls are typically most vulnerable (UNDP, 2020).
Contrary to this, given men are not tied to domestic responsibilities in the same way that women are, they have increased
mobility and often migrate to other areas in search of income in the aftermath of extreme climate events. While this additional
income is important for their families, it does mean that women then need to take up roles that were previously allocated to
the men in their family (IISD, 2018).
3.1.2 Legal, policy and stakeholder landscape
Kenya’s commitments to gender equality are enshrined in its Constitution, legal and policy frameworks, and dedicated gender
machinery mandates at national and subnational scales. (USAID, 2022) National instruments and key stakeholders working
at the intersection of gender, climate and agriculture in Kenya are captured in the following tables.
National instruments and commitments
Table 3-1 Legal and policy landscape: Kenya
Instrument Relevant content

(2019) are flagged – relating to women being largely relegated to the informal sector and
underpaid, lacking collateral which restricts their credit access, and balancing the
constant tension of domestic, social and economic responsibilities. Amongst the
policy actions proposed to tackle these challenges, one focuses specifically on
facilitating adoption of engendered climate processes to ensure related livelihood
practices are climate resilient. Women’s role in adaptation and mitigation strategies
more broadly is also highlighted within the ENR thematic area.

(2020) implement its NDCs in a gender responsive manner. As such, four adaptation
